Regarding This Volume

Volume 6 is a record of uprisings, protests, regime overthrows, civil wars, and coups in 45 countries of Europe and Eurasia. The regional composition and country order are the same as those of the Country Analysis Series (2026-10-07). The method of description, dataset definitions, rules for connecting countries, axioms, and formulas follow Volume 1.

The numbers in the table below represent the number of records linked to this country in each dataset, as counted by the program from the raw data. A value of 0 indicates that there are no records in the corresponding dataset, and the magnitude of the numbers varies depending on the dataset's definition, threshold, and reporting scope (Volume 1, Part 4).

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Greek Civil War and the Truman Doctrine Civil War · 1944–1949

Immediately after World War II, a civil war continued in Greece between government forces and Communist Party (KKE)-affiliated forces. When Britain announced on March 31, 1947, that it would end military and economic aid to the Greek government, U.S. President Harry Truman requested $400 million in aid for Greece and Turkey in a speech to a joint session of Congress on March 12, 1947 (U.S. Department of State Office of the Historian). This request for aid was later known as the 'Truman Doctrine.' The communist side lost the civil war in 1949, and Al Jazeera describes that left-wing figures subsequently experienced exile, torture, and forced labor (Al Jazeera 2019.11.17). Accounts of casualties on both government and communist sides and estimates of total civil war fatalities could not be verified from original sources in this round (see Uncorroborated Items).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
U.S. Aid Request Amount (Greece and Turkey)$400 million (Truman 1947.03.12 speech, U.S. Department of State Office of the Historian)Same figure (U.S. Department of State Office of the Historian compilation)
Combat FatalitiesUnconfirmedCOW 1944–45: 1,737 · 1946–49: 68,180 (see data/GRC.json table)

Data record: COW records the 1944–45 (Round 2, EAM-ELAS) and 1946–49 (Round 3, communist side) as separate civil wars, while UCDP records the 1946–49 government versus DSE conflict as a war with over 1,000 fatalities for four consecutive years. The 1943–44 Round 1 is not in data/GRC.json.

  1. Office of the Historian, U.S. Department of State, 'The Truman Doctrine, 1947' · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. government historical agency (perspective of aid donor) — https://history.state.gov/milestones/1945-1952/truman-doctrine
  2. Al Jazeera (2019.11.17), 'Greece remembers the brutality that felled its dictatorship' · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2019/11/17/greece-remembers-the-brutality-that-felled-its-dictatorship

April 21, 1967 Military Coup and the Junta Regime Coup · 1967–1974

In 1967, military officers seized power, bringing in the so-called 'Regime of the Colonels (1967–1974)' led by Georgios Papadopoulos (To Vima). The Powell & Thyne data (data/GRC.json) record the coup date and the king's counter-coup attempt in December of the same year as a success and failure, respectively; the sources cited in this round only state the duration of the regime.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Duration of RegimeUnconfirmed1967–1974 (To Vima; Al Jazeera)

Data record: Powell & Thyne: success on 1967.04.21, failure on 1967.12.13, success on 1973.11.25. Comparison with Archigos irregular transition records, see table.

  1. To Vima, 'How Greece's Military Junta Crushed the Polytechnic Uprising' · Media · Orientation: Greek center-left daily history section — https://www.tovima.com/vima-history/how-greeces-military-junta-crushed-the-polytechnic-uprising/amp/
  2. Al Jazeera (2019.11.17) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2019/11/17/greece-remembers-the-brutality-that-felled-its-dictatorship

Athens Polytechnic Uprising Uprising · 1973 (suppressed 1973.11.17)

In November 1973, students at the Athens Polytechnic (National Technical University of Athens) occupied the school and demanded free elections for university governing bodies (Al Jazeera). The uprising peaked on November 16 (Friday), and the first tank arrived in the Ambelokipi area shortly after midnight on November 17; at 3:00 AM, tanks rammed through the main gate, and police and LOK commandos entered to clear the campus by 3:30 AM (To Vima). Initial reports at the time gave 4 deaths and approximately 130 injuries, with no official figures released. The National Hellenic Research Foundation's provisional list confirmed 24 deaths (To Vima). Al Jazeera reports that no deaths were recorded on campus, and the 24 were people who died directly or indirectly from police and military suppression in Athens streets afterward. About 500,000 participated in the first commemorative march in 1974 (Al Jazeera).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsNo official announcement (initial reports at the time: 4, To Vima)National Hellenic Research Foundation list: 24 (identified individuals, To Vima · Al Jazeera)
InjuriesUnconfirmedApproximately 130 reported at the time (To Vima)
Participation in the first commemorative march in 1974UnconfirmedApproximately 500,000 (Al Jazeera)

Data record: Whether included in NAVCO and Mass Mobilization is indicated in the data/GRC.json table. Eight days after the uprising, Ioannidis's internal coup on 1973.11.25 is recorded as 'successful' by Powell & Thyne.

  1. To Vima, 'How Greece's Military Junta Crushed the Polytechnic Uprising' · Media · Orientation: Greek center-left daily history section — https://www.tovima.com/vima-history/how-greeces-military-junta-crushed-the-polytechnic-uprising/amp/
  2. Al Jazeera (2019.11.17), 'Greece remembers the brutality that felled its dictatorship' · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2019/11/17/greece-remembers-the-brutality-that-felled-its-dictatorship

November 25, 1973 Ioannidis coup and collapse of the regime Coup · 1973–1974

On November 25, 1973, Lieutenant General Dimitrios Ioannidis, commander of the EAT-ESA (Hellenic Gendarmerie), ousted Papadopoulos and established a more hardline regime. The military junta collapsed in July 1974 following Turkey's invasion of Cyprus (To Vima). Al Jazeera describes how the Polytechnic affair damaged the colonels' trust, leading to an internal coup by the hardliners that became the foundation for their downfall.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Time of Regime CollapseUnconfirmedJuly 1974 (To Vima; Al Jazeera)

Data record: Consistent with Powell & Thyne's 1973.11.25 successful coup.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The Uprising of the Priests in 1775 1775

In 1775, under Grand Master Ximenes de Texada, a 'priests' uprising' led by the priest Dun Gaetano Mannarino (1733–1814) broke out (Sciberras 2013, OAR@UM). A University of Malta dissertation (Cini 1968) treats the event in the context of a conflict between clergy and the Order's government, noting that earlier Maltese historiography had only briefly described it and some prior studies had not identified their sources. Regarding Mannarino, conflicting assessments as 'patriot' or 'traitor' coexist in scholarly and local literature, which are not adjudicated here. Details such as the fortifications seized and the scale of executions could not be verified from primary sources and are left unconfirmed.

  1. Sciberras, S. (2013). Dun Gaetano Mannarino (1733-1814): patrijott jew traditur. Festa Santa Marija, Birkirkara, 18-19 (OAR@UM) · Academic · Orientation: Local diocesan publication of Malta (archived in university repository) — https://www.um.edu.mt/library/oar/handle/123456789/56356
  2. Cini, C. (1968). The rising of the priests. B.A. dissertation, University of Malta (OAR@UM) · Academic · Orientation: University of Malta dissertation (focused on historiography of Maltese history) — https://www.um.edu.mt/library/oar/handle/123456789/90809

The 1798–1800 Maltese uprising against France and the Siege of Valletta 1798–1800

On June 12, 1798, a surrender agreement between the Order and French forces brought Malta under French occupation, and Grand Master Hompesch left the island on June 18. The auction of treasures from the Rabat convent in September 1798 triggered an uprising by Maltese people, and the insurgents seized Mdina (Rabat) on September 3. The French garrison on Gozo surrendered on October 28, 1798, and the sieges of Floriana, Valletta, and Cottonera continued until the French forces surrendered to British forces on September 8, 1800 (Bonnici 2014, OAR@UM). The date of surrender is reported differently as September 4, 5, or 8 in different sources.

  1. Bonnici, A. (2014). French siege letters in Gozo carried by hand: 12th June 1798 - 8th September 1800. Journal of the Malta Philatelic Society 43(1), 3-26 (OAR@UM) · Academic · Orientation: Journal of the Malta Philatelic Society (focused on postal history) — https://www.um.edu.mt/library/oar/handle/123456789/64778

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1919 'Sette Giugno' Uprising Uprising · 1919

After World War I, food shortages, rising prices, and postwar layoffs coincided in the British Crown Colony of Malta. On June 7, 1919, while a National Assembly gathered in Valletta demanding a better constitution from Britain, crowds of students and dockyard workers gathered outside; when shops opened to be attacked, troops deployed to quell the unrest fired on unarmed protesters (Festivals Malta). Four people—Manuel Attard, Joseph Bezzina, Lorenzo Dia, and Carmelo Abela—died over two days of unrest (same source). Following negotiations, a first constitution of self-government was introduced in 1921. A University of Malta dissertation (Zahra 1979) analyzes that the Labour Party, Constitutional Party, and Nationalist Party have each retold this event and its victims differently according to their circumstances and objectives, and that existing published histories of the event are incomplete and contradictory.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths4 (Maltese government cultural events agency Festivals Malta commemorative page)4 (Zahra 1979 dissertation abstract: 'Four victims')
InjuriesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: The 1919 event occurred during the period when Malta was a British colony, so there is no entry for the country in the national unit datasets (COW, UCDP, Archigos, NAVCO) (MLT.json in data/ is empty for all modern dataset entries, with only protest counts from ACLED after 2020).

  1. Festivals Malta, 'Sette Giugno' · Government · Orientation: Cultural event organization under the Maltese government (description of the national day) — https://www.festivals.mt/sette-giugno
  2. Zahra, A. (1979). The 'Sette Giugno' in history: 1919-1979. B.A. (Hons) dissertation, University of Malta (OAR@UM) · Academic · Orientation: University of Malta dissertation (analysis of party politics of memory) — https://www.um.edu.mt/library/oar/handle/123456789/91014

Anti-government protests related to the investigation into the murder of Daphne Caruana Galizia in 2019 Protest · 2019

On November 30, 2019, businessman Yorgen Fenech was charged with conspiracy to murder in the investigation of the 2017 car bombing that killed investigative journalist Daphne Caruana Galizia (he pleaded not guilty). Earlier, Prime Minister's Office Secretary Keith Schembri and former Tourism Minister Konrad Mizzi had resigned, both denying wrongdoing (Al Jazeera 2019.12.01). Thousands marched in Valletta at a protest organized by civil society groups including Repubblika, demanding the resignation of Prime Minister Joseph Muscat; nearly 20,000 gathered on Republic Street outside the court, the largest protest in weeks (NBC News 2019.12.01). On the same day, Muscat announced that he would step down from his position as Labour Party leader on January 12, 2020, and subsequently resign as prime minister. Labour MPs supported his continued tenure (Al Jazeera). The government described it as fulfillment of promises of judicial justice, while protesters demanded immediate resignation; both accounts are reported side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Protest Participation (December 1, Valletta)Unconfirmed (original Malta Police tally not consulted)Nearly 20,000 (NBC News 2019.12.01)
DeathsUnconfirmed0 (ACLED: 0 deaths since 2020; 2019 figures outside data/MLT.json scope)

Data record: Because the ACLED aggregation in data/MLT.json begins in 2020, the 2019 protests are not included, and the Mass Mobilization (protests_mm) entry is also empty. The change of government was a resignation pursuant to intra-party procedures, so it does not qualify as an Archigos irregular transfer.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1981 Kosovo Protests (Pristina University Protests) Protest · 1981

In March 1981, protests broke out in Kosovo, an autonomous province of the Socialist Federal Republic of Yugoslavia, with many Albanians participating. The protests began as a protest against poor living conditions at the student cafeteria of the University of Pristina but soon took on nationalist character (Christian Science Monitor 1983.01.04). At the time, Secretary of the Federal and Autonomous Province Councils Joran Miskovic stated that the younger generation had been 'brainwashed' and that students should learn the languages of other Yugoslav peoples (same article). The Kosovo government commemorated the protests at a 45th-anniversary ceremony in 2026 as an event in which police violence killed nine people and thousands were arrested and imprisoned — the federal authorities described it as a problem of Albanian ethnic separatism (irredentism), while today's Kosovo government describes it as protests against police violence; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed (original text of 1981 Yugoslav federal government official tally not consulted)9 (Christian Science Monitor 1983.01.04)
InjuriesUnconfirmed250 (Christian Science Monitor 1983.01.04)
ImprisonmentUnconfirmedOver 1,000 (Christian Science Monitor 1983.01.04)

Data record: Kosovo was an autonomous province of Yugoslavia at the time, so this protest does not appear in the XKX entry of national-level datasets (NAVCO, Mass Mobilization, etc.) (data/XKX.json). It may appear under the YUG/SRB entry, but it was not cross-checked in this round.

  1. Christian Science Monitor (1983.01.04), 'Kosovo: surface calm masks old troubles' · Media · Affiliation: U.S. media (centering on interviews with Yugoslav officials at the time) — https://csmonitor.com/1983/0104/010442.html
  2. Office of the Prime Minister of Kosovo, 'Commemorative Academy Held to Mark the 45th Anniversary of the 1981 Demonstrations' · Government · Affiliation: Announcement of a Kosovo government commemoration event (from the perspective of Albanian protesters) — https://kryeministri.rks-gov.net/en/?p=199408

Kosovo War (KLA uprising · Yugoslav federal and Serbian security forces crackdown · NATO airstrikes) Civil War · 1998–1999

From 1998, armed clashes between the Albanian armed organization Kosovo Liberation Army (KLA/UÇK) and the Yugoslav federal and Serbian security forces escalated, and in March–June 1999, large-scale killings and refugee movements occurred alongside NATO airstrikes. In February 2015 in Belgrade, the Humanitarian Law Center (HLC), a Serbian human rights organization, announced that it had analyzed 31,600 documents and found that 13,535 people were killed or disappeared between January 1, 1998, and December 31, 2000, in connection with this conflict (1812 Albanians, 2,197 Serbs, 526 Roma, Bosniaks, Montenegrins, etc.). A 2002 statistical report submitted to the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia (ICTY) by Patrick Ball and others concluded that the patterns of killings and refugee flows in March–June 1999 were consistent with a hypothesis of organized violence by the Yugoslav federal forces, and that KLA activity and NATO airstrikes were not significant causes (HRDAG) — the Yugoslav federal government (the Milošević side) contested this in court, and both accounts are presented side by side. After June 1999, the UN Mission in Kosovo (UNMIK) and NATO-led KFOR entered, and Kosovo declared independence in 2008.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Killed and Missing (1998–2000)Unconfirmed (original texts of Yugoslav federal and Serbian government official tallies not consulted)13,535 — Albanian 1812, Serbian 2,197, other 526 (HLC Kosovo Memory Book, 2015.02.06)
Estimated deaths for March–June 1999UnconfirmedOver 10,000 (Vol et al., AAAS/ABA-CEELI report · HRDAG summary)

Data record: COW internal war, UCDP armed conflict, and Archigos lists in data/XKX.json are all empty. This war was likely recorded under the Yugoslavia (Serbia) entry for the then-state unit; it is not linked to Kosovo's country code (coverage gap).

  1. Humanitarian Law Center (2015), '31,600 documents undoubtedly confirm death or disappearance of 13,535 individuals during war in Kosovo' · Human rights organization · Affiliation: Serbian human rights organization (name-based record basis, victim-centered) — https://www.hlc-rdc.org/en/dokumentovanje-i-pamcenje-2/31600-documents-undoubtedly-confirm-death-or-disappearance-of-13535-individuals-during-war-in-kosovo/
  2. HRDAG, 'Killings and Refugee Flow in Kosovo, March–June 1999 (A report to ICTY)' · Research report · Affiliation: Prosecution-submitted statistical report (author's interpretation explicitly stated as the author's responsibility) — https://hrdag.org/publications/killings-and-refugee-flow-in-kosovo-march-june-1999-a-report-to-icty/

March 2004 unrest (anti-minority violence) Uprising · 2004

Between March 17 and 18, 2004, at least 33 incidents of unrest occurred across Kosovo over a period of 48 hours, involving approximately 51,000 participants. Serbian, Roma, and Ashkali communities were attacked, resulting in 19 deaths; at least 550 homes and 27 Serbian Orthodox churches and monasteries were burned, and approximately 4,100 minority members were displaced (Human Rights Watch 2004.07). Triggers cited include false reports that Serbs had drowned three Albanian boys, a Serbian road blockade, and protests by war veterans and KLA-affiliated groups on March 16. HRW reported that NATO-led KFOR and UNMIK police failed to protect minorities. Serbian media call this event the 'March pogrom,' while Albanians refer to it as the 'March unrest' — designations diverge, so accounts from both sides are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed (original UNMIK official tally not consulted)19 (Human Rights Watch 2004.07.26)
ParticipantsUnconfirmedApproximately 51,000, at least 33 incidents of unrest (Human Rights Watch)
DamageUnconfirmedOver 550 homes destroyed, 27 churches and monasteries lost, approximately 4,100 minorities displaced (Human Rights Watch)

Data record: As this falls under the UNMIK administration period, there is no XKX entry in the national unit dataset. Mass Mobilization records only two events for Kosovo in 2003 (data/XKX.json) and has no entry for the 2004 unrest.

  2. Human Rights Watch (2004.07), 'Failure to Protect: Anti-Minority Violence in Kosovo, March 2004' · Human rights organization · Affiliation: International human rights organization — https://www.hrw.org/report/2004/07/25/failure-protect/anti-minority-violence-kosovo-march-2004

2023 Armed Attack in Vraneša Uprising · 2023

On September 24, 2023, near the village of Vraneša in northern Kosovo, approximately 30 armed assailants attacked a police patrol, killing one policeman (Afrim Bajraktaraj) and injuring another. The assailants used an armored vehicle to break into and occupy a Serbian Orthodox monastery, barricading themselves inside; at least three assailants were killed in the ensuing gunfight, and most fled overnight (Al Jazeera 2023.09.24). Kosovo Prime Minister Albin Kurti characterized this as terrorism and described it as an organized crime operation supported by Belgrade authorities with political, financial, and material backing. Serbian President Aleksandar Vučić stated that the killing of the policeman could not be justified but said the clashes stemmed from pressure on Serbs by the Kosovo government (AP report) — accounts from both sides are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths1 policeman, 3 armed assailants (Kosovo Police announcement; reported by AP 2023.09.24)1 policeman, at least 3 assailants (Al Jazeera 2023.09.24)
Number of Armed AssailantsAt least 30 (Prime Minister Kurti)Approximately 30 (Al Jazeera)

Data record: ACLED records 31 political violence events and 5 deaths in Kosovo for 2023 (data/XKX.json), overlapping with this event period. Not listed in the UCDP armed conflict list.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The 1764 Nicosia Uprising and the Rebellion of Halil Agha 1764–1766

In autumn 1764, the Ottoman governor (mutasarrıf) Chalil Agha, who was meeting with imperial envoys in Nicosia, was killed when the floor of the building collapsed. Accounts differ as to whether the collapse was planned or an accident caused by a rotten beam. An armed crowd that included people gathered in the market stormed and burned the governor's residence; about 18 governor and his subordinates died. Background factors cited include heavy taxes levied by the governor to recoup the cost of purchasing his office, drought, locust swarms, and the 1760 plague. The Ottoman government pardoned most of the residents on condition of paying reparations to the treasury and victims' families and ordered punishment for about 20 individuals named by name. In August 1765, Halil Agha, commander of the Kerynia fortress (kazasker), led a Muslim-led rebellion against the reimposed taxes, grew to several thousand fighters, established its own administration, and cut off grain supplies to Nicosia. When Köprülü Ahmed Pasha arrived with the Karman army in summer 1766, Halil retreated to Kerynia Castle with about 200 men, surrendered, and was executed along with his subordinates on false promises (Stavrides 2025). The main contemporary sources are the accounts of Archbishop Kyprianos and Mariti.

  1. Stavrides, T. (2025), 'Shades of Disorder in Eighteenth-Century Cyprus: The Case of the 1764-1766 Rebellion', Cahiers du Centre d'Études Chypriotes 55 · Academic article · Orientation: University of Cyprus scholar (comparison of Ottoman documents and contemporary Greek chronicles) — https://journals.openedition.org/cchyp/2130
  2. Cyprus Visual Archive (Severis Foundation), 'History today: 1 June 1766' · Memorial foundation · Orientation: Excerpt from the Chronicle of Kyprianos (Greek clergy perspective) — https://cvar.severis.org/en/history-today/6/564-1-june-1766

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

October Uprising of 1931 (Oktoiobriana) Uprising · 1931

In October 1931, British colonial authorities sought to raise taxes to cover fiscal deficits caused by the economic downturn of the early 1930s, and unrest broke out as existing grievances over issues such as the so-called 'Cyprus Corvee' overlapped with demands for unification with Greece (Enosis). In Nicosia, crowds burned down Government House, the residence of the British High Commissioner, and events occurred in about one-third of the 598 villages across the island. Six civilians were killed, dozens were injured, and approximately 2,000 people were convicted of related crimes. The UK responded with troop reinforcements, suspension of the constitution, press censorship, and bans on political parties; two bishops and eight prominent figures were deported, and the High Commissioner governed by decree. Restrictions on Greek and Turkish historical education and flag flying applied equally to Turkish Cypriots who did not participate in the unrest (U.S. Congressional Research Service). The then High Commissioner Ronald Storrs later wrote in his memoirs that the residence was burned down in about 10 minutes after being pelted with stones (Severis Foundation Archive) — colonial authorities' recollections and Turkish Cypriot commemorative accounts of the 'October Events' are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed (original British colonial government report not consulted)6 civilians (Library of Congress Country Study Cyprus: A Country Study)
ConvictionsUnconfirmedApprox. 2,000 (Library of Congress Country Study)

Data Record: As an event from the colonial period, it is absent from COW, UCDP, NAVCO, and Mass Mobilization CYP entries (data/CYP.json).

  1. Library of Congress, Cyprus: A Country Study, 'British Annexation' · Government · Affiliation: Library of Congress Federal Research Division country study (1991, secondary English-language account) — https://countrystudies.us/cyprus/9.htm
  2. Cyprus Visual Archive (Severis Foundation), '21 October 1931' · Memorial Foundation · Affiliation: Excerpt from the memoirs of Governor Storrs (colonial authorities' perspective) — https://cvar.severis.org/en/history-today/10/175-21-october-1931b

Inter-Communal Conflict of 1963–1964 and the 1974 Coup and Turkish Military Intervention Coup · 1963–1974

Following independence in 1960, armed clashes broke out between the Greek and Turkish communities in 1963–1964, followed by a coup on July 15, 1974, in which the military ousted President Makarios (recorded in Powell & Thyne · Archigos: 'Overthrow by foreign-supported military', Makarios's flight into exile, and successor Sampson serving July 16–23). This was followed by an interstate armed conflict between the governments of Cyprus and Turkey in the same year, recorded by UCDP as a territorial dispute (data/CYP.json, generator table). The official list of missing persons compiled by the Committee on Missing Persons (CMP), which seeks the remains of those missing from both periods, lists 2,002 individuals (1,510 Greek Cypriots and 492 Turkish Cypriots); as of May 31, 2026, 1,717 have been exhumed and 1,069 (764 Greek Cypriots and 305 Turkish Cypriots) have been identified (Politis 2026.07.07). The Greek Cypriot member of the committee, Pantelides, reported to a House committee that approximately 1,100 individuals had been identified (same article). Greek Cypriot officials claim that Turkish Cypriots restrict information, but the Turkish Cypriot response to this was not published in the articles reviewed for this round (AP 2023.07.28) — no determination is made.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
CMP Official List of Missing Persons (1963–64 and 1974)2,002 individuals — Greek Cypriots 1,510, Turkish Cypriots 492 (official CMP figures, reported by Politis 2026.07.07)Other figures cite 1,619 missing Greek Cypriots, but Pantelides stated that this is 126 fewer (search summary; original not verified)
Identified Individuals (as of 2026.05.31)1,069 individuals (official CMP figure)Approx. 1,100 individuals (Pantelides, remarks to House committee)
Total Deaths in 1974UnconfirmedUnconfirmed — original independent tallies not consulted in this round

Data record: The COW civil war list is empty, and UCDP records only the 1955–1959 British vs. EOKA (non-state) conflict and the 1974 Cyprus vs. Turkey (inter-state, over 1,000 killed per year). The 1963–1964 inter-communal clashes are not listed in UCDP or COW under CYP.

  2. AP (2023.07.28), 'Divided Cyprus rival leaders appeal for information on the missing as eyewitnesses die' · Media · Affiliation: U.S. news agency (republication by local broadcaster) — https://www.wsls.com/news/world/2023/07/28/divided-cyprus-rival-leaders-appeal-for-information-on-the-missing-as-eyewitnesses-die/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Salt Riot of 1648 in Moscow Early Modern (Moscow State) · 1648

Following the Time of Troubles, the government had converted several tax categories into a uniform salt tax to replenish state coffers, causing salt prices to rise. In June 1648, a riot broke out in Moscow. The crowd demanded the surrender of Leonid Plyshevsky, head of the Zemsky Prikaz and chief of Moscow's security, among others, on June 2 at the Kremlin. Boris Morozov, a close confidant of Tsar Alexis, was the primary target of the anger; however, the Streltsy (musketeers), who also served as Moscow artisans, refused to obey his order to drive the rioters from the Kremlin. Accounts state that Plyshevsky, Trakhaniotov, and Chistoy died in this process, while Morozov was not handed over to the crowd but exiled to a monastery. One of the uprising's demands, the summoning of the Zemsky Sobor, led to the 1649 Code of Laws (Sobornoye Ulozheniye), which codified serfdom in its most stringent form (accounts differ on whether individual persons were killed).

  1. Russian Life (1998.06), 'Rubbing Salt in Peasants' Wounds' · Media · Affiliation: Russian history and culture popular magazine (published in the U.S.), based on search summaries — https://russianlife.com/magazine/june-july-1998/rubbing-salt-in-peasants-wounds/
  2. Russian Life (2019.01), 'Rewriting the Code' · Media · Affiliation: Same magazine, account related to the 1649 Code of Laws — https://russianlife.com/magazine/jan-feb-2019/rewriting-the-code/

Stenka Razin's Rebellion Early Modern (Moscow State) · 1670–1671

The rebel force led by Stepan (Stenka) Razin, a Don Cossack, moved up the Volga in 1670 and besieged Simbirsk (now Ulyanovsk) in the fall. The defenders were reinforced with large government forces and did not surrender; a government force under Yuri Baryatinsky defeated the poorly trained rebels. Wounded Razin retreated south to the Don River but was captured by Cossacks loyal to his patron, Don Cossack leader Kornil Yakovlev, in April 1671 and handed over to tsarist authorities. Razin, brought to Moscow, was tortured and executed by dismemberment on Red Square — dates are given as June 6 (Julian) or June 16 (Gregorian) of 1671. Government forces burned rebel villages and executed their leaders; the last stronghold, Astrakhan, surrendered in December 1671. Accounts stating that thousands were tortured and executed after the suppression could not be verified against a second independent source (EBSCO).

  1. Encyclopedia.com, 'Stenka Razin' · Encyclopedia · Affiliation: Republished from an academic reference work (English-speaking), based on search summaries — https://www.encyclopedia.com/history/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/stenka-razin
  2. Encyclopaedia Britannica, 'Stenka Razin' (print edition) · Encyclopedia · Affiliation: English-language encyclopedia, original text 403 — based on search summaries — https://www.britannica.com/print/article/492611
  3. EBSCO Research Starters, 'Razin's Rebellion' · Encyclopedia · Affiliation: Educational reference work (English-speaking) — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/razins-rebellion/

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Pugachev's Rebellion 1773–1775

The largest of the popular uprisings that followed Catherine II's accession in 1762, it is commonly described as the largest peasant revolt in Russian history. A Cossack from the Yaik (Ural) region, Yemelyan Pugachev, proclaimed himself Peter III, Catherine's deceased husband, abolished serfdom, and raised a large army. The rebels captured and burned Kazan in 1774 but were defeated near Charashev by government forces under Michelson later that year. Pugachev fled westward toward the Urals in September 1774 but was betrayed by his Cossack followers and executed in Moscow in January 1775 (January 10, Julian calendar; January 21, Gregorian). Suppression of residual resistance took several more months, thousands of rebels were executed, and the government changed the name of the Yaik River and its Cossacks (Ural River and Ural Cossacks).

  1. EBSCO Research Starters, 'Pugachev's Revolt' · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Educational reference work (English-speaking), based on search summaries — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/pugachevs-revolt
  2. EBSCO Research Starters, 'Yemelyan Ivanovich Pugachev' · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Educational reference work (English-speaking) — https://ebsco.com/research-starters/biography/yemelyan-ivanovich-pugachev

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Bloody Sunday, 1905, and the Revolution of 1905 Protest · 1905

On January 9, 1905 (Julian), troops fired on a workers' petition march in Saint Petersburg led by Father Georgy Gapon. In the Narva Gate procession directly led by Gapon alone, approximately 40 people were shot and killed (History Today). The total number of deaths for the day varies widely across sources — approximately 200 dead and 800 wounded (History Today), 143–234 dead and 439–800 wounded (New World Encyclopedia), over 130, etc. The original text of the government's official tally could not be confirmed in this round. This event is described as the trigger for the 1905 Revolution, which spread into strikes and uprisings later that year.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (1905.1.9)Unconfirmed (original text of imperial government official announcement not consulted)Approximately 200 (History Today) / 143–234 (New World Encyclopedia)
WoundedUnconfirmedApproximately 800 (History Today) / 439–800 (New World Encyclopedia)

Data Record: As a single-day suppression event, there is no independent entry in the COW civil war list. The COW, coup, and Archigos entries in the modern data table (data/RUS.json) are populated separately by the generator.

  1. History Today, ''Bloody Sunday' in St Petersburg' · Media · Leanings: British history-focused monthly magazine; based on search summary — https://www.historytoday.com/archive/months-past/bloody-sunday-st-petersburg
  2. New World Encyclopedia, 'Bloody Sunday (1905)' · Encyclopedia · Leanings: Editorially reviewed online encyclopedia (based on original text from a wiki project — only the numerical ranges are consulted) — https://www.newworldencyclopedia.org/entry/Bloody_Sunday_(1905)

1962 Novocherkassk Incident Protest · 1962

In June 1962, workers' strikes and protests broke out in Novocherkassk, and troops fired on protesters. The number of deaths varies across sources, ranging from 23 to 26 — the Michigan State University Soviet History Project records 24, while a 1990s Moscow Times article cited official statistics of 26 dead and 87 wounded, noting that the actual figure could be lower. The burials were conducted in secret, and Soviet authorities remained silent on the incident for 27 years. Since 1991, the Chief Military Procuracy has conducted a formal investigation into the entire incident, but the number of deaths in its conclusion could not be confirmed in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths26 (official statistics, cited by Moscow Times) — according to records, 23–2424 (MSU Soviet History Project); Moscow Times noted the possibility that the official figure is lower
Wounded87 (official statistics, cited by Moscow Times)Unconfirmed

Data Record: As a domestic suppression incident during the Soviet period, there is no separate entry for this event in the COW, UCDP, or NAVCO RUS items (data/RUS.json basis; see generator table).

  3. OSA Archivum blog, 'A Massacre under Khrushchev' · Academic · Leanings: Open Society Archives (Budapest) — https://legacy.osaarchivum.org/blog/a-massacre-under-khrushchev-novocherkassk-shooting-on-screen-on-air-and-on-banners

October 1993 Constitutional Crisis Regime Overthrow · 1993

The confrontation between President Yeltsin and the Supreme Soviet (parliament) escalated into the siege of the Ostankino TV center and shelling of the White House on October 3–4, 1993. The U.S. Department of State's 1993 Human Rights Report notes disagreement over the death toll, recording 147 official deaths while citing press estimates of over 100 dead at Ostankino and up to 150 at the White House. Subsequently, the official final tally cited 187 deaths and 437 wounded; non-government and anti-Yeltsin sides claimed over 1,000 (some claimed up to 2,000). Both figures are recorded without adjudication. Data classification (whether it was an attempt to overthrow the regime or a coup) follows the records of the Powell & Thyne coup dataset in the generator table.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (10.3–4)147 (then-official, cited by U.S. State Department report) → 187 (cited as the final official tally)Press estimates: over 100 at Ostankino · up to 150 at the White House (U.S. State Department report); non-government and anti-Yeltsin sides claimed over 1,000
Wounded437 (cited as the final official tally)Unconfirmed

First Chechen War Civil War · 1994–1996

In late 1994, Russian federal forces entered Chechnya and the war continued until 1996. Russia did not provide an official figure for civilian deaths. Military fatalities are cited as 3,826 by the General Staff and 5,732 by other official figures. The Russian State Statistics Committee reported seeing 30,000 to 40,000 civilian deaths in 1994–1996 (Jamestown), while human rights group Memorial reported seeing at least 50,000 civilian deaths over the period (reported). Scholar John Dunlop put the figure at at least 35,000 (Tufts Mass Atrocity Endings Project). Neither side kept precise records of civilians, and Chechen claims of more than 100,000 have not been independently verified. Figures from both sides are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Russian Military Fatalities3,826 (General Staff) / 5,732 (officially cited)Estimated range of 3,500–7,500 (globalsecurity compilation); higher claims exist (not verified)
Civilian Fatalities (1994–96)30,000–40,000 (State Statistics Committee reports)At least 50,000 (Memorial, Jamestown reports) / at least 35,000 (Dunlop, Tufts)

Data Record: The COW and UCDP entries for the Chechen conflict follow the generator table (data/RUS.json). Civilian fatalities are not directly compared to the dataset's battle deaths, as their definitions differ.

  3. GlobalSecurity.org, 'Chechnya' · Academic · Affiliation: US security information site (secondary compilation) — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/war/chechnya.htm

2023 Wagner Group Armed Rebellion Coup · 2023

On June 24, 2023, the private military company Wagner Group, led by Evgeny Prigozhin, seized the Southern Military District headquarters in Rostov-on-Don and then moved north toward Moscow. A Reuters reporter witnessed military helicopters firing on the convoy near Voronezh, but there was little resistance on the roads. Prigozhin stated in a voice message that he had turned back the convoy 'to avoid bloodshed' at a point about 200 km from Moscow. Following mediation by Belarusian President Lukashenko, Wagner fighters were granted safe passage and returned to their bases, Prigozhin went to Belarus, and the Kremlin agreed to drop criminal charges against the company and its leader. The Kremlin characterized it as a mutiny, while Prigozhin's side described it as a 'march of justice' aimed at the defense command — both characterizations are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
CasualtiesUnconfirmed (original text of Russian government official tally not consulted)Unconfirmed — no figures in articles checked for this round

Data Record: The 2023 event does not appear in the Powell & Thyne coup data (data/RUS.json) (RUS contains only one entry, from 1991). As it was an attempt by a private military company, it may not fit the definition of 'an attempt by elites within state institutions or the military'.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Bărbulești Peasant Uprising Middle Ages (Kingdom of Hungary, Transylvania) · 1437–1438

It is described as triggered by Bishop Lepșig György's demand for back-tithes accumulated over several years on the grounds of a temporary decline in currency value. In 1437, Hungarian and Blach (Romanian) peasants, along with some minor nobles, established a fortified camp on Bărbulești Hill, led by the impoverished noble Buda Nyír Üllői. The time when the camp was established varies among sources: spring or June. On July 6, an agreement was reached in Kolozsmonostor that met many of the rebels' demands, and on October 6 in Deliha, representatives from both sides agreed to ask the king for arbitration. In the same year, three estates—the nobility, the Saxon urban nobility, and the Szeklers—concluded a mutual aid pact (Unio trium nationum) targeting the entire peasant class, excluding the peasants. After the noble army recaptured Kolozsvár (Cluj) on January 8, 1438, resistance was suppressed by the end of January.

  1. Magyar Elektronikus Könyvtár (MEK, Hungarian National Széchenyi Library), History of Transylvania · Academic · Orientation: Hungarian academic narrative (Hungarian perspective) — based on search summaries — https://mek.oszk.hu/03400/03407/html/83.html
  2. Connexions Library, 'Budai Nagy Antal Revolt' · Academic · Orientation: Social movement history archive (Canada), secondary compilation — https://www.Connexions.org/CxLibrary/Docs/CxP-Budai_Nagy_Antal_Revolt.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Uprising of Horea, Cloșca, and Crișan 1784–1785

In January 1784, when Joseph II ordered an increase in the number of border troops in Transylvania through pre-registration for military service, many serfs accepted this as a way to escape serfdom. In April of the same year, Horea (Basile Ursu Nicolae) petitioned the emperor, but the only result was that his death sentence was commuted to imprisonment and flogging. The uprising began on October 31, 1784, in the Jízera County (Apuseni Mountains), spreading across all of Transylvania; it demanded the abolition of serfdom for Romanian serfs and political equality with privileged groups. The rebels defeated imperial forces at Brad and Halmagiu on November 27 but the uprising ended around December 14. The leaders were betrayed and captured in January 1785, Crișan committed suicide in prison in February, and Horea and Cloșca were burned at the stake (breaking on the wheel) on February 28, 1785, on Dealul Furcilor ('Wheel Hill') in Alba Iulia.

  1. Alba24 (regional media of Alba County), '28 februarie: Execuția prin tragere pe roată a lui Horea, Cloșca și Crișan' · Media · Orientation: Romanian regional media (Romanian commemorative narrative) — https://alba24.ro/28-februarie-executia-prin-tragere-pe-roata-a-lui-horea-closca-si-crisan-povestea-unei-zile-triste-in-istoria-motilor-1070508.html

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1907 Romanian Peasant Uprising Uprising · 1907

A peasant uprising that began in northern Moldavia in 1907 spread to Wallachia, and the government mobilized the military to suppress it. The number of deaths has not been confirmed. Official figures are cited as 419, while independent estimates range widely from 1,786 (Dabija) to 11,000 (Chirot & Ragin). The figure of 11,000 was first published by socialist newspapers 『Adevărul』 and 『Dimineața』 at the time; it has been criticized for lacking substantiation and was accepted as is under the communist regime (11,000 representatives were invited to a farmers' congress convened by Ceaușescu in 1977). Estimates based on death registration records of 1907 are around 2,000, while Ionescu Bulei's estimate is 2,000–3,000. Records from the Ministry of Internal Affairs were lost, and there are accounts that the king ordered the destruction of records, so the exact number cannot be determined.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths419 (cited in official figures)Approx. 2,000 (based on death registration records) / 2,000–3,000 (Bulei) / 1,786 (Dabija) / 11,000 (Chirot & Ragin — based on figures from left-wing newspapers at the time; subject to criticism)

Data Record: Whether the 1907 uprising has an independent entry in the COW Civil War and UCDP ROU items follows the generator table (data/ROU.json).

  1. Radio Romania International, '110 years since the Peasants' Uprising in 1907' · Media · Orientation: Romanian public international broadcaster — https://rri.ro/en/features-and-reports/the-history-show/110-years-since-the-peasants-uprising-in-1907-id129873.html
  2. EVZ, 'Răscoala din 1907 și mitul celor 11.000 de morți' · Media · Orientation: Romanian daily (article criticizing figures) — https://evz.ro/rascoala-din-1907-si-mitul-celor-11-000-de-morti-anatomia-unei-cifre-intre-propaganda-si-adevar-istoric.html
  3. EBSCO Research Starters, 'Romanian Peasant Revolt' · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Educational reference work (English-speaking) — https://ebsco.com/research-starters/history/romanian-peasant-revolt

1989 Romanian Revolution Regime Overthrow · 1989

The uprising that began with the Timișoara protests on December 16, 1989 spread to Bucharest; after the Ceaușescus fled on December 22, the National Salvation Front (FSN) seized power. From December 17, 1989 to January 10, 1990, 1,104 deaths and 3,352 injuries were recorded, of which 162 died during the protests of December 16–22 and 942 after Ceaușescu's flight (AP reports also describe 'most' as dying after his downfall). Mediafax reports a different breakdown: 221 military deaths and 3,321 injuries among the fatalities. Investigations into the causes of deaths after December 22 (so-called 'terrorist' shootings) were repeatedly reopened and closed, and claims about the conclusions diverge between the sides — no determination is made.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (1989.12.17–1990.1.10)1,104 (162 on 12.16–22 / 942 after 12.22)AP approx. 1,104 (mostly post-fall)
Injuries3,3523,321 (reported by Mediafax)

Data Record: The ROU entry in the Powell & Thyne coup data is empty (data/ROU.json). Archigos includes irregular transfers such as Carol II in 1940 (ousted by the military) and Antonescu in 1944 (ousted by other government actors) — the 1989 classification follows the generator table.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Christmas Uprising (Božićni ustanak) Uprising · 1919

In protest against the Podgorica Assembly's decision in November 1918 to directly integrate the Kingdom of Montenegro into the Kingdom of Serbia, the 'Greens' (Zelenaši), who opposed the integration, rose up in early January 1919 (around Orthodox Christmas). According to records at the U.S. National Archives, the uprising began on January 7, 1919, the day the American Red Cross Montenegro Committee arrived. The uprising forces surrounded Cetinje but were unable to occupy it due to resistance from the pro-integration 'Whites' (Bjelaši) and Allied troops. Accounts differ regarding Italy's involvement and extent (Femić's article examines Italian agitation using 1920 sources). The pro-integration side described it as a rebellion, while the Greens described it as defending the kingdom's sovereignty.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
CasualtiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: No Montenegro entry in pipeline-collected datasets such as COW and UCDP (the country was not recorded as a state unit in 1919).

  1. U.S. National Archives, The Text Message blog 'The Full Montenegro' (2011.11.04) · Government report · Orientation: Commentary by U.S. government archivist (mentions only the start date of the uprising) — https://text-message.blogs.archives.gov/2011/11/04/the-full-montenegro
  2. Femić, Radovan. 'Dva primjera aktivnosti zelenaškog pokreta' (Istorijski institut, Beograd) - CEEOL · Article · Orientation: Published by a Serbian academic institution — https://www.ceeol.com/search/article-detail?id=1015652

Plot to Overthrow the Government on Election Day in 2016 Coup · 2016–2024

The prosecution indicted that there was a plot by a Russian organization to attack parliament and assassinate then-Prime Minister Milo Đukanović on election day in 2016. In May 2019, the High Court sentenced the defendants to a total of approximately 70 years in prison on charges including terrorism, but the Appeals Court overturned the first-instance verdicts for 13 individuals on February 5, 2021, citing 'serious violations of criminal proceedings' and ordering a retrial with a new panel. On July 12, 2024, the High Court acquitted all 13 defendants. The presiding judge stated that there was no evidence of guilt, as weapons had never entered Montenegro and equipment to enter parliament had not been imported. Among the defendants were Democratic Party of Socialists leaders Andrija Mandić (then President of Parliament) and Milan Knežević; two Russians tried in absentia were charged with organizing and funding the plot. Russia denied involvement, and the defense argued that it was a political affair. An appeal by the Special Prosecutor's Office was expected.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Number of Defendants13 (subject to retrial by Appeals Court and High Court)13 (RFE/RL report)
Sentence in First InstanceTotal approximately 70 years (May 2019, subsequently overturned)Approximately 70 years (RFE/RL report)

Data record: No Montenegro entry in Powell & Thyne coup data (pipeline collection) — an unexecuted plot event acquitted at retrial.

  1. RFE/RL 'Montenegrin High Court Acquits Alleged 2016 Coup Plotters' (2024.07.12, ecoi.net refiled) · Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded broadcaster — https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2112598.html
  2. RFE/RL 'Montenegro Court Overturns Coup Plot Verdicts' (2021.02.05) · Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded broadcaster — https://www.rferl.org/a/montenegro-court-overturns-coup-plot-verdicts/31088156.html

Anti-Religious Freedom Law Protests (Lijeviće) and the 2020 Government Change Protest · 2019–2020

Parliament adopted the Religious Freedom Act on December 27, 2019, with the votes of 45 ruling coalition MPs. The law requires religious communities to prove ownership of property prior to 1918 (the year Montenegro lost its independence). Chaos erupted during the vote, and police arrested 24 people, including 18 opposition MPs of Serbian background. The Serbian Orthodox Church said the law was 'discriminatory and unconstitutional,' while the government countered that it was not a law to seize church property. From December 2019 to summer 2020, large-scale marches led by Orthodox clergy continued nationwide, with the last protest related to the law occurring on August 28, 2020 (ACLED). In the general election of August 30, 2020, the DPS, which had ruled for about 30 years, remained the largest party with 35.06% but failed to secure a majority; an opposition coalition that promised to repeal the law pursued the formation of a government. Accounts from the government and church sides differ, and this series makes no determination.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
MPs Voting for the Bill45 (ruling coalition)45 (Al Jazeera report)
Arrested on Vote DayUnconfirmed24 people (including 18 opposition MPs, Al Jazeera report)
Protest Participation ScaleUnconfirmedRally 'of tens of thousands' one week after the election (ACLED report)

Data record: ACLED (pipeline collection): protests increased from 124 in 2019 to 353 in 2020. NAVCO 1.3 records only the 2019 '97000 Resist campaign' (against Vučković, failed); religious law protests are not recorded under a separate entry. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 3 events in 2019, 1 event in 2020.

  2. ACLED 'Religion and Politics: Demonstrations in Montenegro' (2020.11.03) · Public DB · Orientation: conflict data agency analysis — https://acleddata.com/report/religion-and-politics-demonstrations-montenegro

Opposition protests of 2015 and the '97000 Resistance' movement of 2019 Protests · 2015, 2019

NAVCO 1.3 records the 2015 opposition protests against the government and the 2019 '97000 Resistance' movement against the Vučković government as nonviolent movements aimed at regime change, classifying both as 'failed.' In this round, individual developments could not be verified from separate source texts, so only dataset records are noted.

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Montenegrin Opposition Protest' 2015 failed; '97000 Resist campaign' 2019 failed (pipeline collection data/MNE.json).

5. Modern Events in the Literature

Dniester (Transnistria) Conflict Civil War · 1990–1992

During the dissolution of the Soviet Union, separatist forces along the Dniester River (Transnistria) declared the 'Pridnestrovian Moldavian Republic (PMR)' and clashed with the Moldovan government; armed clashes intensified in spring 1992. On July 21, 1992, in Moscow, the presidents of Russia and Moldova signed a ceasefire agreement in the presence of separatist leader Igor Smirnov, and on July 27, a Joint Control Commission was established with representatives from Moldova, Russia, and Transnistria (Ukraine and OSCE as observers). The CSCE (now OSCE) established a mission in Moldova in 1993. The Moldovan government sought territorial integrity, while the PMR side demanded self-determination and secession; the conflict remains unresolved politically.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Combat Fatalities1,000 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1, war_num 873, COW minimum threshold value)'Hundreds of casualties' (OSCE account, excerpted from search)
Temporary Displaced PersonsUnconfirmedOver 100,000 (OSCE account, excerpted from search)

Data Record: COW v5.1: 'Dniestrian Independence War of 1991-1992', internationalized=true, outcome code 3. UCDP/PRIO v26.1: conflict_id 394, active only in 1992 (territorial dispute, 0 war years with over 1,000 fatalities per year). NAVCO 1.3: 'Dniestr' 1992 violence, partial success. The COW (1991 start) and UCDP (1992 single-year) periods differ.

  2. Veridica '30 years since the war in Transnistria. A chronology of fighting and truce' · Media · Lean: Romanian fact-checking outlet (Moldovan and Romanian perspectives) — https://www.veridica.ro/en/analyses/30-years-since-the-war-in-transnistria-a-chronology-of-fighting-and-truce
  3. OSCE Mission to Moldova introductory materials · International Organization · Lean: Mediation body — https://www.osce.org/files/f/documents/0/2/85681.pdf

April 2009 Parliamentary Election Dispute Protests Protest · 2009

Following the announcement of a Communist Party victory in the April 5, 2009 parliamentary election, thousands protested against electoral fraud in the capital Chisinau; on April 7, several dozen protesters entered the presidential palace and parliament building. Police arrested over 600 people, many of whom were arbitrarily detained, and numerous reports of abuse during detention emerged (Amnesty International). Student Valeriu Boboc died on April 7; the police officer accused of his murder was acquitted in December 2013. The authorities confirmed one death, while the 2009 U.S. State Department human rights report stated that human rights groups claimed up to three deaths at the hands of security forces. NAVCO 1.3 records this movement as the 'Grapevine Revolution' and classifies it as a success (regime change).

Abuse CasesUnconfirmed108 reports → 71 criminal cases → 29 trials (Amnesty International 2014)

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Moldovan "Grapevine" Revolution' 2009, nonviolent, successful. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 5 events in 2009. Archigos 4.1 contains no record of an irregular leadership change during this period.

2015–16 '1 Billion Dollar Bank Fraud' Anti-Government Protests Protest · 2015–2016

In April 2015, it was revealed that Moldova's three largest banks had lost up to $1 billion (about one-eighth of GDP) through fraudulent loans and transfers in 2014. In a September 2015 march in central Chișinău, protesters chanted 'Give back the billion' and demanded the resignation of President Timofti, the dissolution of the government, an impartial investigation, and early elections. Estimates of turnout differ by a factor of about three between police and organizers. NAVCO 1.3 classifies the 2015–2016 anti-government protests as 'partially successful'.

Amount of OutflowUnconfirmed (original central bank figures not verified)Up to $1 billion, approximately 1/8 of GDP (OCCRP)

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Moldovan Anti-Government Protest' 2015–2016, nonviolent, partially successful. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 14 events in 2015 and 16 events in 2016, the largest number recorded during the coverage period.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Protests over the 2020–2021 Presidential Election Results Protest · 2020–2021

On August 10, 2020, the Central Election Commission of Belarus (CEC) announced the provisional results of the August 9 presidential election: 80.23% for Alexander Lukashenko, 9.9% for Sviatlana Tsikhanouskaya, and a turnout of 84.23% (Euroradio 2020.08.10, citing the CEC announcement). Protests against the results broke out in cities nationwide on the evening of election day, and police used rubber bullets, tear gas, pepper spray, and water cannons to disperse them. The Investigative Committee opened a criminal case on charges of 'large-scale riots' and violence against the dispersing police (same article). A report released by the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights (OHCHR) on March 9, 2022 stated that at least 37,000 people were detained between May 2020 and May 2021 (most under administrative detention for up to 15 days), approximately 13,500 were arbitrarily arrested and detained alone on August 9–14, 2020, and torture and ill-treatment were 'widespread and systematic' (UN News 2022.03). The government describes the protests as large-scale riots, while the opposition and the UN report describe arbitrary detention and human rights violations; both accounts are presented side by side. At the 52nd session of the UN Human Rights Council in March 2023, Deputy High Commissioner for Human Rights Nada Al-Nashif stated that at least five unlawful deaths had been confirmed in the context of the 2020 protests (reporting period 2020.05.01–2022.12.31), and Belarusian Ambassador Larisa Belous rejected the report as one-sided (UNOG Newsroom 2023.03.22).

Data record: ACLED protest counts surged from 170 in 2019 to 1,758 in 2020 and 1,393 in 2021 before declining to 82 in 2022 and 5 in 2023 (data/BLR.json). Mass Mobilization lacks 2020 protests because it covers only up to 2019, and NAVCO 1.3 also lacks a 2020 campaign, recording only the 'Jeans Revolution' of 2006. Archigos and P&T contain no records of abnormal transfers or coups during this period.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Herzegovina Uprising (Nevšinje Uprising) Uprising · 1875–1878

In the summer of 1875, a Christian peasant uprising broke out in the Ottoman-ruled region of Nevesinje in Herzegovina, spreading to Bosnia. It is known as the 'Nevesinjska puška' in Serbia. The two principalities of Serbia and Montenegro initially remained cautious but gradually supported the rebels, joining the Ottoman Empire in war in 1876. The so-called 'Eastern Crisis' — including the Bulgarian uprising (1876) and Russia's declaration of war on the Ottomans (1877) — ended with the Treaty of Berlin in 1878 (Popović·Obrenović 2025, proceedings of an academic conference at the University of Kragujevac). Serbian academia describes this uprising as a 'Serbian liberation movement,' while Ottoman records describe it as a local rebellion; both accounts are presented side by side. Following 1878, Bosnia and Herzegovina came under the occupation and administration of Austria-Hungary.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths · RefugeesUnconfirmed (original Ottoman government official tally not consulted)Unconfirmed (original academic estimates not consulted in this round)

Data Record: Although an event after 1816, Bosnia was not an independent state at the time and is therefore absent from the COW civil war list in data/BIH.json (it may have been recorded under the Ottoman Empire entry, but this was not cross-checked in this round — coverage gap).

  1. Popović, N. & Obrenović, M. (2025), 'The Serb Liberation Response (1875–1878)', proceedings of the academic conference '150 years since the Herzegovina Uprising', pp.190–203, DOI 10.46793/Herzegovina.190P · Academic article · Orientation: Serbian academia (Serbian liberation history perspective) — https://doi.ub.kg.ac.rs/doi/herzegovina-190p/

Bosnian War (1992–1995) Civil War · 1992–1995

Following its independence in 1992, armed conflict broke out between the government of Bosnia and Herzegovina and Bosnian Serb forces (Republika Srpska), Bosnian Croat forces (Herzeg-Bosnia), and the Autonomous Province of Western Bosnia, turning into an internationalized civil war with intervention by Serbia and Croatia (UCDP/PRIO, data/BIH.json). There is no official death toll tally; Bosnian and international officials have long cited approximately 200,000. In June 2007, the Sarajevo-based non-governmental research institute and documentation center IDC released its 'Bosnia Death List,' compiled over four years with Norwegian government funding, stating that at least 97,207 people had died and up to 10,000 more could be added. The victims were 66% Bosniaks (Muslims), 26% Serbs, and 8% Croats, with over 40% civilians (RFE/RL 2007.06.21). Regarding the July 1995 killings of Bosnian men and boys in Srebrenica, the Srebrenica Memorial Center stated that at least 8,372 were killed or missing; both the International Criminal Tribunal for the former Yugoslavia (ICTY) and the International Court of Justice (ICJ, 2007) ruled it a genocide — some Republika Srpska entities have disputed the range of this figure (SEECheck 2024 fact-checking), so both accounts are presented side by side. The war ended with the Dayton Agreement in 1995.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
War Deaths (Total)No official tally — officials have cited approximately 200,000 by convention (RFE/RL 2007)At least 97,207 (IDC Bosnia Death List, 2007)
Ethnic Composition of VictimsUnconfirmedBosniaks 66% · Serbs 26% · Croats 8% (IDC 2007, RFE/RL report)
Srebrenica Killings and Disappearances (1995)Genocide verdict (ICTY·ICJ 2007) — Verdict text not consultedAt least 8,372 (Srebrenica Memorial Center)
Combat Deaths (COW)—48,953 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1, data/BIH.json)

Data Record: COW records 48,953 combat deaths for a single 'Bosnian-Serb rebellion' in 1992–1994, with an end year (1994) that differs from UCDP (Serbian conflicts 1992–1995) and the Dayton Agreement (1995). UCDP divides the Serbian, Croatian, and Autonomous Province of Western Bosnia conflicts into three separate events (data/BIH.json). The IDC figure of 97,207 includes all military and civilian deaths, which differs in definition from COW combat deaths.

  1. RFE/RL (2007.06.21), 'Bosnia: Study Puts War Dead at Fewer Than 100,000' · Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded international broadcaster (reporting on the IDC release) — https://www.rferl.org/a/1077262.html
  2. Srebrenica Memorial Center, 'Srebrenica Memorial Key Messages' · Memorial Foundation · Orientation: Victims' commemoration institution (Bosniak victim perspective) — https://srebrenicamemorial.org/assets/photos/editor/Srebrenica_Memorial_Key_Messages.pdf
  3. SEECheck (2024), 'Revisionist Distortion of Genocide Facts: Victims From Bijeljina to Sarajevo Were Not Buried in Potočari' · Fact-checking · Orientation: Regional fact-checking outlet (rebuts claims of the Republika Srpska side) — https://seecheck.org/?p=9945

February 2014 Protests (Tuzla Protests · Citizens' Assembly) Protest · 2014

In February 2014, protests began in Tuzla by former workers from privatized companies that had closed their doors. The protests spread to major cities such as Sarajevo and Zenica. On February 7, protesters set fire to parts of the local government building in Tuzla and the presidency building in Sarajevo; AP reported that at least 200 people were injured in the clashes on that day (Al Jazeera 2014.02.08, citing AFP and AP). The Center for Investigative Reporting (CIN) reported that approximately 200 people, including police officers and journalists, were injured. Subsequently, citizens held a horizontal citizens' assembly called 'Plenum' to consolidate their demands, and the protests ceased in May (introduction of an academic event at Graz University). NAVCO 1.3 records this period as the 'UDAR' campaign with regime change as its goal, nonviolent methods, and failure (data/BIH.json). Protesters and supporting organizations described it as a social protest against privatization failures and corruption, while some in politics described it as violent unrest; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Injuries (2014.02.07)Unconfirmed (original texts of government/police official tallies not consulted)At least 200 (AP, cited by Al Jazeera 2014.02.08) / Approximately 200 (CIN)

Data Record: The number of protests per year in NAVCO 1.3 'UDAR' (2014, nonviolent, failed) and Mass Mobilization is entered into the table by the generator (data/BIH.json). The names of the protests and the plenum do not match the dataset campaign names (connection rules to be verified).

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Ilinden Uprising and the Krusevo Republic Uprising · 1903

In August 1903 (Ilinden, the Feast of St. Elijah), an uprising led by secret revolutionary organizations broke out in Ottoman-ruled Macedonia, and the uprising forces seized Krusevo to establish a short-lived 'Krusevo Republic.' Ottoman forces recaptured Krusevo on August 12–13. A paper published in the repository of St. Cyril and St. Methodius University of Skopje analyzed contemporary press reports from 1903, noting that hundreds of houses and shops were burned in Krusevo and more than 100 non-combatants were killed, while accounts of the ethnic composition of the murdered civilians diverged among contemporary press outlets. North Macedonia describes this uprising as the origin of state formation, while Bulgaria describes it as part of the Bulgarian national movement; both narratives are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Non-combatant deaths in KrusevoUnconfirmed (original Ottoman government official tally not consulted)Over 100 (1903 press reports, summarized in UKIM repository paper)
Total uprising fatalities and destroyed villagesUnconfirmed200 villages destroyed, 6,000 military and 5,000 civilians killed (Macedonian diaspora organization commemoration page — not academically verified)

Data Record: Although this event occurred after 1816, Macedonia was not an independent state at the time, so it is not included in the COW and UCDP lists in data/MKD.json (coverage gap).

  1. St. Cyril and St. Methodius University (UKIM) repository paper, Krusevo as seen in the press of 1903 · Academic paper · Affiliation: North Macedonian academia — https://repository.ukim.mk/handle/20.500.12188/6788
  2. United Macedonian Diaspora, Ilinden Uprising commemorative article · Memorial foundation · Affiliation: Macedonian diaspora advocacy organization (figures not academically verified) — https://umdiaspora.org/?p=5101

2001 Armed Conflict (NLA Uprising · Ohrid Framework Agreement) Civil War · 2001

In 2001, armed conflict broke out between the Albanian armed organization National Liberation Army (NLA/UÇK) and Macedonian government forces and police. The Uppsala Conflict Data Program (UCDP) estimates that up to 100 people died during the conflict and notes that the last week was the period with the highest number of fatalities. According to a U.S. Congressional Research Service (CRS) report, the Ohrid Framework Agreement was signed on August 13, 2001, amid the most lethal violence of the conflict. Amnesty International's 2002 annual report recorded that more than 170,000 people were displaced at one point during the year and reported accounts of unlawful killings by both sides. The government described it as terrorist and separatist armed activity, while the NLA described it as a struggle for expanded rights for Albanians; both narratives are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed (original government official tally not consulted)Up to 100 (UCDP)
DisplacementUnconfirmedOver 170,000 (Amnesty International 2002 annual report)

Data record: UCDP records 'Government of North Macedonia - UCK' (2001, government issue, fewer than 1,000), whereas NAVCO 1.3 records the 'NLA Insurgency' as a target of 'separatist independence' that failed, so the classification of the issue (government vs. separatist) differs (data/MKD.json). It is not listed in COW's civil war list (appears to fall short of the 1,000 combat deaths threshold).

  1. UCDP, 'Government of North Macedonia - UCK' · Public history database · Affiliation: Academic conflict data repository (Uppsala University) — https://ucdp.uu.se/statebased/877
  2. Amnesty International Report 2002 - Macedonia (Refworld) · Human rights organization · Affiliation: International human rights organization (focused on victims and human rights violations) — https://www.refworld.org/reference/annualreport/amnesty/2002/en/24314
  3. CRS Report for Congress, 'Macedonia: Country Background and Recent Conflict' · Government report · Affiliation: Congressional Research Service (U.S. policy perspective) — https://legistorm.com/reports/view/crs/63225/Macedonia_Country_Background_and_Recent_Conflict.html

Storming of Parliament on April 27, 2017 Protest · 2017

Triggered by the disclosure of wiretapped recordings from 2015–2017, a political crisis ended when, on April 27, 2017, the Social Democratic Union and an Albanian party coalition elected the Albanian Talaat Xhaferi as speaker of parliament. Protesters stormed the parliament building. A live broadcast captured SDSM leader Zoran Zaev with blood on his forehead, and an Albanian party representative, Gjëzën Selado, was injured (citing Reuters reports; Global News · Bloomberg). AP reported that 102 people were injured, citing police statements. The Committee to Protect Journalists (CPJ) recorded at least two journalists injured. The storming side described the election of the speaker as illegal, while the majority coalition described it as a legal election; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Injuries102 (police statement, reported by AP)At least 2 journalists (CPJ)

Data record: Mass Mobilization (4 events in 2017) and NAVCO 1.3 'Colorful Revolution' (2015–2017) are entered into the table by the generator. The storming of parliament itself is not listed in Archigos or the coup list (it was not classified as an attempt to change the government, data/MKD.json).

  2. Bloomberg (2017.04.27), 'EU, U.S. Condemn Storming of Parliament in Rep. of Macedonia' · Media · Affiliation: U.S. business news outlet (citing Macedonian state news agency MIA) — https://www.bloomberg.com/politics/articles/2017-04-27/protesters-storm-into-parliament-in-rep-of-macedonia-mia-says
  3. CPJ (2017.04), 'At least two journalists injured as protesters storm Macedonian parliament' · Human rights organization · Affiliation: Organization advocating press freedom — https://cpj.org/2017/04/at-least-two-journalists-injured-as-protesters-sto/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Chiprovtsi Uprising Ottoman period · 1688

After Habsburg forces occupied Belgrade in September 1688, an uprising broke out around Chiprovtsi in northwestern Bulgaria, led by Catholic Bulgarian merchant class and joined by Orthodox residents. The decisive battle took place near Kultuk (present-day Montana) in October 1688; Ottoman forces captured and destroyed Chiprovtsi. Many survivors migrated to Wallachia under the leadership of Metropolitan Petar Knežević. Bulgarian historiography reconstructs the event based on records of the Catholic community, reports by Dubrovnik merchants, and Habsburg accounts. The number of casualties could not be verified in this round.

  1. Dzheni Ivanova, 'The Chiprovtsi Events from 1688–1690' (abstract of a Bulgarian Academy of Sciences research paper) · Academic article · Affiliation: Bulgarian academia — https://bas.academia.edu/DzheniIvanova
  2. Archaeology in Bulgaria (2016.04.05), report of 17th-century silver coins hidden during the Chifrovtsi uprising discovered · Media · Orientation: Bulgarian archaeology specialist outlet (citing a press release from the National Museum of History) — https://archaeologyinbulgaria.com/2016/04/05/17th-century-silver-treasure-hidden-during-bulgarian-catholics-uprising-against-ottoman-empire-discovered-in-northwest-bulgaria

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

April Uprising and the Batak Massacre Uprising · 1876

In April–May 1876, an uprising for national independence broke out in Ottoman-ruled Bulgaria, and it was suppressed by Ottoman regular forces and irregular units (bashi-bazouks). The British lawyer Edwin Pears, who was then in Constantinople, reported to the Daily News that 37 villages were initially destroyed, followed by 60, and wrote in his memoirs that an official report he had seen estimated the death toll at 12,000. At the request of U.S. Consul Eugene Schuyler, the journalist Januarius MacGahan, who investigated the area, reported finding over a hundred skulls around Batak. This report moved European public opinion and influenced the debate on British policy in the run-up to the 1877–78 Russo-Turkish War. The Ottoman government described it as the suppression of a rebellion, while Bulgaria described it as a national liberation uprising and massacre; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (entire suppression)Unconfirmed (original text of Ottoman government official tally not consulted)12,000 (estimate of the 'official report' seen by Pears, Pears 1916 memoir)
Destroyed VillagesUnconfirmed60 (Pears letter of June 30, 1876)

Data Record: Bulgaria was not an independent state in 1876, so it is not included in the COW civil war list in data/BGR.json (coverage gap).

  1. Excerpts from the Fordham Internet History Sourcebook and Edwin Pears, Forty Years in Constantinople (1916) · Primary Sources · Orientation: British contemporary account (anti-Ottoman public opinion formation side) — https://sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu/mod/1876massacre-bulgaria.asp

June 9 coup and September uprising Civil War · 1923

On June 9, 1923, military and nationalist forces overthrew the Agrarian Union government of Alexander Stamboliyski in a coup, and Stamboliyski was killed. In September of the same year, an armed uprising led by the Bulgarian Communist Party and joined by some members of the Agrarian Union broke out in parts of northwestern Bulgaria but was suppressed, followed by retaliatory violence. The Bulgarian National Radio (BNR) reported in a 100th anniversary article on the June coup that tens of thousands died in the subsequent civil-war-like violence. The National Museum of History of Bulgaria recorded approximately 1,000 deaths in the September events and described it as a small rebellion in limited areas rather than a nationwide uprising. The coup side describes it as restoring order, while the Agrarian Union and Communist Party sides describe it as counterrevolution and white terror; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
September Uprising DeathsUnconfirmed (original text of official government tally not consulted)Approx. 1,000 (Bulgarian National Museum of History, search summary) / combat deaths 1,500 (COW, generator table)
Total Deaths from Violence Since 1923UnconfirmedTens of thousands (BNR article, broad range)

Data Record: COW records 'Agrarian Rising of 1923' (combat deaths 1,500), and NAVCO 1.3 records 'Agrarian League movement' (violence and failure). The June 9 coup is not in Powell-Thyne's list of coups (1950 onwards) nor in Archigos' list of irregular transfers (coverage gap, data/BGR.json).

  1. BNR (Bulgarian National Radio), '100 years since the 9 June coup d'etat' · Media · Orientation: Bulgarian public broadcaster — https://bnr.bg/en/post/101835691/100-years-since-the-9-june-coup-d-etat
  2. BNR, 'The 1923 September Uprising in Bulgarian history' · Media · Orientation: Bulgarian public broadcaster — https://old-news.bnr.bg/en/post/100214107/the-1923-september-uprising-in-bulgarian-history
  3. National Museum of History of Bulgaria, '100 септември 23' · Memorial Foundation · Orientation: National museum (current official Bulgarian historical interpretation) — https://en.historymuseum.org/100-septemvri-23/

Winter 1996–97 Protests and Storming of Parliament Protest · 1997

Large-scale protests demanding early general elections from the Socialists government continued amid the 1996–97 financial crisis and hyperinflation. According to RFE/RL's January 10, 1997 report, thousands of opposition supporters threw stones at and broke windows of the Sofia Parliament building, some breached police barricades and entered the building to set fire to a room, and police drove them out with tear gas. The report stated that at least five people, including three policemen, were injured. The Socialists abandoned forming a new government on February 4, 1997 and agreed to early general elections; the transitional cabinet of Stefan Sofiyanski took office on February 13. The protest side describes it as civil resistance against economic collapse, while the government side describes it as parliamentary violence; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Injuries on January 10Unconfirmed (original text of official Ministry of Interior tally not consulted)At least 5, including 3 policemen (RFE/RL report on the same day) / reports also exist that over 200 were transported to hospitals when the protest was dispersed at night (source unverified)

Data Record: Mass Mobilization annual event counts are entered into the generator table. The NAVCO 1.3 list does not include a separate entry for the 1996–97 protest campaign (only the 1989–90 anti-communist campaign, 2009, and 2013–14 are included, data/BGR.json).

  1. RFE/RL (1997.01.10), report on clashes outside the Bulgarian Parliament · Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded international broadcaster — https://rferl.org/a/1083243.html
