2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Narrative survey pending — not yet written.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

First Serbian Uprising 1804–1813

In early 1804, the rebel Janissary forces (dahis) who had seized Belgrade killed large numbers of Serbian local leaders (knezes), and in February 1804, leaders gathered at Orašac to resolve on an uprising and elected George Petrović (Karadjordje), a merchant, as their leader. The uprising forces drove out the dahis and established an autonomous system, but after Russia concluded a peace with the Ottomans in 1812 they became isolated, and the uprising was suppressed when Ottoman forces recaptured Belgrade in 1813. Karadjordje and most of the commanders fled to Austria. The date of the fall of Belgrade and the scale of reprisals after suppression are stated differently across sources, so they are not confirmed in this round. The Ottoman side describes it as the suppression of a rebellion, while Serbia describes it as the beginning of a national revolution; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. Official website of the Serbian Royal House (Kraljevina Srbija), 'Djordje Petrovic, known as Karadjordje' · Memorial Foundation · Orientation: Karađorđević dynasty (ancestral rehabilitation perspective) — https://kraljevinasrbija.rs/en/the-royal-family-of-serbia/history-of-the-dynasty/djordje-petrovic-known-as-karadjordje/

5. Modern Events through the Literature

October 5, 2000 Revolution (Milosevic's Resignation) Regime Overthrow · 2000

On September 24, 2000, following the results of the Yugoslav Federal Republic presidential election, opposition candidate Vojislav Koštunica claimed victory and led large-scale protests and strikes. On October 5, protesters gathered in Belgrade stormed the federal parliament building; despite police use of tear gas, parts of the parliament were broken and burned, and a fire broke out at the state broadcaster RTS building. Many police ceased resistance. Slobodan Milošević conceded defeat the following day. N1 reports one young woman killed and 65 injured in a traffic accident, while European Western Balkans reports two deaths and more than 60 injuries. Protesters described it as a popular uprising against electoral fraud, while the government at the time described it as an illegal seizure of power by violent forces; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed (original text of the Ministry of Internal Affairs official tally not consulted)1 (N1) / 2 — 1 traffic accident death, 1 heart attack (N1 · Beta 2019, European Western Balkans)
InjuriesUnconfirmed65 (N1) / over 60 (European Western Balkans)

Data Record: The NAVCO 1.3 'anti-Milošević' campaign and Archigos leader turnover records are entered by the generator into tables. Because of the nature of the event, it is not included in the Powell-Thyne coup list (data/SRB.json).

  1. N1 (2022), '22nd anniversary of overthrow of Milosevic regime' · Media · Affiliation: Serbian private broadcaster (affiliate of CNN; received critical evaluations from the government) — https://n1info.rs/english/news/22nd-anniversary-of-overthrow-of-milosevic-regime/
  2. European Western Balkans (2015.10.05), 'October 5 regime change in Serbia – 15 years on' · Media · Affiliation: Policy outlet oriented toward EU integration — https://europeanwesternbalkans.com/2015/10/05/october-5-regime-change-in-serbia-15-years-on/

Protests Following the Novi Sad Station Canopy Collapse Protest · 2024–2025

On November 1, 2024, a concrete canopy at the newly renovated Novi Sad railway station collapsed, killing 16 people (initial reports stated 15). Subsequently, protests demanding accountability and the eradication of corruption, centered on university students, spread nationwide. Regarding the Belgrade rally on December 22, 2024, the Ministry of Internal Affairs estimated a peak of 28,000 to 29,000 participants, while the civic group 'Public Assembly Archive (AoPG)' estimated 100,000 to 102,000. For the Belgrade rally on March 15, 2025, estimates diverged between the Ministry of Internal Affairs' figure of at least 107,000 and AoPG's estimate of 275,000 to 325,000. The first anniversary Novi Sad rally on November 1, 2025, was estimated by AoPG at approximately 110,000 participants. Protesters describe it as a citizen resistance against corruption and accountability evasion, while the government describes it as an attempt to overthrow the regime with foreign intervention; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Canopy Collapse Deaths16 (initially 15)16 (consistent with media reports)
2024.12.22 Belgrade Rally Participants28,000 to 29,000 (Ministry of Internal Affairs)100,000 to 102,000 (AoPG)
2025.03.15 Belgrade Rally ParticipantsAt least 107,000 (Ministry of Internal Affairs)275,000 to 325,000 (AoPG)

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Rebellions

Banská Bystrica Miners' Uprising Modern Era (Hungarian Kingdom period) · 1525–1526

This was a social conflict initiated by miners in the northern mining towns of Banská Bystrica, Banská Štiavnica, and Hodruša-Hámre in the Kingdom of Hungary. The historical account of Banská Bystrica records an armed uprising by miners in 1526, citing as causes the wage cuts imposed by the Pogány family, who managed the mines, and disputes over the operation and finances of the miners' brotherhood (Brotherhood of the Holy Body). The uprising ended in failure for the miners, after which a ruling by Palatine Werbőczy divided leadership of the brotherhood between two guild masters and two miners. Maliniak (2025), an article published in the Slovak Military History Institute's academic journal Vojenská história, summarizes that this event became one of the most thoroughly studied topics in Slovak medieval history thanks to the research of historian Péter Rátzky, and introduces additional evidence from correspondence between Banská Štiavnica and Kremnica regarding the fire in Banská Bystrica at the end of the uprising and its suppression.

  1. Maliniak, P. (2025), 'K organizácii a vojenským aspektom povstania baníkov v Banskej Bystrici v rokoch 1525–1526', Vojenská história · Academic article · Affiliation: Military History Institute of the Slovak Republic — https://www.vhu.sk/9855-sk/maliniak-pavol-k-organizacii-a-vojenskym-aspektom-povstania-banikov-v-banskej-bystrici-v-rokoch-1525-1526/
  2. Banská Bystrica City, 'History of Banská Bystrica' · Government (local authority) · Affiliation: Brief historical narrative for city promotion — https://www.banskabystrica.sk/en/history-of-banska-bystrica/

5. Modern Events through Literature

Slovak National Uprising (SNP) Uprising · 1944

On August 29, 1944, under the command of Major Ján Golian, part of the Slovak Army and partisans rose up against the Tiso government and German forces. According to an article at The National WWII Museum, the uprising began with about 18,000 fighters and grew to around 60,000 by late September, with 12,000–18,000 partisans joining; regular fighting ended on October 27 when Banska Bystrica fell. Over 15,000 soldiers were taken prisoner to Germany, and commanders Golian and Viest were arrested, transported to and executed at Flossenbürg. From September 1944 to March 1945, between 4,000 and 5,300 people were killed in reprisals, and over 100 villages were destroyed. The same article records that partisans also executed at least 100 ethnic Germans in Slobodnica. The European Memorial Portal states that Einsatzkommando 14 and the SS Hlinka Guard killed up to 900 people at the Nemecká lime works. The uprising has been described both as a communist-led anti-fascist struggle under the communist regime and as part of democratic and civic resistance since 1989, while those defending the Slovak State (1939–1945) have evaluated it differently. Both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Uprising Force SizeUnverified (SNP Museum page: approx. 18,000 rebel soldiers initially, 47,000 after mobilization — search summary)Over 80,000 soldiers · approx. 18,000 partisans (National WWII Museum, USA)
Reprisal Killings (1944.9–1945.3)Unconfirmed4,000–5,300 killed, over 100 villages destroyed (National WWII Museum, USA)
Nemecká MassacreUnconfirmedUp to 900 (European Memorial Portal)
Captured and Transferred to GermanyUnconfirmedOver 15,000 (National WWII Museum, USA)

Data Record: Slovakia was the Slovak State under German protection in 1944; its current country code SVK does not continue from it, so it is not listed in data/SVK.json under COW or UCDP (coverage gap). UCDP covers only after 1946.

Candlelight Protest (Bratislava) Protest · 1988

On March 25, 1988, a candlelight prayer rally demanding religious freedom and civil rights was held in Hviezdoslav Square, Bratislava, led by an anti-regime group affiliated with the Catholic underground church. The Slovak National Memory Institute (ÚPN) describes this as the first large-scale protest against the communist regime of Czechoslovakia since 1969, part of the 'third resistance' against communism. Police dispersed it using batons and water cannons and arrested some participants. March 25 later became Slovakia's 'Day of Struggle for Human Rights.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
ParticipantsUnverified (original communist government announcement not consulted)Approx. 6,000 (Slovak state broadcaster RSI) / up to 10,000 (ÚPN publication)

Data Record: MM (1990–) and NAVCO 1.3 have no separate entry (NAVCO 'Public Against Violence' event before 1989–1992). 1988 falls outside the SVK data scope, as it was part of Czechoslovakia.

  1. Nation's Memory Institute (ÚPN), 'Anti-communist resistance and dissent' · Government (National Memory Institute) · Orientation: Memorial institution for anti-communist resistance — https://www.upn.gov.sk/en/anti-communist-resistance-and-dissent
  2. RSI (Slovak state broadcaster international edition), '30 years later: the Candle Manifestation' · Major media · Orientation: Public broadcaster — https://enrsi.stvr.sk/articles/topical-issue/159971/30-years-later-the-candle-manifestation

Protest for a Dignified Slovakia Following the Murder of Journalist Kuciak Protest · 2018

Following the murder of investigative journalist Jan Kuciak and his fiancée Martina Kusnirova in February 2018, large-scale protests spread across the country. The March 9 rally in Bratislava was reported as the largest since 1989, and rallies were held in over 30 cities. Prime Minister Robert Fico resigned on March 15, succeeded by Peter Pellegrini of the same party; protesters continued to hold rallies demanding early elections and other reforms. The government side held it responsible for his resignation, while protesters demanded judicial justice and political renewal.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
March 9 Bratislava ParticipantsUnverified (original police tally not consulted)Over 40,000 (cited by BBC) / up to 50,000 (estimated by public broadcaster, cited by Global Voices)
Nationwide ParticipantsUnconfirmedApprox. 65,000 (week 3 reports) ~ 100,000 (estimates)

Data Record: MM v5.1 records 13 protests in 2018, the highest for any SVK year (data/SVK.json). A government change (prime minister's resignation) occurred but is not listed in Archigos irregular changes or coups (resignation within constitutional procedure).

Anti-Russian Line Protest against the Pišťan Government Protests · 2024–2025

Following Prime Minister Pišťan's visit to Moscow and meeting with President Putin in December 2024, anti-government rallies continued across the country, including in Bratislava, early 2025. On January 24, the organizers claimed over 60,000 participants at a rally in Freedom Square, with simultaneous protests held in approximately 20 cities. The government warned of preventive measures against opposition forces seeking to cause unrest, and Pišťan asserted without presenting evidence that the organizers were plotting to overthrow the government. Protesters demanded an end to the pro-Russian line and Pišťan's resignation. Accounts from each party are presented side by side.

Data Record: ACLED records 197 protest events in SVK for 2024 and 368 for 2025, the highest since 2020 with 0 fatalities (data/SVK.json). MM does not cover the period after 2020.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The 1515 Slovenian Peasant Uprising ('Old Rights' Uprising) Early Modern (Habsburg Carniola) · 1515

This was a peasant uprising that began in Habsburg Carniola and spread across the Slovenian region. A government commemorative article explains that the long-running Habsburg–Venetian War (1508–1516) had severely impacted peasants' social conditions. The rebelling peasants demanded the restoration of 'old rights (stara pravda)' and a voice in tax determination. The uprising was suppressed by an army, including mercenary forces of the Holy Roman Empire. The National Bank of Slovenia's commemorative coin data notes that the slogan of the uprising peasants was one of the first texts printed in Slovenian.

  1. Government of the Republic of Slovenia, 'The Slovenian peasant revolt of 1515' (2019-06-01) · Government · Orientation: Slovenian government commemorative article (national narrative) — https://www.gov.si/en/news/2019-06-01-the-slovenian-peasant-revolt-of-1515/
  2. Banka Slovenije, '500th anniversary of the first Slovenian printed text 2015' · Government (Central Bank) · Orientation: Commemorative coin commentary — https://www.bsi.si/en/cash/numismatics/500th-anniversary-of-the-first-slovenian-printed-text-2015

The Tolmin Peasant Uprising Early Modern (Habsburg Gorizia) · 1713

Amid deteriorating conditions for farmers due to livestock diseases and floods, an uprising broke out when local authorities imposed new taxes on meat and wine. According to an RTV SLO article, after tax collectors imprisoned several delinquent farmers and confiscated salt, hundreds marched to Gorizia, and the authorities released the detainees. The uprising spread to parts of Carniola as well as Gorizia, some priests supported the farmers, Duino Castle was attacked, and miners from the Idrija mercury mine briefly joined in. Imperial troops suppressed the uprising; 11 people were executed, 150 were imprisoned and had their property confiscated, and priests who sympathized were exiled. The same article calls this event 'the last peasant uprising on Slovenian soil' and notes that subsequent reforms under Maria Theresa's government later eased tax rates.

  1. RTV SLO, 'An uprising under the Alps' (Slovenia revealed) · Major media · Orientation: Slovenian public broadcaster (national narrative) — https://www.rtvslo.si/news-in-english/slovenia-revealed/an-uprising-under-the-alps/411928
  2. Banka Slovenije, '300th anniversary of the Tolmin Peasant Revolt 2013' · Government (central bank) · Orientation: Commemorative coin commentary — https://www.bsi.si/en/cash/numismatics/300th-anniversary-of-the-tolmin-peasant-revolt-2013

5. Modern Events through Literature

Ten-Day War (Slovenian War of Independence) Civil War · 1991

Following Slovenia's declaration of independence on June 25, 1991, armed clashes broke out between the Yugoslav People's Army (JNA) and Slovenian Territorial Defense Forces and police; the conflict ended in early July with a ceasefire and the Brioni Declaration. The Slovenian government's 30th anniversary of independence records 45 JNA fatalities and 146 wounded, 19 Slovenian fatalities and 182 wounded, and approximately 4,693 JNA personnel captured as prisoners. The Uppsala Conflict Data Program (UCDP) classifies this conflict as 'Serbia (Yugoslavia): Slovenia' and records 47 JNA soldiers and 6 Slovenian soldiers killed by July 4, reflecting a different counting scope. The shooting down of a JNA helicopter during the war remains a disputed issue, described differently in Slovenia and Serbia to date (BIRN report). Slovenia describes it as a defense of independence, while the Yugoslav federal government at the time described it as a federal border and order-maintenance operation; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
JNA Fatalities45 (146 wounded) — Slovenian Government47 (by July 4, UCDP)
Slovenian Fatalities19 (182 wounded) — Slovenian Government6 soldiers (UCDP)
JNA PrisonersApprox. 4,693 — Slovenian GovernmentUnconfirmed

Data Record: The UCDP records this conflict under Serbia's (Yugoslavia's) state code, so the UCDP list in data/SVN.json is empty. It also does not appear in the COW civil war list (falling short of the 1,000 deaths threshold). NAVCO 1.3 classifies 'Slovenian independence' (1990–1991) as 'nonviolent and successful,' which differs from the armed conflict phase.

  1. Government of Slovenia, '30 years of Slovenia's independence — 30 years ago' · Government · Affiliation: Slovenian government (independent party) — https://gov.si/en/registries/projects/30-years-of-slovenias-independence/30-years-ago/videos
  2. UCDP, 'Serbia (Yugoslavia): Slovenia' · Public history database · Affiliation: Academic conflict data database (Uppsala University) — https://ucdp.uu.se/conflict/384
  3. Detektor (BIRN), 'Pilot's killing in Slovenia's Ten-Day War causes enduring controversy' (2021-06-22) · Major media · Affiliation: Balkan investigative reporting network — https://detektor.ba/2021/06/22/pilots-killing-in-slovenias-ten-day-war-causes-enduring-controversy/?lang=en

2012–2013 Nationwide Anti-Corruption Protests (Maribor Uprising) Protest · 2012–2013

In November 2012, protests against a traffic camera project linked to Maribor Mayor Franc Kangler began in Maribor and spread to cities nationwide; protesters demanded the resignation or prosecution of politicians and elites accused of corruption. Mayor Kangler announced his resignation. In January 2013, the Anti-Corruption Commission reported that Prime Minister Janez Janša (SDS) and Zoran Janković, leader of Positive Slovenia (Mayor of Ljubljana), had systematically violated regulations. According to Freedom House's 'Nations in Transit 2014,' the Janša government was dissolved by parliamentary no-confidence vote in late February, and a new government led by Prime Minister Alenka Bratušek took office in March. Police reportedly stated that they had not exercised crowd control on this scale since the breakup of Yugoslavia in 1991.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Injuries · ArrestsUnconfirmed (original police official tally not consulted)Unconfirmed (figures of 65 injured and 150 arrested found only in the Wikipedia article; academic and media original texts not consulted)

Data Record: MM v5.1 records 2 events in 2012 (2 involving protester violence) and 6 in 2013, making 2013 the highest year among SVN years (data/SVN.json). The government change was a constitutional procedure following parliamentary no-confidence, so it is not included in Archigos's list of irregular changes.

  1. Freedom House, 'Nations in Transit 2014 - Slovenia' (Refworld) · International organization/research institute report · Affiliation: U.S. democracy assessment organization — https://www.refworld.org/docid/5465ea2124.html
  2. Cafébabel, 'Rough guide to Slovenian protests, 2012/2013' · Major media · Affiliation: European youth-oriented online outlet (protestor perspective weight) — https://cafebabel.com/en/article/rough-guide-to-slovenian-protests-20122013-5ae00869f723b35a145e38b4

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Collapse of Pyramid Financial Investments and Nationwide Armed Unrest in 1997 Uprising · 1997

In early 1997, pyramid investment schemes collapsed in a chain reaction, leaving nearly every Albanian household with losses. According to a U.S. Congressional Research Service (CRS) report, many residents blamed the Democratic Party government for failing to regulate these products and profiting from them; unrest spread as armories were looted and state control broke down, particularly in the south. The same report notes that up to 2,000 people died in the first half of 1997. A multinational force of approximately 6,000 troops led by Italy conducted Operation Alba and withdrew in August (the UN Security Council had authorized the deployment on March 28). In subsequent general elections, the Socialist Party won 121 of 155 seats, changing the government. The then-ruling Democratic Party described the unrest as an armed rebellion and collapse of order, while opposition parties and protesters framed it as resistance questioning government responsibility; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (First Half of 1997)Unconfirmed (original text of Albanian government official tally not consulted)Up to 2,000 (U.S. Congressional Research Service)
Size of Multinational Force (Operation Alba)Unconfirmed (original text of UN Security Council resolution not consulted)Approx. 6,000 (CRS) / Approx. 7,000 (GlobalSecurity)

Data Record: Although this is an event with a large death toll (up to 2,000), it does not appear in the COW civil war or UCDP armed conflict lists in data/ALB.json (appears to fail the requirement of organized belligerent violence on both sides). The change of ruling party through general elections is also absent from the Archigos list of irregular transfers of power. It is only partially reflected in the 1997 entry of MM v5.1.

  1. Congressional Research Service, 'Albania: Country Background Report' (98-974) · Government report · Affiliation: U.S. congressional research agency — https://www.everycrsreport.com/reports/98-974F.html
  2. GlobalSecurity.org, 'Albania' (Operation Alba) · Research institution data · Affiliation: U.S. private security information site — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/war/albania.htm

Shooting of Anti-Government Protesters in Tirana on January 21, 2011 Protest · 2011

On January 21, 2011, an opposition-led anti-government protest was held in front of the Prime Minister's Office in Tirana, and protesters were killed by gunfire from the Republican Guard. On January 26, HRW stated in a press release that three protesters had been killed by gunfire; the prosecutor ordered the arrest of six Republican Guard officers, and Prime Minister Sali Berisha publicly criticized the warrant; HRW called for non-interference in the investigation. The 2012 Amnesty International Annual Report recorded that three people were killed by gunfire and one later died, with over 140 police and protesters injured. The Norwegian Helsinki Committee reported four deaths and over 113 arrests. The government described the protest as an attempted coup, while the opposition described it as state violence against a peaceful protest; both accounts are presented side by side.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Kosynsky Uprising Early Modern (Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth period) · 1591–1593

The Encyclopedia of Ukraine describes the 1591–1593 uprising led by Krzysztof Kosynski as the first large-scale uprising of Ukrainian peasants and Cossacks against Polish nobility. Kosynski died in battle during the siege of Cherkasy in May 1593 (same source).

  1. Encyclopedia of Ukraine, 'Kosynsky, Kryshtof' · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Edited by the Canadian Institute of Ukrainian Studies (focus on Ukrainian national history) — https://www.encyclopediaofukraine.com/pages%5CK%5CO%5CKosynskyKryshtof.htm

Nalyvaiko Uprising Early Modern (Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth period) · 1594–1596

Severyn Nalyvaiko became hetman of an independent Cossack detachment in the Brześć region in 1594, and his uprising spread from Brześć to all of Volynia and Belarus. In 1596, the rebel forces were surrounded near Lubny; on July 7, Nalyvaiko and Shaula were surrendered to the Polish side, and Nalyvaiko was executed in Warsaw (Encyclopedia of Ukraine).

  1. Encyclopedia of Ukraine, 'Nalyvaiko, Severyn' · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Edited by the Canadian Institute of Ukrainian Studies (focus on Ukrainian national history) — https://www.encyclopediaofukraine.com/pages%5CN%5CA%5CNalyvaikoSeveryn.htm

Khmelnytsky Uprising Early Modern (Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth period) · 1648–1657

The Cossack uprising led by Bohdan Khmelnytsky began in 1648 and culminated in the establishment of the Zaporozhian Cossack Hetmanate (1648–1782). The Encyclopedia of Ukraine describes him as hetman of the Zaporozhian Host from 1648 to 1657 and founder of the Hetmanate. Large-scale violence against Jewish communities during the uprising period is addressed separately in Jewish historiography; the Ukrainian national history narrative (state-building) and the Jewish historiography narrative (massacres) emphasize different aspects — both narratives are presented side by side.

  1. Encyclopedia of Ukraine, 'Khmelnytsky, Bohdan' · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Edited by the Canadian Institute of Ukrainian Studies (Ukrainian national history perspective) — https://www.encyclopediaofukraine.com/pages%5CK%5CH%5CKhmelnytskyBohdan.htm

Haidamaka Uprisings (1734·1750) Modern Era (Ukrainian Cossacks) · 1734, 1750

The 1734 Haidamaka uprising spread to parts of Podolia and Volynia from the Kyiv region, led by Berlan and Sava Chalych, and was suppressed by Russian forces after Augustus III's accession as King of Poland. The 1750 uprising spread from southern Podolia Guberniya to most of Ukrainian Cossack territory but was suppressed by a coalition of Kyiv, Podolia, and Volynia nobles (Encyclopedia of Ukraine, 'Haidamaka uprisings').

  1. Encyclopedia of Ukraine, 'Haidamaka uprisings' · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Edited by the Canadian Institute of Ukrainian Studies (Ukrainian national history perspective) — https://www.encyclopediaofukraine.com/pages/H/A/Haidamakauprisings.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Koliyivshchyna (1768 Haidamaka Uprising) 1768

The Encyclopedia of Ukraine describes Koliyivshchyna as the largest and most bloody of the Haidamaka uprisings. It broke out in 1768 in the Kyiv and Brześć region, spreading to Podolia and Volynia; it was led by Zaporozhian Cossack Maxim Zhylitsynak and Ukrainian Cossack militia leader Ivan Goncha. The rebels occupied Uman in June 1768, killing Polish nobles, Jews, and members of the Uniate Church, and the uprising was suppressed with Russian assistance. The same encyclopedia cites serfdom and religious oppression of Orthodox peasants and Cossacks as causes, along with the trigger of the Bar Confederation. Dates for the occupation of Uman differ across sources: June 19–21.

  1. Encyclopedia of Ukraine, 'Koliivshchyna rebellion' · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Edited by the Canadian Institute of Ukrainian Studies (Ukrainian national history perspective) — https://www.encyclopediaofukraine.com/pages/K/O/Koliivshchynarebellion.htm
  2. Encyclopedia.com, 'Haidamacks' · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Entry from a Jewish encyclopedia series (focused on Jewish community victimization) — https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/haidamacks

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

EuroMaidan (Revolution of Dignity) and the Collapse of the Yanukovych Regime Regime Overthrow · 2013–2014

Protests that began in Kyiv's Independence Square (Maidan) in November 2013 in protest against the suspension of the EU association agreement escalated in January–February 2014 amid clashes with police use of force. The OHCHR 2014 overview recorded over 100 deaths, including sniper fire, at Maidan in January–February 2014, and a July 2016 report tallied 108 protesters and others along with 13 law enforcement officers killed. As of June 1, 2016, approximately 55 individuals had been charged, including 10 senior officials and 29 former commanders and operatives of the Berkut special police. On February 22, 2014, parliament ousted President Yanukovych from office; the coup data set (Powell–Thyne) classifies this as a successful coup (included in the generator table) — Ukraine describes it as a civic revolution, Russia as an unconstitutional coup, and both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (Protesters, etc.)Unconfirmed (original Ukrainian government tally not consulted)108 (OHCHR 2016.07)
Deaths (Law Enforcement Officers)Unconfirmed13 (OHCHR 2016.07)
Charges (as of 2016.06.01)UnconfirmedApproximately 55 (OHCHR 2016.07)

Data Record: UCDP records 'Government of Ukraine vs Maidan' as a 2014 government-targeted conflict, NAVCO as the 'Euromaidan' campaign, and Powell–Thyne as a successful coup on 2014-02-22 (data/UKR.json) — three data sets classify the same event under different categories.

  1. UN News (2016.07.14), 'UN report finds impunity for killings remains rampant in Ukraine conflict' · International Organization · Orientation: Summary of UN Human Rights Monitoring Mission report — https://news.un.org/en/story/2016/07/534392
  2. OHCHR, Report on the human rights situation in Ukraine (A/HRC/27/75) · International Organization · Orientation: Official UN Human Rights Office report — https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/780616/files/A_HRC_27_75-EN.pdf

Odesa May 2 Clashes Protest · 2014

On May 2, 2014, in Odesa, a 'unification' group and a 'federalism' group clashed, resulting in 48 deaths (summarized by UN News 2016.07.14 from an OHCHR report). OHCHR noted that accountability for deaths at large-scale protests, including Maidan and Odesa, remained severely limited.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed48 (OHCHR 2016.07)

Data Record: This conflict is not recorded as a separate event in the national-level conflict data sets (data/UKR.json) — it is captured only in event-level data (ACLED, etc.).

  1. UN News (2016.07.14) · International Organization · Orientation: Summary of UN Human Rights Monitoring Mission report — https://news.un.org/en/story/2016/07/534392

Donbas Armed Conflict (Donetsk and Luhansk) Civil War · 2014–2022

From April 2014, armed conflict continued in Donetsk and Luhansk between Ukrainian government forces and armed groups of the 'Donetsk People's Republic (DPR)' and 'Luhansk People's Republic (LPR)'. A 2021 source cited by OHCHR estimates deaths from April 14, 2014 to June 30, 2021 at 13,200–13,400 (at least 3,390 civilians, approximately 4,200 Ukrainian military personnel, and approximately 5,800 armed groups), with 14,200–14,400 through the end of 2021. Adding the 298 MH17 victims to OHCHR's September 30, 2021 tally of 3,095 civilian deaths yields at least 3,393. The Ukrainian government describes it as a Russian military intervention, while the Russian government characterizes it as an internal civil war in Ukraine; both accounts are presented side by side. From February 2022 onward, it escalated into interstate warfare (UCDP interstate conflict 2022–2025, included in the generator table).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (2014.04.14~2021.06.30, total)Unconfirmed (original Ukrainian government tally not consulted)13,200–13,400 (OHCHR, citing OSCE-related documents)
Civilian Deaths (~2021.09.30, including MH17)UnconfirmedAt least 3,393 (OHCHR 2021.10 revised edition)

Data Record: COW records 5,826 combat deaths for the 'Donbas War of 2014-present' (internationalized civil war), while UCDP records the DPR and LPR conflicts as internationalized internal conflicts in 2014–2022 (data/UKR.json). The COW combat death toll of 5,826 is close to OHCHR's armed group death estimate (approximately 5,800), but the definitions differ.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The First Prague Defenestration and Hussite Uprising Late Middle Ages (Kingdom of Bohemia) · 1419

On July 30, 1419, a Hussite crowd in Prague threw several members of the city council out of a window to their deaths (the First Defenestration), which subsequently led to the Hussite Wars. In 1618, participants in the later defenestration deliberately re-enacted this 1419 event (German History in Documents and Images).

  1. German History in Documents and Images, 'The War Begins: The Defenestration of Prague (May 1618)' · Public history DB (source collection) · Lean: commentary on sources compiled by the German Historical Institute (GHI) — https://germanhistorydocs.org/en/from-the-reformations-to-the-thirty-years-war-1500-1648/the-war-begins-the-defenestration-of-prague-may-1618

The Second Prague Defenestration and the Bohemian Estates' Revolt Early Modern (Habsburg Bohemia) · 1618–1620

Britannica describes the May 23, 1618, event as resistance to Habsburg authority in Bohemia prior to the Thirty Years' War. In 1617, when Catholic officials closed Protestant places of worship, violating the religious guarantees of the 'Letter of Majesty,' Protestant protectors convened and threw two imperial regents and a secretary out of a window at Prague Castle.

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica, 'Defenestration of Prague' · Academic encyclopedia · Lean: general English-language academic encyclopedia — https://www.britannica.com/event/Defenestration-of-Prague-1618
  2. German History in Documents and Images, 'The War Begins: The Defenestration of Prague (May 1618)' · Public history DB (source collection) · Lean: commentary on sources compiled by the German Historical Institute (GHI) — https://germanhistorydocs.org/en/from-the-reformations-to-the-thirty-years-war-1500-1648/the-war-begins-the-defenestration-of-prague-may-1618

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Warsaw Pact Invasion of Czechoslovakia and Civilian Resistance (End of the Prague Spring) Protest · 1968

On August 21, 1968, the Warsaw Pact forces entered Czechoslovakia to end the 'Prague Spring' reforms, and citizens responded with unarmed resistance. Historians Prokop Tomek and Ivo Pejčoch of the Institute for the Study of Totalitarian Regimes (ÚSTR), based on new records, revised the death toll from August 21 to December 31, 1968, from 108 to 137, with most dying between August 21 and mid-September (Radio Prague International). On the first day alone, more than 50 people were shot dead, and approximately 500 were reported injured (RFE/RL). Over the entire occupation period until 1991, more than 400 people died (Radio Prague). At the time, the Soviet side described it as 'fraternal assistance,' while the Czech side described it as an invasion and occupation; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (1968.08.21~12.31)Unconfirmed (original texts of official counts from Czechoslovakia and the Soviet Union not consulted at the time)137 (ÚSTR Tomek & Pejčoch; previous estimate 108)
InjuriesUnconfirmedApproximately 500 (RFE/RL)
Total Deaths During Occupation Period (~1991)UnconfirmedOver 400 (Radio Prague)

Data Record: Recorded under the NAVCO 'Czech uprising' campaign (data/CZE.json). The COW and UCDP entries for Czechia do not include this event — interstate intervention does not meet the definitions of civil war or internal conflict.

  1. Radio Prague International, 'Historians pin down number of 1968 invasion victims' · Public Broadcaster · Affiliation: Czech public international broadcaster — https://english.radio.cz/node/8184417
  2. RFE/RL (2017), article on the 1968 invasion · Media · Affiliation: U.S. government-funded international broadcaster — https://www.rferl.org/a/28688205.html

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Great Croatian–Slovenian Peasant Revolt of 1573 (Matija Gubec) Early Modern (Habsburg Croatia and Slavonia) · 1573

This was a peasant uprising that broke out in early 1573 in northern Croatia (Zagorje) and adjacent Styria and Carniola. A Global Voices article on the 450th anniversary cites feudal oppression, excessive taxation, and the abuse of Lord Ferenc (Ferrant) Thury as background, stating that the rebelling peasants demanded the abolition of serfdom and recognition of their status as border militia on the Ottoman front. The uprising was suppressed by noble forces near Stubička in about 12 days, and its leader Matija Gubec was executed in Zagreb Square on February 15, 1573. The same article describes subsequent 'bloody reprisals' but does not provide a death toll. Accounts of the method of execution (burning at the stake) mix traditional lore with historical accounts.

  1. Global Voices, 'Not forgotten: The 450th anniversary of the Great Peasant Revolt in Croatia and Slovenia' (2023-02-15) · Major media · Orientation: International citizen journalism outlet (Uprising-side commemorative perspective) — https://globalvoices.org/2023/02/15/not-forgotten-the-450th-anniversary-of-the-great-peasant-revolt-in-croatia-and-slovenia/
  2. Croatia.hr National Portal, 'Matija Gubec' (Croatian greats) · Government · Orientation: Croatian national promotion portal (state narrative) — https://www.hr/croatia/greats/gubec

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Croatian War of Independence (Homeland War) and the Serb Separatist Movement in Kraja Civil War · 1991–1995

Around Croatia's declaration of independence in 1991, Serbs in the Kraja region established the 'Serbian Republic of Kraja' and engaged in armed conflict with Croatian government forces alongside the Yugoslav People's Army (JNA). The Croatian government's 1995 report to the UN Commission for the Prevention of Genocide cites a record from the Ministry of Health's Information Research Department dated January 12, 1994, stating that 8,968 Croatian citizens were killed (2,347 civilians) and 26,443 were injured (7,491 civilians). In Croatia, it is known as the 'Homeland War (Domovinski rat)', while on the Serbian side it has been described as a struggle for self-determination and defense of Serbs in Croatia; both accounts are presented side by side. The number of missing persons has decreased over time according to Croatian government announcements, ranging from 1,860 to 1,792 (by period, Croatian government/HINA).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Croatian Side Deaths (1991–1994.1)8,968 (2,347 civilians) — Ministry of Health of Croatia, Government Report CAT/C/16/Add.6Unconfirmed (original independent tally not consulted)
Croatian Side Injuries (1991–1994.1)26,443 (7,491 civilians) — Ministry of Health of CroatiaUnconfirmed
Missing Persons1,860 → 1,792 (by period, Croatian government/HINA)Unconfirmed

Data Record: COW records this period as the 'Bosnian-Serb Rebellion' (1992–1994, linked to country code Croatia) and the 'Croatia-Krajina War of 1995' (1,000 deaths), and does not link the 1991 Croatian–JNA War to Croatia's country code. UCDP records it as Conflict 390 (1992–1995, territorial, internationalized civil war). NAVCO 1.3 records the Krajina secessionist movement (1991–1995, violent, failed). Because start years and scopes differ across datasets, it is recorded as 'Data Inconsistency'.

  1. Government of Croatia, Initial report to the UN Committee Against Torture, CAT/C/16/Add.6 (UMN Human Rights Library) · Government · Orientation: Official report of the Croatian government (warring party) — https://hrlibrary.umn.edu/cat/croatia1996.html
  2. HINA, report on missing persons tally · Major media · Orientation: Croatian state news agency — https://hina.hr/OTS/4101750
  3. UCDP, Conflict 390 (Croatia: Serbian Republic of Krajina) · Public history DB · Orientation: Academic conflict database (Uppsala University) — https://ucdp.uu.se/conflict/390

Operation 'Storm' in 1995 and Serb Displacement from Krajina Civil War · 1995

An operation in which the Croatian military recaptured areas under Serbian Krajina control in August 1995. In Croatia, August 5 is commemorated as 'Victory and Homeland Gratitude Day', while Serbia and Republika Srpska commemorate it as a day of remembrance for victims during the same period. The Humanitarian Law Center (HLC) notes that approximately 200,000 Serbs fled during or immediately after the operation, with property destruction and looting. Casualty tallies vary significantly across sources: the Croatian Helsinki Committee (2001) reports 677 civilian deaths during or immediately after the operation, while the Serbian Krajina organization 'Veritas' maintains a list of 1,893 deaths and disappearances (1,236 civilians). Official Croatian sources report 243 Croatian military deaths (BTA report). No determination is made.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Croatian Military Deaths243 (2 missing) — Croatian official data (BTA report)Unconfirmed
Serbian Civilian DeathsUnconfirmed (original Croatian official text not consulted)677 — Croatian Helsinki Committee (2001) / 1,893 deaths and disappearances (1,236 civilians) — Veritas (party organization)
Displaced PersonsUnconfirmedApprox. 200,000 — Humanitarian Law Center (HLC)

Data Record: COW's 'Croatia-Krajina War of 1995' battle deaths of 1,000 cannot be directly compared to civilian casualty tallies due to differing definitions (undercounting). This is the final active year of UCDP Conflict 390.

Humanitarian Law Center, 'Marking 29 years since Operation Storm: the political abuse of remembrance' · Human rights organization report · Orientation: Serbian human rights organization (war crimes documentation) — https://www.hlc-rdc.org/en/public-information/press-releases-informisanje/marking-29-years-since-operation-storm-the-political-abuse-of-remembrance/
  2. BTA, 'Croatia Marks 31st Anniversary of Operation Storm' · Major media · Orientation: Bulgarian state news agency (reporting on Croatian official event) — https://www.bta.bg/en/news/1179977-croatia-marks-31st-anniversary-of-operation-storm
  3. Detektor (BIRN), 'Croatian Serbs commemorate victims of 1995 Operation Storm' (2021-08-04) · Major media · Orientation: Balkan investigative reporting network — https://detektor.ba/2021/08/04/croatian-serbs-commemorate-victims-of-1995-operation-storm/?lang=en

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Zbyszko Rokosz (Noble Rebellion) Early Modern (Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth) · 1606–1608/1609

This is the event in which nobles, including Mikołaj Zebrzydowski, a Voivode of Kraków, formed an illegal noble alliance called a rokosz against King Sigismund III Vasa's attempt to strengthen royal power (suspected weakening of the Sejm and introduction of hereditary monarchy). The description of the NCN research project states that historians disagree on the king's deposition declaration by the rokosz side and the year of its conclusion (1608 or 1609), and notes that the only comprehensive academic study on this rebellion is Henrik Schmidt's work from 1858. As a noble rebellion, it differs in character from peasant and urban uprisings, and since the claims to legitimacy of both the royal side and the rokosz side conflict, no judgment is made.

  1. Narodowe Centrum Nauki (NCN), Research Project 394524 Description (Zybrzydowski Rokosz) · Government (Research Foundation) · Affiliation: Polish National Science Centre project summary (academic) — https://projekty.ncn.gov.pl/opisy/394524-en.pdf

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Kościuszko Uprising 1794

This is the event in which Tadeusz Kościuszko declared an uprising on March 24, 1794, in Kraków's Main Square and became commander-in-chief after the Second Partition of Poland (1793). In the Battle of Racławice in April, peasants armed with scythes (kosynierzy) participated, and battles around Warsaw and Vilnius followed. The uprising was suppressed by Russian and Prussian forces, and the Commonwealth ceased to exist with the Third Partition in 1795. The Museum of Polish History describes this uprising in its 2025 exhibition 'The Insurgent Republic' as the starting point of a tradition leading to the January Uprising of 1863 and the 'Solidarity' movement (state narrative).

  1. Muzeum Historii Polski, 'The exhibition The Insurgent Republic is open' (2025) · Government (National Museum) · Affiliation: National Museum of Polish History (national commemorative narrative) — https://muzhp.pl/en/articles/the-exhibition-the-insurgent-republic-is-open

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

June 1956 Poznań Uprising Uprising · 1956

On June 28, 1956, workers at the Stalin Works (formerly Cegielski) in Poznań protested against the withdrawal of wage increase promises and deteriorating living conditions by starting a strike and march; as about 100,000 gathered, the protests expanded to political demands. Protesters stormed the Mwiniska prison to seize weapons, and a gunfight broke out in front of the Ministry of State Security building. The government deployed more than 10,000 troops and suppressed the unrest by June 30. An ENRS article reported approximately 73 deaths, hundreds of injuries, and about 700 arrests, noting that among the dead was 13-year-old Roman Strzałkowski. Polish Radio reported an estimated range of 57 to 79 deaths (including 8 military and police) and over 600 injuries.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (original text of the then-government's official announcement not consulted)Approx. 73 — ENRS (Włusek 2015) / 57–79 estimated — Polish Radio
InjuriesUnconfirmedHundreds — ENRS / Over 600 — Polish Radio
ArrestsUnconfirmedApprox. 700 — ENRS

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 records the 'Poznań protests' as a separate campaign. Archigos records Gomułka's accession (1956) in connection with the political upheaval of October that year, which is a separate item from the Poznań event itself.

  1. ENRS, Andrzej Włusek, 'The Poznań Uprising of June 1956' (2015-08-21) · International Organization/Research Institution Report · Affiliation: ENRS — Common Memory Research Network for Central and Eastern Europe — https://enrs.eu/article/the-poznan-uprising-of-june-1956
  2. Polskie Radio, 'Sunday marks 70th anniversary of the 1956 Poznań June protests' · Major Media · Affiliation: Polish Public Broadcaster — https://www.polskieradio.pl/395/7784/Artykul/3702876,sunday-marks-70th-anniversary-of-the-1956-poznan-june-protests

Bloody Suppression of Worker Protests in Baltic Coastal Cities in December 1970 Protest · 1970

In December 1970, worker protests broke out in Baltic coastal cities such as Gdańsk, Gdynia, Elbląg, and Słupsk, and the authorities suppressed them with force. The bloodiest day was December 17, known as 'Black Thursday,' when gunfire was directed at shipyard workers heading to work in Gdynia. The Institute of National Remembrance (IPN) Poland reports 45 deaths, 1,165 injuries, and approximately 3,000 people arrested after being beaten. Other tallies cite 44 deaths, and the IPN portal noted that some views hold that the actual number of fatalities was higher (dzieje.pl).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths45 — IPN (National Institution)44 — Polish Radio (2020), etc.
Injuries1,165 — IPNUnconfirmed
ArrestsApprox. 3,000 (arrested after being beaten) — IPNUnconfirmed

Data Record: Archigos records Gomułka's departure in the same period (1970) as a change of leadership. This connects to the NAVCO 1.3 'Poland Anti-Communist' campaign segment (refer to data/POL.json table).

  2. dzieje.pl, article on the IPN portal regarding December 1970 · Major Media · Affiliation: Portal operated by the Polish History Museum — https://dzieje.pl/node/7113

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Dózsa György Peasant Uprising Late Middle Ages (Kingdom of Hungary) · 1514

In 1514, about 100,000 peasants gathered for a crusade against the Ottoman Empire; when they turned against their lords, the uprising was led by Dózsa György, a nobleman. Britannica notes that the rebel forces were defeated by Szapolyai János at Temesvár (now Timișoara, Romania), Dózsa was executed on July 20, 1514, and in the same year parliament tied peasants to their land and increased feudal dues and taxes, worsening their socioeconomic position.

  1. Encyclopaedia Britannica, 'Dózsa Rebellion' · Academic Reference Work · Orientation: Anglophone encyclopedia (editorial staff reviewed) — https://www.britannica.com/print/article/170596

5. Modern Events through the Literature

The 1956 Hungarian Revolution and Soviet Suppression Uprising · 1956

On October 23, 1956, student and civilian protests in Budapest escalated into an armed uprising, and the government of Imre Nagy took power; however, on November 4, Soviet troops intervened on a large scale and suppressed it (HISTORY.com). At the time, the Hungarian authorities characterized the event as a 'counterrevolution,' while since 1989 the Hungarian state has commemorated it as the 'Revolution and Struggle for Freedom.' Both characterizations are presented side by side. A KSH infographic notes that approximately 150,000 people had left the country by December 31, 1956, while HISTORY.com describes nearly 250,000 Hungarians leaving the country. The number of fatalities varies widely across sources; the statistical office's tally document from the period ('Number of Deaths and Injuries During the Counterrevolution') is held at the OSA Archives but could not be accessed in this round. Subsequently over the next three years, 200–300 people, including Imre Nagy, were executed (The Orange Files).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Hungarian FatalitiesUnconfirmed (1957 KSH tally document held — OSA, text not reviewed)Unconfirmed (figures of 2,500–3,000 found only in the Wikipedia article)
Departures and RefugeesApprox. 150,000 (by Dec. 31, 1956) — Hungarian Central Statistical Office (KSH)Approx. 250,000 near — HISTORY.com
ExecutionsUnconfirmed200–300 (over the subsequent three years) — The Orange Files

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 records it as a 'Hungarian anti-Soviet' campaign. The COW civil war list in data/HUN.json does not include the 1956 event (only the 1919–1920 Red and White Terror are included), and the Soviet intervention overlaps with the inter-state war category, creating a coverage gap (recorded as such).

  1. HISTORY.com, 'Soviets put brutal end to Hungarian Revolution' · Major Media · Orientation: U.S. history-specialized cable channel — https://www.history.com/this-day-in-history/soviets-put-brutal-end-to-hungarian-revolution
  2. KSH (Hungarian Central Statistical Office), '1956' infographic (2024) · Government (statistical office) · Orientation: Hungarian national statistical agency — https://ksh.hu/infografika/2024/1956_2024_infograf_eng.pdf
  3. OSA Archivum, '[The Number of Dead and Wounded During the Counter-Revolution]' held record · Public history DB · Orientation: Central European University Archive (holds documents from the then-government) — https://catalog-legacy.osaarchivum.org/catalog/osa:39b7e681-7b29-426a-9c8d-ece6689daabb
  4. The Orange Files, 'The 1956 Hungarian Revolution' · Major Media · Orientation: Hungarian political history commentary site (leaning critical of the current government) — https://theorangefiles.hu/the-1956-hungarian-revolution/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Narrative survey pending — not yet written.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Strile War (Strilekrigen) — Resistance to Bergen Special Tax 1765

The entry on Ulrik Frederik de Cicignon, Governor of Bergen in the Store norske leksikon Norwegian encyclopedia, notes that residents rose up against him during the unrest of April 1765. According to the same entry, the tax in question was a special levy imposed from 1762 on all residents to cover the costs of the Holstein War, and its collection was carried out in a fairly harsh manner. The Norwegian historiography refers to this incident, in which farmers from Nordhordland north of Bergen marched into the city to protest, as the 'Strile War.' The number of participants and the results of punishment could not be verified from the original text of the public dictionary in this round and remain unconfirmed.

  1. Store norske leksikon, 'Ulrik Frederik de Cicignon' · Public history DB (encyclopedia) · Orientation: Norwegian academic encyclopedia — https://snl.no/Ulrik_Frederik_de_Cicignon

Lofthus Uprising (Lofthusreisingen) 1786~1787

Store norske leksikon states that farmer leader Christian Jensen Lofthuus led the largest peasant uprising in Norway during the Danish rule of 1786–1787. The rebelling peasants protested against the officials and citizens (merchants) of Agder, and grievances centered on officials collecting excessively high taxes and fees and merchants' trade monopolies. Authorities issued an arrest warrant for Lofthuus, but this only led to new agitation. The Danish Encyclopaedia (lex.dk) states that after local authorities' arrest attempts failed, the Copenhagen government issued an arrest warrant in 1786 and established a committee to investigate peasant grievances. Lofthuus himself was arrested and died in Akershus Fortress in 1797.

  1. Store norske leksikon, 'Christian Jensen Lofthuus' · Public history DB (encyclopedia) · Orientation: Norwegian academic encyclopedia — https://snl.no/Christian_Jensen_Lofthuus
  2. Den Store Danske (lex.dk), 'Christian Jensen Lofthus' · Public history DB (encyclopedia) · Orientation: Danish academic encyclopedia (then-ruling state) — https://lex.dk/Christian_Jensen_Lofthus

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Mentstad Clash (Menstadslaget) Protest · 1931

According to Store norske leksikon, on June 8, 1931, protesters and police clashed in Mentstad, Syen, and later military forces were deployed. The immediate cause was a workplace lockout at the Norsk Hydro Mentstad wharf and Union & Co. by the Scottish police that spring. Approximately 2,000 protesters, mostly members of the Norwegian Chemical Industry Workers' Union, marched to Mentstad, where 100 police officers were guarding replacement workers (strikebreakers). The government dispatched a guard detachment from Oslo, and four naval vessels headed for Mentstad. Twenty-eight people were prosecuted: six acquitted, two fined, and twenty sentenced to prison terms of 30 days to 10 months. Norsk Hydro's company history states that the clash lasted about five minutes, followed by large-scale troop deployments in the area and demands for harsher action from conservative media. The labor side described it as a protest for workers' rights against lockouts and replacement workers, while the government and employers at the time described it as maintaining order and protecting replacement workers; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Protest ParticipantsUnconfirmedApproximately 2,000 (Store norske leksikon)
On-Site PoliceUnconfirmed100 (Store norske leksikon)
Prosecutions and Sentences28 prosecuted — 6 acquitted, 2 fined, 20 sentenced to prison (30 days to 10 months) (courts' verdicts reported by SNL)Unconfirmed

Data record: Because there were no fatalities, this labor dispute is not recorded in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO. Mass Mobilization data cover only events from 1990 onward.

  2. Norsk Hydro, '1931 labor conflict' (company history) · Corporate source · Orientation: History of the company that was the employer at the time — https://www.hydro.com/en/about-hydro/history/1929---1945/1931-labor-conflict/

July 22 Oslo and Utøya Terror Attacks Uprising · 2011

On July 22, 2011, a far-right lone actor detonated a car bomb in the government quarter of Oslo before shooting up a Labour Party youth camp on Utøya Island. The 22 July Committee, established by the government on August 12, 2011 (chair: Alexandra Bech Gjørv), submitted its official report (NOU 2012:14) to the Prime Minister in August 2012 and concluded that the government building attack could have been prevented if security measures already adopted had been implemented, and that the perpetrator on Utøya could also have been stopped earlier. Fatalities are recorded as 8 in the government quarter and 69 on Utøya, for a total of 77. This event is an act of political violence by a lone actor rather than a popular uprising; it is placed in the 'Uprising' cell among the series categories but its nature is specified as such.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Fatalities (Total)77 — Norwegian Government · 22 July Committee77 (Britannica)
Fatalities (Utøya)6969 (most media), some early reports 67
Fatalities (Government Quarter)88

Data record: Because it does not meet the UCDP armed conflict (state vs. organization) criteria, it is not included in the data list. ACLED aggregated data (2020–) do not cover this year. The nature of this event differs from the dataset's 'Uprising' category, so it is recorded as an anomaly beyond the series comparison.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Count's War (Grevens Fejde) and the Peasant Uprising of Skjærbæk Clement Reformation Period (Civil War) · 1534–1536

The Danish Great Encyclopedia (lex.dk) describes how, after King Frederick I died in 1533 and the State Council delayed the election of a new king, the 'Count's War' broke out as a civil war in 1534–1536. According to the same encyclopedia's entry on Skjærbæk Clement, the former pirate captain Clement (Clemens Andersen), who had previously served Christian II, was dispatched to Odense with two ships in September 1534 and immediately seized the city; within weeks he raised peasants and townspeople in northern and western Jutland. During the uprising, several manors of nobles in northern and western Jutland were burned. Clement's peasant army defeated the Jutland noble cavalry at Svenstrup on October 16 but was defeated near Randers around November 1. After John Rantzau's attack on Odense on December 18, Clement was captured by a farmer and handed over to Rantzau; he was beheaded in Viborg in 1536. The date of his execution is given as June 9 by lex.dk and September 9 by other sources, differing from each other. The uprising is described by the rebel side as an uprising of peasants and townspeople against noble rule, and by the nobles and king's side as the suppression of a rebellion; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. Den Store Danske (lex.dk), 'Grevens Fejde' · Public history DB (encyclopedia) · Orientation: Danish academic encyclopedia — https://lex.dk/Grevens_Fejde
  2. Den Store Danske (lex.dk), 'Skipper Clement' · Public history DB (encyclopedia) · Orientation: Danish academic encyclopedia — https://lex.dk/Skipper_Clement
  3. Den Store Danske (lex.dk), 'Clementsfejden' · Public history DB (encyclopedia) · Orientation: Danish academic encyclopedia — https://lex.dk/Clementsfejden

5. Modern Events as Recorded in Literature

1944 Copenhagen General Strike (Folkestrejken) Uprising · 1944

On June 26, 1944, approximately 1,200 workers at the B&W shipyard protested against the German occupation authorities' state of emergency and curfew by stopping work, which sparked a general strike in Copenhagen against the occupation regime. The 'Folkestrejke' (general strike) entry and the 'Hvidsten Group Execution' entry in the Danish Encyclopedia (lex.dk) note that the execution of the resistance Hvidsten group on June 29 was intended to intimidate Copenhagen residents into ending the strike, but the strike instead expanded the next day. A supplementary document to a proposal by the Copenhagen City Council (2024) records that Copenhagen citizens were killed by occupation authorities during this strike and proposed an 80th-anniversary memorial (July 5, 2024), though the total number of fatalities was not small. NAVCO 1.3 records the Danish resistance (1940–1943) as nonviolent and partially successful, and the 1944 general strike falls outside this period.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
First striking workers (B&W)UnconfirmedApproximately 1,200 (Copenhagen City Council proposal supplementary document · lex.dk)
FatalitiesUnconfirmed (German occupation authorities' tallies not reviewed)Many — total figure unconfirmed (Copenhagen City Council supplementary document)

Data record: The Danish resistance campaign in NAVCO 1.3 ends in 1943 and does not include the 1944 general strike. It is also absent from COW and UCDP.

May 18, 1993 Brainstrup Protest and Police Shooting Protest · 1993

On May 18, 1993, Denmark approved the Maastricht Treaty in a second referendum. That night in Nørrebro, Copenhagen, approximately 100 anti-EU protesters erected barricades in some areas and declared an 'area without the EU'; police fired on the protesters, wounding at least 11 (European civil liberties monitoring organizations Statewatch and FECL). Statewatch reported that police had also fired warning shots over protesters' heads twice several days earlier. The police announced that 103 officers were injured, but a follow-up report by Statewatch stated that fewer than 50 officers visited the emergency room, giving different figures. The 1994 report to the Attorney General commissioned by the Minister of Justice, which defended the police response, drew criticism; in 1996 an investigative committee was established and issued its report in 2000 (lex.dk 'Nørrebrosagen'). The police side describes the use of force as self-defense during riot suppression, while protesters and civil society groups describe it as excessive force; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Wounded ProtestersUnconfirmed (figures from the Attorney General's report not reviewed)At least 11 (Statewatch)
Injured Police Officers103 — announced by the Copenhagen policeFewer than 50 visited emergency rooms (Statewatch)

Data Record: Presumed to be one of two Mass Mobilization records from 1993 (noting violence participants), but individual records have not been cross-checked. A 'shootings' state response in 1993 may correspond to this event.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Peasant unrest in Vidzeme and the enactment of the Vidzeme Land Law 1800~1819

A bachelor's thesis from the Faculty of Humanities at the University of Latvia (Lācis, 2025, supervised by Gvido Straube) analyzes the impact of peasant unrest that occurred in Vidzeme Province (Russian Empire Livonia) in the early 19th century on the 1804, 1809, and 1819 Vidzeme Land Laws, concluding that the impact varied across laws and cannot be answered with a single response. The thesis's keywords are peasant unrest, Landtag, province (gubernia), tsarist government, and Alexander I. The specific course of the unrest in the Kauguri estate near Valmiera in 1802 and its suppression by the military, along with the ensuing punishments, could not be verified from original sources in this round (unverified). The lordship and imperial authorities described the events as restoring order and reforming the law, while peasants described them as resistance to servitude and serfdom; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. Lācis, L. Ģ. (2025), 'Zemnieku nemieru ietekme uz agrārajiem likumiem Vidzemes guberņā (1800-1819)', Bachelor's thesis, University of Latvia · Academic (thesis) · Affiliation: Latvian academia, bachelor's level — https://dspace.lu.lv/handle/7/71369

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1905 Revolution and Punitive Expeditions Uprising · 1905–1906

Amid the revolution across the Russian Empire in 1905, strikes, peasant uprisings, and burning of manor houses also occurred in the Latvian region (Courland and Livonia). A speech by the Ambassador of Latvia to Canada published by the Latvian Ministry of Foreign Affairs (2020) states that a special punitive expedition known as the 'Black Cossacks' was dispatched to suppress this movement, and that they executed 1,170 people without trial or investigation, burned down 300 farms, and exiled thousands to Siberia. A list of dissertations on Liepāja from the University of Latvia records that the first punitive expedition did not begin 'pacifying' the area around Liepāja until early 1906. The imperial authorities described this as suppressing rebellion and restoring order, while Latvian commemorative accounts describe it as repression of a national and social liberation movement; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Summary Executions by Punitive ExpeditionsUnconfirmed (Imperial authorities' tallies not consulted)1,170 (Ambassador's speech published by the Latvian Ministry of Foreign Affairs, 2020)
Burned FarmsUnconfirmed300 (Same source)
Siberian ExileUnconfirmedThousands (Same source)

Data Record: The 1905 Latvian uprising is not recorded separately in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO (outside the scope of unit records for the Russian Empire).

January Barriers and the January 20 OMON Attack in 1991 Protest · 1991

From January 13, 1991, Riga citizens erected barricades to protect the Supreme Council, Ministry of Internal Affairs, and broadcasting center. A commemorative notice from Riga Stradiņš University (RSU) states that the January 20 attack left 5 dead and 13 barricade participants injured; it notes that photographer Andris Slāpins was killed by a gunshot and photographer Gvido Zvīgzne suffered fatal injuries among the fatalities. A Baltic Times article cites student Edīte Riiekstina, police officers Vladimirs Gomonojevics and Sergejs Kononenko, and photographers Slāpins and Zvīgzne as the fatalities. The Soviet side (OMON) described it as an operation to restore order, while the Latvian side described it as a violent attack during the process of regaining independence; both accounts are presented side by side. January 20 was established in 1997 as Barricades Defenders' Memorial Day.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Fatalities on January 205 (Latvian official commemorative account, RSU notice)5 (Baltic Times reporting names)
Injured (barricade participants)13 (RSU notice)Unconfirmed

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 classifies this period as a 'democratic movement (1989–1991, nonviolent and successful).' The separate death on January 16 by OMON (driver Roberts Murnieks) could not be confirmed from primary sources in this round.

The Baltic Times, '1991 barricades remind that state is not just border posts, buildings and slogans - Rinkevics' · Media · Orientation: English-language media of the Baltics — https://www.baltictimes.com/1991_barricades_remind_that_state_is_not_just_border_posts__buildings_and_slogans_-_rinkevics/

Riga unrest on January 13, 2009 Protest · 2009

Amid the global financial crisis, more than 10,000 citizens gathered in Riga's Dome Square on January 13, 2009 to demand the dissolution of parliament (Saeima) and early general elections from President Valdis Zatlers. According to The Baltic Times (2009-01-14), the rally was organized by the opposition party 'Social Movement for Different Politics' and several NGOs; after the peaceful rally, hundreds of youths sparked unrest toward the parliament building. Several police officers (10 according to photo captions) and civilians were injured, and more than 100 people were detained. The organizers stated that they opposed violence and had not planned the unrest. Official accounts emphasize punishment of violent actors, while protest accounts stress government responsibility; both narratives are presented side by side.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Samogitian (Žemaitian) Uprising Middle Ages (era of Teutonic Order rule) · 1401, 1409

According to the entry on the 'Žemaitijos sukilimas' in the Lithuanian Encyclopedia (vle.lt), on March 13, 1401, Samogitians separated the main force of the Teutonic Order from its crusader contingents, burned down the newly built castles of Dubysa and Priebruk, and captured the garrisons. The encyclopedia notes that free peasants, who constituted the majority of the population, resisted the order's rule and the introduction of serfdom most strongly. In 1404, under the Treaty of Laisvėnai, Samogitia was once again ceded to the Order; the inhabitants obstructed castle construction, disobeyed orders, or fled to Lithuania, while the Order closed the borders and blocked trade. In May 1409, insurgents burned down Skirsnemune Castle and killed the knights, while another group attacked Klaipėda, causing significant damage to German settlements without capturing the castle. A cavalry detachment under the noble Rumbautas Valiavaitis, at the behest of Vytautas, provided support. The encyclopedia describes that this uprising led to Samogitia being reunited with Lithuania and leaving only the Klaipėda area under the Order's control, and that Vytautas' support was one of the causes of the Battle of Grunwald (Žalgiris) in 1410. The Order side described it as a rebellion within its territory under the treaty, while the Lithuanian side described it as a liberation uprising against foreign rule; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. Visuotinė lietuvių enciklopedija (vle.lt), 'Žemaičių sukilimai' · Public History DB (National Encyclopedia) · Orientation: Lithuanian academic encyclopedia (Lithuanian perspective) — https://www.vle.lt/straipsnis/zemaiciu-sukilimai/

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

December 17, 1926 Military Coup Coup · 1926

The entry on the '1926 Lithuanian coup' in the Danish Encyclopedia (lex.dk) states that on December 17, 1926, nationalist-minded officers ousted the Social Democratic coalition government led by President Kazys Grinius and Mikelis Sleževičius. Two days later, parliament elected Antanas Smetona president with the support of the Christian Democrats and nationalists, while other parties refused to vote. Smetona dissolved parliament in 1927; the 1928 constitution expanded presidential powers, and the 1938 constitution established a de facto personal dictatorship. The same entry notes that the coup's cause is not explained by a single factor, citing as background the instability of 13 prime ministers since 1918, officers' concerns about a government majority relying on minority parties (Polish- and German-speaking), land reform, and plans to reduce the military; it also mentions Pilsudski's march on Warsaw in May 1926 and Mussolini's rise to power as influences. The coup forces described the measures as necessary for national stability, while the ousted government side described them as an interruption of democratic constitutionalism, and both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
CasualtiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (not mentioned in lex.dk)

Data Record: Archigos 4.1 records Grinius's ouster as 'overthrow by a military without foreign support' (1926-12-19) and Smetona's accession as an irregular entry. The coup dataset (coups_pt) covers only events from 1950 onward and contains no entry for this event.

  1. Den Store Danske (lex.dk), 'statskuppet i Litauen 1926' · Public History DB (Encyclopedia) · Orientation: Danish academic encyclopedia (third-country perspective) — https://lex.dk/statskuppet_i_Litauen_1926

January 13, 1991, Vilnius TV Tower Incident Protest · 1991

After Lithuania's declaration of independence in March 1990, on January 8, 1991, Soviet troops occupied the Ministry of Defense and the House of the Press. Vilnius citizens formed human shields around key buildings, including parliament and the TV tower. The exhibition at the Lithuanian National Museum of Recent History (LTMKM) states that immediately after midnight on January 13, the military surrounded the TV tower, fired live rounds over crowds and into unarmed civilians, and drove tanks through the streets. The museum tallies 14 deaths by gunfire or under tanks and one death from a heart attack, with 702 people sustaining injuries of varying severity. The Soviet side describes it as a military operation to restore order, while the Lithuanian side describes it as an armed attack on an independent state; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other

Injuries702 (same museum)A Lithuanian Ministry of Foreign Affairs page listing over 800 injured was found in search results but could not be accessed due to a blocked connection (403)

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 categorizes it as 'Democratization Movement/Reasons' (1988–1991, nonviolent and successful). It is absent from COW and UCDP.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Engelbrekt's Rebellion Late Middle Ages (Kalmar Union period) · 1434–1436

According to the commentary of the Swedish education portal SO-rummet (Herman Lindqvist, 2020), the uprising began on the night before Ash Wednesday in 1434 when miners and peasants from Dalarna burned down the bailiff's castle at Borga in Borgholm, led by Engelbrekt Engelbrektsson. Background factors included economic decline following the Battle of Västerås, salt shortages and new taxes, the cessation of iron exports due to the Baltic War and unemployment, and the tax pressure exerted by bailiffs appointed by King Eric of Pomerania (especially Bailiff Yse Eriksen of Vestero). The uprising spread to Svealand and Östergötland, and in August 1434 the Riksdag at Västerås withdrew its oath of loyalty to King Eric. The 1435 Arbrå meeting, attended by representatives of all estates, is known as the 'first Riksdag'. Engelbrekt became supreme commander (rikshövitsman) and member of the council of state, but on April 27, 1436, he was murdered by the nobleman Mönk Bengtsson (Natt och Dag) on a small island in Lake Yllefjärden while traveling to Stockholm. The royal party described it as rebellion, whereas later Swedish accounts and narratives describe it as resistance against foreign bailiffs; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. SO-rummet, 'Engelbrektsupproret 1434-1436' (Herman Lindqvist) · Education portal (commentary) · Orientation: Swedish educational commentary, journalist author — https://www.so-rummet.se/fakta-artiklar/engelbrektsupproret-1434-1436

Dacke Uprising (Dackefejden) Early modern period (formation of the Vasa state) · 1542–1543

The 'Nils Dacke' entry in the Swedish National Archives' Swedish Biographical Lexicon (SBL), edited by Albert Vejde, regards the killing of King's bailiffs including Nils Larsson and others at Västerås in June 1542 by Dacke and his men as the beginning of the Dacke uprising. Causes cited include Gustav Vasa's restrictions on border trade with Denmark, price controls, heavier taxes, harsh bailiffs, restrictions on grazing by forest settlers, and Catholic resentment toward Lutheran clergy. Dacke negotiated as an equal party with the Riksdag at the Linsköping ceasefire of November 8, 1542, receiving a pardon in exchange for an oath of loyalty; on November 25 he proclaimed peace at a meeting in Växjö and appointed bailiffs while restoring Catholic worship. Fighting resumed in February 1543, and around March 20 the rebel forces were defeated near Birseleum, where Dacke was shot in both thighs. He reappeared in May but with weakened support, and was killed while fleeing in the Rödeby forest at the end of July. The royal party described it as treason, whereas peasant accounts describe it as defending ancient rights; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. Svenskt biografiskt lexikon (Riksarkivet), 'Nils Dacke' (Albert Vejde) · Public history DB (National Archives biographical dictionary) · Orientation: Swedish academic biographical dictionary — https://sok.riksarkivet.se/sbl/artikel/15765

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

The Ådalen shootings of 1931 Riots · 1931

According to SO-rummet's 'Ådalen shootings' topic page, a strike at Långrörs fabriker AB turned violent when employers used replacement workers to unload ships; protests were held in response, and on May 14, 1931 (Ascension Day), the day after the first protest rally, soldiers called from Sollefteå fired on protesters in Örnskoldsvik, killing five and wounding five. The radio commentary on the same page notes that the strike was against wage cuts. The incident sparked widespread outrage and led to a debate at the Riksdag that had not been on the agenda; unlike the communists, the Social Democrats took the position that violence against replacement workers could not be tolerated. SVT (2021) also records five deaths. The military and authorities describe it as an incident occurring while maintaining order, while the labor movement describes it as a shooting of unarmed protesters, and both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (original text of the investigation committee not consulted)5 (SO-rummet, SVT)
InjuriesUnconfirmed5 (SO-rummet)

Data Record: Not found in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or MM (1990 onward). Deaths in prewar labor protests fall outside the scope of these datasets.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Resistance and Execution of Bishop Jón Arason Reformation Period · 1548–1550

At the time, Iceland was under the rule of the Kingdom of Denmark-Norway, and King Christian III of Denmark applied the Lutheran Reformation to the island. The last Catholic bishop of Hólar, Jón Arason, resisted this. According to an article by the Institute for Christian History (Christian History Institute), Arason was arrested along with his two sons Ari and Björn and taken to Skálholt, where he was executed in November 1550 without a formal trial, together with his two sons. Following this, church and monastery lands were transferred to the royal household, and organized resistance against the Reformation in Iceland came to an end. The Catholic and national history sides describe it as resistance against foreign coercion, while the royal authority side describes it as suppression of rebellion under the law (banishment sentence); both accounts are presented side by side. Accounts differ on the circumstances of his arrest (whether the Battle of Sauðafell occurred) and the exact date of his execution (November 6 or 7).

  1. Christian History Institute, 'God and man in Iceland, part II' (2016) · Article by a religious history research institute · Orientation: Christian history perspective (may include favorable accounts of Catholic resistance) — https://christianhistoryinstitute.org/blog/post/god-and-man-in-iceland-part-ii
