5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

March 30, 1949 Anti-NATO Membership Protest at Austurvöllur Protest · 1949

On March 30, 1949, while the Alþingi (parliament) voted to join NATO, opponents gathered in front of the parliament building on Austurvöllur Square. Reykjavík Grapevine (2017) notes that police fired tear gas to disperse protesters after stones were thrown at the parliament building, and explains that there was widespread perception that it was contrary to Iceland's principle of peace that a country that had gone without an army since 1869 should join a Cold War military alliance. The government describes the protest as a defense of parliament, while opponents describe it as a protest to defend peace and neutrality; both accounts are presented side by side. The number of injured and the number of convictions could not be verified from the original text in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
InjuredUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (original text not consulted)
Convicted PersonsUnconfirmed (original Alþingi document text 403)Unconfirmed

Data Record: This is an event absent from COW, UCDP, NAVCO, and Mass Mobilization (1990–).

  1. Reykjavík Grapevine, 'Time Capsule: Austurvöllur Square' (2017-12-08) · Major Media (Local English-Language Outlets) · Orientation: Reykjavík English-language cultural and current affairs outlet — https://grapevine.is/mag/2017/12/08/time-capsule-austurvollur-square

The 'Pots and Pans Revolution' of 2008–2009 Protest · 2008–2009

Following the collapse of the banks in October 2008, citizens continued to protest outside Austurvöllur Square in front of the parliament demanding the resignation of the Independence Party–Social Democratic Alliance coalition government, and protests continued throughout January 2009. Reykjavík Grapevine's 2009 year-end summary notes that Prime Minister Geir H. Haarde resigned in January and a left-green–social democratic emergency coalition government took office, and that Central Bank Governor Davíð Oddsson stepped down in February. The date of the government's resignation (January 23 or 26) differs across sources. NAVCO 1.3 records this campaign as nonviolent and successful.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
ParticipantsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (original text not consulted)
FatalitiesNone reportedNone reported (based on reviewed materials)

Data Record: Consistent with NAVCO 1.3 'Cutlery Revolution' (2008–2009, nonviolent · success).

  1. Reykjavík Grapevine, 'The Year In Brief' (2010-01-14) · Major Media (Local English-Language Outlets) · Orientation: Reykjavík English-language cultural and current affairs outlet — https://grapevine.is/society/article/2010/01/14/the-year-in-brief/

Anti-Government Protests over the Panama Papers in 2016 Protest · 2016

Following revelations in April 2016 from the Panama Papers that Prime Minister Sigmundur Davíð Gunnlaugsson had owned an offshore company (Wintris) without disclosing it, a large protest was held outside the parliament building on April 4. The prime minister denied any wrongdoing. UPI and CBC reported that the prime minister resigned on April 5, but a statement from the Prime Minister's Office spokesperson stated that he would step down for an unspecified period while retaining his party leadership post, leading to conflicting announcements on the same day. Progressive Party deputy leader Sigurður Ingi Jóhannsson took over as prime minister. The government describes it as a matter of the prime minister personally, while protesters describe it as a conflict-of-interest issue; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
April 4 Protest ParticipantsApprox. 8,000 — police estimate (cited by CBC)Approx. 22,000 (Reykjavík Grapevine)

Data Record: Consistent with NAVCO 1.3 'Panama Papers Anti-Government Protest' (2016, nonviolent · partial success). Individual cross-checks against the Mass Mobilization 2016 record were not conducted.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Night of St. George's Uprising (Jüriöö ülestõus) Middle Ages (under Danish and German knightly rule) · 1343–1345

In 1343, Estonian natives in Harju (Härjedalen), a Danish-ruled territory of the Duchy of Estonia, rose up against their Danish and German rulers and the feudal lords. The uprising spread to the Bishopric of Ösel-Wiek in western Estonia and the Livonian Order's island territories. The Danes brought in the Livonian Order through the Bishop of Reval (Tallinn), and the order suppressed the Danish-ruled uprisings by the end of 1343, while Saaremaa resisted until February 1345. In 1346, the Danish king sold the Duchy of Estonia to the Livonian Order. Opinions differ on the cause of the uprising: some point to Valdemar IV's special taxation, while others argue that the order used the uprising to seize control of all of Livonia. Estonian national historiography regards it as a liberation uprising against foreign rule, whereas contemporary German chronicles describe it as a pagan rebellion; both accounts are presented side by side.

  1. Tallinna Linnamuuseum (City of Tallinn Museum) blog, 'Carlsberg Foundation 19: A fresh look at the St. George's Night's Uprising' · Public institution (museum) · Orientation: City of Tallinn Museum (research introduction) — https://blogi.linnamuuseum.ee/?p=3839
  2. Leiden University Scholarly Publications — Research on the Night of St. George's Uprising · Academic article · Orientation: Academic (Netherlands university repository) — https://scholarlypublications.universiteitleiden.nl/access/item%3A4308505/view

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

Communist Coup Attempt of December 1, 1924 Coup · 1924

On December 1, 1924, the Communist Party of Estonia and Bolshevik armed detachments, many of whom had infiltrated from the Soviet Union, attempted a coup in Tallinn under Comintern instructions. The Estonian public broadcaster ERR reported that insurgents stormed the military academy and initially succeeded but government forces soon regained control. Earlier, on January 21, 1924, police preemptively cracked down on Communist cells, arresting over 200 leaders, and in November a trial of 149 Communists was held. The government side describes it as the suppression of an armed coup organized by foreign powers, while the Communist and Soviet sides describe it as a workers' uprising; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Government Fatalities26 (including military personnel and cadets) — ERR reportApprox. 20 (including Transport Minister Karl Kark) — other literature, varying by source
Insurgent FatalitiesApprox. 125 (at least 2 arrested and summarily executed) — ERR reportUnconfirmed

Data Record: Outside the period covered by Powell & Thyne coup data (1950–), and absent from COW and UCDP as well.

The 'Bronze Night' of 2007 (Protests against the relocation of the Bronze Soldier) Protest · 2007

On April 26–27, 2007, protests centered on Russian-speaking residents opposing the relocation of the Soviet war memorial 'Bronze Soldier' in central Tallinn escalated into riots; the government relocated the monument on April 27. The Baltic Times reported that a Russian-speaking protester (Dmitry, born in 1987) was stabbed with a knife and died during surgery. The government side describes it as legitimate administrative measures to quell unrest and relocate the monument, while Russian-speaking residents and the Russian government describe it as protest against the desecration of historical memory; both accounts are presented side by side. Subsequently, defendants accused of organizing the unrest were acquitted (The Baltic Times).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

Detentions/ArrestsOver 1,160 (officially cited in the European Court of Human Rights judgment text)Approx. 1,200 detained, most released within hours (The Baltic Times)

Data Record: Mass Mobilization 2007 records three events (three involving violent participants) that appear related to this event, but individual records have not been cross-checked.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Cudgel War (Nuijasota) Early modern era (Swedish Kingdom rule; House of Vasa civil war) · 1596–1597

According to the English commentary published by the Jaakko Ilkka Association (Jaakko Ilkka seura ry), even after the end of the 25-year war with Russia in 1570–1595, King Charles IX's Finnish representative Claus Fleming did not disband the army, so the soldiers continued to impose feudal burdens on peasants, and nobles and officials sought to keep peasants in a state of servitude. The trade embargo imposed at the end of the confrontation between Fleming and Duke Charles struck merchant shipping, leading merchants and tenant farmers to join the uprising. The commentary also describes this as part of a power struggle among the descendants of Gustavus Vasa. Ostrobothnian peasants elected Jakko Ilkka as their commander. In the last few days of 1596, Fleming at Nokia dispersed the peasant camp with false promises and Ilkka went into hiding. Captured peasants were killed, and uprising commanders were executed; Ilkka was executed before Fleming's letter dated January 29, 1597. The uprising ended in defeat for the peasants at the Battle of Santavuori on February 24, 1597. After Duke Charles ascended the throne, participants in the uprising recovered property seized illegally. The king (Sigismund) and Fleming's side described it as a rebellion, while later local accounts and Finnish historiography describe it as resistance against unjust burdens; views coexist in Finnish historical scholarship that see it as part of the Karl–Sigismund power struggle (e.g., Heikki Ylikangas) and as class conflict. The number of fatalities is not recorded in the primary sources verified in this round (unverified).

  1. Jaakko Ilkka seura ry, 'In English' (Cudgel War commentary) · Local history association commentary · Orientation: Association commemorating uprising leader — close to the peasant perspective — https://jaakkoilkka.fi/?p=182

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Finnish Civil War of 1918 Civil War · 1918

The Civil War was an internal conflict fought from January to May 1918 between the Red Guards (a Social Democratic-aligned militia) and the White Guards (civil defense forces loyal to the government) immediately following Finland's declaration of independence in 1917. Finnish public broadcaster Yle (reported on 2004-05-27, updated in 2008), citing the 'Finland at War 1914–1922' electronic database compiled by Professor Heikki Ylikangas, reported 26,100 total war dead (including approximately 1,000 with Russian, Swedish, or German nationality), 5,200 Red Guard combat deaths (1,800 more than previous estimates), and 13,500 Red Guard prison camp deaths (mostly from disease, many presumed to be Spanish flu). The same report notes that the database still contains errors such as omissions, duplications, and misprocessing of survivors. The database is currently maintained by the National Archives of Finland, and Aalto University's SeCo research group describes its semantic portal Sotasurmasampo as containing approximately 40,000 death records from 1914 to 1922, most of which are related to the civil war. The victorious White Guard side called it the 'War of Liberation', while the defeated Red Guard and labor movement sides referred to it as a 'class war' or 'revolution'; both designations are given.

ItemOfficial ReleasesIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Total Civil War DeathsNational Archives of Finland database: approximately 40,000 death records from 1914 to 1922 (civil war deaths tallied separately not shown in portal)26,100 (Yle 2004, citing Ylikangas project) / COW combat deaths 24,150
Red Guard Combat DeathsUnconfirmed5,200 (Yle 2004)
Red Guard Prison Camp DeathsUnconfirmed13,500 (Yle 2004)

Data Record: COW records Finland separately in the 'Russian Civil War of 1917-1920' as well as the 'Finnish Civil War of 1918' (combat deaths 24,150). NAVCO classifies it as a communist rebel violence campaign that failed. Outside the scope of UCDP (1946–). COW's combat deaths of 24,150 differ in definition from the war death database (approximately 40,000 records covering 1914–1922) that includes prison camp deaths.

  1. Yle, report on the 1918 Civil War death toll database (2004-05-27, updated 2008) · Media (public broadcaster) · Affiliation: Finnish public broadcaster, citing a research project — https://yle.fi/a/3-5158261
  2. Aalto University SeCo, 'War Victims of Finland 1914–1922' project · Academic research project (national archives data) · Orientation: university research group — Data description — https://seco.cs.aalto.fi/projects/sotasurmat-1914-1922/en

Mäntsälä Uprising (Lapua Movement) Coup · 1932

According to the exposition at the Finnish Infantry Museum (Jalkaväkimuseo), the uprising began on February 27, 1932, when a Social Democratic Party rally in the Mäntsälä Workers' Hall was surrounded by more than 400 armed individuals who fired into the air and the ground to disrupt it. Lapua Movement Secretary-General Artturi Paavilainen emerged as the leader of the group, and the declaration sent to the president by the uprising side opposed 'Red Marxism' rather than the state itself and demanded swift action to prevent civil war. On March 4, a Mäntsälä delegation demanded a change of government. President Svinhufvud sought a peaceful resolution, and Eino Rahikainen served as the official negotiator. After negotiations on March 4–5, the leadership decided to end the uprising; the last entry in the uprising's logbook reads '5.3.32. End'. The only civilian death recorded was that of farmer Matti Yskeläinen, who died of a heart attack after an attempt to disperse a local rally in Utsikka on March 3 failed. Lapua leader Kustaa Ritvala committed suicide in Seinäjoki. On March 24, Interior Minister Arvo Manninen suspended the Lapua Movement's activities, and the courts confirmed the ban in May; the uprising leaders were sentenced to suspended sentences. The uprising side viewed it as a demand for the restoration of an anti-communist order, while the government side saw it as an armed challenge to the legitimate government; both accounts are presented side by side.

ItemOfficial ReleasesIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of Armed Participants (Initial)UnconfirmedMore than 400 (Infantry Museum exposition)
DeathsUnconfirmed1 civilian (heart attack), 1 uprising leader committed suicide (Infantry Museum exposition)

Data Record: Not found in Powell & Thyne coup data (coups_pt), Archigos, NAVCO, or COW. As a failed armed pressure-type regime change attempt led not by regular forces but by civil defense units and civilian movements, it may have been classified outside the definition of a coup.

  1. Jalkaväkimuseo (Finnish Infantry Museum), 'The Mäntsälä Rebellion' · Public museum exposition · Orientation: Finnish military museum — https://jalkavakimuseo.fi/the-mantsala-rebellion/

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

The 1933 Movement for Universal Male Suffrage in Andorra (the so-called '1933 Revolution') Protest · 1933

According to the Swarthmore College Global Nonviolent Action Database (GNAD) entry 'Andorrans seek universal male suffrage, 1933', from April to September 1933 Andorran youth occupied and seized the parliament building Casa de les Valls in demand of universal male suffrage; approximately 200 young supporters joined the first group upon entering. Subsequently, general residents and Andorrans living in Barcelona also joined. Methods recorded include collective petitions, picketing, protest rallies, election boycotts, refusal to dissolve existing institutions, and non-cooperation by lower-level government units; it is also described how residents blocked the parliament building with donkeys to prevent elections. Demands for independence were briefly raised and then withdrawn. As a result, in the August 31 election, all men aged 25 and over were granted suffrage, electing a new General Council (Consell General) and the first syndic. The same entry notes that universal male suffrage was revoked during the Vichy French period in 1941 and only restored in 1947. The movement side framed it as a demand for expanded suffrage, while the co-sovereigns (France and Spain) viewed it as a challenge to their co-sovereignty and an issue of order; both accounts are presented side by side. The official chronicle of the General Council of Andorra also states that the group of youth demanding universal suffrage held the General Council hostage until it agreed to grant it, followed by the dismissal of the General Council and new elections in which all men aged 25 and over voted.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Number of Participants Joining Initial OccupationUnconfirmedApprox. 200 (GNAD)
FatalitiesUnconfirmedNot recorded (no description of casualties in GNAD entry)

Data Record: In none of the datasets in the modern data file (COW, UCDP, coups, Archigos, NAVCO, MM, ACLED) does Andorra have a record. NAVCO 1.3 does not include this movement, but Swarthmore GNAD includes it as a nonviolent campaign.

  1. Global Nonviolent Action Database (Swarthmore College), 'Andorrans seek universal male suffrage, 1933' · Academic case database · Orientation: Case description from the perspective of nonviolent resistance research — https://nvdatabase.swarthmore.edu/index.php/node/4033
  2. Consell General d'Andorra, 'El segle XX. Un segle per la Constitució' · Official parliamentary chronicle · Orientation: Andorran Parliament (official) account — https://www.consellgeneral.ad/fitxers/documents/consell-en-la-historia/3-el-segle-xx

Petition for Women's Suffrage (1968–1973) Protest · 1968–1973

According to the official chronicle of the General Council of Andorra, in May 1968 a petition (súplica) signed by approximately 400 Andorran women was submitted; voting rights were granted in 1970, and eligibility for election on September 8, 1973, with full equality of legal rights reached in 1975. The document describes this as the outcome of more than six years of demands. No conflicting accounts by petitioners or authorities were found in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Petition SignatoriesApproximately 400 (Consell General chronicle)Unconfirmed

Data record: Not present in any dataset. As a nonviolent petition-based suffrage movement, it falls outside the event definitions of uprising and protest datasets.

  1. Consell General d'Andorra, 'El segle XX. Un segle per la Constitució' · Official parliamentary chronicle · Orientation: Andorran Parliament (official) account — https://www.consellgeneral.ad/fitxers/documents/consell-en-la-historia/3-el-segle-xx

    3. 1760–1815 — Literature

    Gordon Riots 1780

    George Rudé's study published in the Transactions of the Royal Historical Society (1956) situates this riot in the context of a political crisis during the American War of Independence, examining Lord George Gordon's anti-Catholic petition movement and attacks on Catholic homes and places of worship following the 1778 Catholic Relief Act. The riots reached their peak on the night of June 7, 1780, with eyewitnesses reporting that London looked like a 'sea of fire.' Hospital records cited by Rudé are partial — St. Bartholomew's Hospital received 36 injured persons on June 9, of whom 8 died, and 3 died from gunshot wounds at Middlesex Hospital and 2 from burns at Guy's Hospital. Records from St. Thomas's, London, and Westminster Hospitals do not survive, so total fatalities are not tallied in this study. A total of 160 persons were brought to trial (107 at the Old Bailey, 50 at Southwark, 3 at Guildford). The number of executions could not be confirmed within the scope of this study's abstract, and conflicting figures exist among secondary sources (19, 25, etc.), so it is left unverified. Authorities describe the event in terms of the collapse of public order and its suppression, while Catholics describe it as religious persecution; both accounts are presented side by side.

    1. Rudé, G. F. E. (1956). 'The Gordon Riots: A Study of the Rioters and their Victims'. Transactions of the Royal Historical Society 6, 93–114 · Academic article · Orientation: Social history (history from below) perspective — https://cambridge.org/core/journals/transactions-of-the-royal-historical-society/article/abs/gordon-riots-a-study-of-the-rioters-and-their-victims/3D4DE80D4DDB4EEB927E8114E7383141

    5. Modern Events in the Literature

    Peterloo Massacre Protest · 1819

    According to the National Archives (UK) commentary, on August 16, 1819 (Monday), approximately 60,000 peaceful protesters gathered in St. Peter's Field, Manchester, demanding parliamentary reform, and radical speaker Henry Hunt took the platform at the invitation of reformers. Magistrates deployed the Manchester and Salford Yeomanry and the 15th Hussars to arrest Hunt and others; armed cavalry charged the crowd. Hunt and others were arrested on charges of treason and imprisoned on lesser charges; the Home Secretary immediately passed the 'Six Acts,' which restricted public meetings, publication, and newspapers, and the radical newspaper Manchester Observer ceased publication in 1821. Casualty figures differ across sources (see below figures).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    DeathsUnconfirmed by official government tally at the timeApproximately 18 (including one child) — National Archives (UK) estimate
    InjuriesUnconfirmed by official government tally at the timeUp to approximately 700 — National Archives (UK) estimate
    Crowd Size—Approximately 60,000 — National Archives (UK)

    Data Record: Modern datasets such as COW, UCDP, and NAVCO do not record this as a single protest event (outside the dataset scope).

    1. The National Archives (UK), 'The Peterloo Massacre' · Government record commentary · Orientation: UK government body (historical commentary) — https://www.nationalarchives.gov.uk/explore-the-collection/explore-by-time-period/georgians/the-peterloo-massacre/

    Bloody Sunday (Derry/Londonderry) Protest · 1972

    According to a UK government (gov.uk) statement, on January 30, 1972 (Sunday), during a civil rights march in Bogside, Londonderry, British Army gunfire killed 13 people and wounded 14; one of the wounded died in June of the same year. A 12-year investigation led by Lord Saville was conducted to determine the definitive circumstances of the event, and a final report of approximately 5,000 pages was published on June 15, 2010. On the same day, the Prime Minister apologized in Parliament on behalf of the government and the nation, stating that the incident was 'unjustified and cannot be justified,' that some soldiers acted wrongly, and that the government bears ultimate responsibility. The report described the event as a tragedy for the bereaved and wounded and a catastrophe for the people of Northern Ireland. Differences between the conclusions of the Widgery inquiry in 1972 and those of the Saville inquiry could not be verified from the original text in this round (unverified).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Deaths13 killed on site + 1 wounded person died in June 1972 (UK government 2010 announcement)Original independent tally not reviewed
    Injuries14 (UK government 2010 announcement, including the deceased)Original independent tally not reviewed

    Data Record: The UCDP Armed Conflict Dataset records the UK government vs. PIRA/RIRA conflict in Northern Ireland, and NAVCO has a 'IRA/Irish nationalists' campaign. Bloody Sunday is recorded as a single event without a separate row and falls under the conflict and campaign context of the table above.

    1. GOV.UK, 'The Bloody Sunday Inquiry' (news story, 2010-06-15) · Government announcement · Orientation: UK government official position — https://www.gov.uk/government/news/the-bloody-sunday-inquiry

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    The Beeldenstorm of 1566 and the Beginning of the Dutch Revolt Early Modern (Habsburg Netherlands) · 1566–1572

    According to the canonical public history of Flanders, the 'Canon van Vlaanderen', on August 10, 1566, Calvinist preacher Sebastiaan Matte preached near Stenboekegem after which iconoclasm began, and on August 20, churches and monasteries in Antwerp were attacked. The Canon cites as background the veneration of saints and Calvinists' opposition to the church, persecution of Protestants under Charles V and Philip II, poverty among textile workers hurt by competition with England, and resentment over new taxes and Spanish-style rule. Many city councils sympathized with the new faith and did not intervene in the 1566 destruction. Rebel forces seized Holland and Zeeland in 1572 and rejected Philip II's authority with the Act of Abjuration in 1581. The Habsburg side characterized this as heresy and treason, while the rebels regarded it as the restoration of the rights of loyal subjects against tyranny. The route by which iconoclasm spread to northern cities (Amsterdam, Delft, Leiden, etc.) could not be confirmed from public sources in this round.

    1. Canon van Vlaanderen, 'The Iconoclastic Fury' · Canonical public history (supported by the Flemish government) · Orientation: Flemish (southern) perspective — sparse description of the northern city route — https://www.canonvanvlaanderen.be/en/events/the-iconoclastic-fury/

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    Belgian War of Independence (1830) (Dutch Side) Civil War · 1830

    The COW modern data table lists the 'Belgian Independence War of 1830' (1,100 combat deaths, Netherlands vs. Belgium). The Belgian account of the same event is in this series' Belgium volume, classifying it as a rebellion of the southern provinces; the Dutch side is classified as an independence revolution. In this round, no source containing official Dutch casualty figures was consulted.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    Combat DeathsDutch government tally unconfirmed1,100 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1)

    Data Record: COW records this war as an internal war within the Dutch state system (war_type 5). The same event appears in the Belgium volume as a pre-COW-incorporation event, so the two volumes' data processing methods differ.

    1. Correlates of War, Intra-State Wars v5.1 · Academic Dataset · Orientation: State-centric classification — counts only combat deaths — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

    February Strike (Februaristaking) 1941 Protest · 1941

    According to the Dutch Jewish memorial 'Joods Monument', a protest broke out in Amsterdam on February 25, 1941, in response to the German occupation authorities' first large-scale roundup in Amsterdam, which sent hundreds of Jews to Mauthausen. Nine people were killed and dozens injured in the crackdown by the occupying authorities; within days, approximately 300 communists who organized the strike were arrested, and 18 were executed in reprisal. The same record states that Joseph Aäijes, who distributed protest leaflets, was executed on March 13, 1941, at Valsburgerplein. The Netherlands Institute for War, Holocaust, and Genocide Studies (NIOD) holds the materials collected by B. A. Ciechanowski in 1954 for his research as 'Fond 254'. The occupying authorities remember it as a disruption of public order, while Dutch society remembers it as collective protest against persecution of Jews.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    DeathsOccupation authority official tally unconfirmed9 (Joods Monument)
    Executions—18 (Joods Monument) — other secondary accounts suggest 4 protesters were executed, so the count may differ
    Arrests—Organizers (communists) approx. 300 (Joods Monument)

    Data Record: As an occupation-era event, it is not recorded as a Dutch event in COW, NAVCO, or Mass Mobilization (1990 onward).

    1. Joods Monument, 'February Strike' · Memorial record (operated by the Jewish Cultural Quarter) · Orientation: Victim commemoration perspective — no participant count — https://www.joodsmonument.nl/en/page/635/february-strike
    2. EHRI Portal, February strike / Februaristaking (Fond 254, NIOD materials) · Public archive listing (EHRI·USHMM) · Orientation: Data list — no narrative — https://portal.ehri-project.eu/units/us-005578-irn95176

    January 2021 Curfew Protest Protest · 2021

    According to DutchNews reporting, on the first night of the 9 p.m. curfew implemented for the first time since World War II (Saturday night, January 23, 2021), 25 people were arrested and 3,600 fines for violations were issued. The following night, Sunday, January 24, unrest broke out in at least 10 cities including Amsterdam, Eindhoven, Tilburg, Enschede, The Hague (Schilderswijk), Helmond, Venlo, Ruremonde, Breda, and Arnhem, with approximately 240 people arrested, mostly in Amsterdam. Eindhoven mayor Joop Zoeteman said 'this is heading toward civil war,' and regional safety council chair Huibert Bruls called the participants 'corona hooligans' and said 'this is not a protest.' The government and local authorities describe it as public violence, while some participants describe it as protest against pandemic measures.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    Arrests (January 24 night)Approx. 240 (police announcement, cited by DutchNews)—
    Arrests (first curfew night, January 23)25 people, 3,600 fines (police announcement, cited by DutchNews)—

    Data Record: Mass Mobilization v5.1 covers only up to 2020 (this edition up to 2019 counts), so the 2021 unrest is not recorded. If an ACLED table exists, it will be consulted there.

    1. DutchNews.nl, 'Dutch police arrest 240 in a night of rioting nationwide' (2021-01-25) · Major media (Dutch English-language) · Orientation: Focused on police and local government announcements — few accounts from participants — https://www.dutchnews.nl/news/2021/01/dutch-police-arrest-around-200-in-a-night-of-rioting-nationwide/

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    German Peasants' War (Bauernkrieg) Early Modern Period (Reformation Era) · 1524–1526

    According to an educational portal Planet Wissen commentary by German public broadcaster WDR, peasants fought against the nobility from 1524 to 1526. The uprising began in the Hochrhein and spread to Oberschwaben, Franconia, Schwarzwald, and Alsace; fighting also continued in the Rheingau and Thuringia, and it reached the Alpine regions by the end. In March 1525, representatives from the Allgäu, Oberschwaben, and Bodensee regions gathered in the free imperial city of Memmingen to draft the 'Twelve Articles,' which demanded the abolition of serfdom, the right to freely elect pastors, participation in hunting, fishing, and forestry rights, and a reduction in corvée labor (the theologian Christoph Schäffer is introduced as one of the drafters). On May 15, 1525, at Frankenhausen, a peasant army led by Thomas Müntzer was defeated by imperial forces. The same commentary states that approximately 70,000 peasants lost their lives in the war as a whole (individual battle casualties are not listed on this page). The nobility side describes the restoration of order, while the peasants' document (the Twelve Articles) describes the recovery of rights based on divine law.

    1. Planet Wissen (WDR), 'Der Bauernkrieg 1524-1526' · Public broadcaster educational commentary · Orientation: popular explanation — Total fatalities are estimates; calculation basis not presented — https://www.planet-wissen.de/geschichte/neuzeit/der_bauernkrieg/index.html

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    East German June 17 Uprising (Volksaufstand vom 17. Juni 1953) Uprising · 1953

    According to an abstract of the study by Edda Ahrberg, Hans-Hermann Hertle, and Tobias Hollitzer published by the Federal Agency for Civic Education (bpb) in 2013 (Die Toten des Volksaufstandes vom 17. Juni 1953, Münster 2004), 55 deaths (including 4 women) have been confirmed from historical records among those killed in the East German uprising of June 17, 1953 and immediately thereafter. Of these, 34 died from shootings by the People's Police (Volkspolizei) and Soviet troops or from gunshot wounds between June 17 and 23, including bystanders and observers; 5 were executed under sentences of death handed down by Soviet occupation authorities, and 2 were executed under sentences of death handed down by East German courts. Four died from poor conditions in detention, four committed suicide in detention (at least two of which cannot be ruled out as murders), one died of a heart attack during an attack on an administrative building, and five security personnel were killed. Of the 25 unconfirmed deaths, 7 have been confirmed to be unrelated to the uprising. The researchers regard claims that 41 Soviet soldiers who refused orders in Berlin and Bitterfeld were shot dead as unverified Cold War legends. At the time, the East German government characterized the event as a 'fascist coup attempt orchestrated by the West,' while the West German side characterized it as a popular uprising (official documents from both sides could not be accessed in this round and remain unverified).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    DeathsStasi 1953-06-25: 25 · West German Ministry of All-German Affairs: 507 — seen only in search summaries, original texts not consulted (unverified)Historical records confirmed: 55 (Ahrberg·Hertle·Hollitzer 2004, published by bpb)
    Deaths from ShootingOfficial tally unconfirmed34 (June 17–23, People's Police and Soviet troops)
    ExecutionsOfficial tally unconfirmed5 by Soviet institutions, 2 by East German courts

    Data Record: The 1953 East German uprising is not recorded as a separate event in the COW, UCDP, or Powell-Thyne coup data (outside dataset scope or DDR separate country code). Archigos records an abnormal departure of Wilhelm II on 1918-11-09 due to 'popular protest with foreign support' (see table).

    1. bpb, 'Die Toten des Volksaufstandes vom 17. Juni 1953' (2013-05-17, Ahrberg·Hertle·Hollitzer) · Summary of academic research published by a government-affiliated political education institution · Orientation: Federal government agency (unified Germany perspective) — the study itself is based on historical records — https://www.bpb.de/themen/deutsche-teilung/der-aufstand-des-17-juni-1953/152604/die-toten-des-volksaufstandes-vom-17-juni-1953/

    Monday Demonstrations in Leipzig and the Peaceful Revolution Protest · 1989

    The Monday demonstrations in Leipzig on October 9, 1989, are described as a turning point of the East German peaceful revolution. The number of participants is typically reported as 70,000, but sociologist Karl-Dieter Opp argues in the journal Soziologie (Vol. 41, No. 2, 2012) that this figure is 'clearly wrong' and attributes it to careless handling of data rather than deliberate manipulation (alternative figures are provided in the full paper, which could not be accessed in this round). The scale of security forces mobilized by the East German authorities (approx. 8,000 personnel) and the non-shooting course of events remain unverified as original texts could not be consulted in this round.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    1989-10-09 ParticipantsOfficial tally unconfirmedTypically 70,000 — Opp (2012) argues this figure is incorrect (alternative figures not consulted)

    Data Record: Whether the East German anti-government movement of 1989 is recorded in nonviolent resistance datasets such as NAVCO could not be confirmed from the DEU code data (possibility of a separate DDR code) — recorded as a coverage gap.

    1. Opp, K.-D. (2012), Soziologie 41(2) · Academic article (journal) · Orientation: Rational choice theory sociologist — numerical critique paper — https://publikationen.soziologie.de/index.php/soziologie/article/view/726

    5. Modern Events as Recorded in Literature

    1942 General Strike (against forced conscription) Protest · 1942

    According to the Liberation Route Europe Foundation and the Luxembourg Tourism Office, when the Nazi occupation authorities introduced forced conscription of Luxembourgish youth in August 1942, a strike broke out on August 31 at a leather factory in Wiltz that spread to several regions nationwide. The Luxembourg English-language media outlet Chronicle.lu (2025-09-01) states that the strike began as a protest against Gauleiter Gustav Simon's announcement of conscription on August 30, 1942. Of the 125 strikers arrested, 21 were sentenced to death and executed (Chronicle.lu; Liberation Route notes that one of them was German). Liberation Route cites the SS special camp at Hinzert as the site of the executions. The national monument to the strike in Wiltz was inaugurated on September 30, 1956, with an engraving on the back depicting strikers falling to gunfire at the Hinzert camp. The occupation authorities characterized it as a breach of order in the annexed territories, while Luxembourg's commemorative narrative characterizes it as resistance against the occupation (the original text from the occupation authorities could not be accessed in this round).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
    ExecutionsOccupation authority counts unconfirmed21 (Liberation Route Europe; Visit Luxembourg)
    ArrestsOccupation authority counts unconfirmed125 (Chronicle.lu 2025); 'many' (Liberation Route)

    Data record: The COW, UCDP, NAVCO, and coup data contain no entry for the Luxembourg event (1942 is under occupation and not recorded at the state level). Mass Mobilization records 3 protests in 1993 and 1997 (2 wage disputes, 1 political process; 3 cases of 'ignored' state response), while ACLED records 20–59 protests per year from 2020 to 2026 with 0 deaths (see table).

    1. Liberation Route Europe, POI 2006 (Wiltz general strike) · Memorial Foundation (World War II Liberation Route) · Orientation: Allied and resistance commemoration perspective — https://liberationroute.com/pois/2006
    2. Visit Luxembourg, 'National Strike Monument Wiltz' · National Tourism Office guide · Orientation: State commemoration perspective — https://www.visitluxembourg.com/place/national-strike-monument-wiltz
    3. Chronicle.lu, 'Commemoration in Wiltz marks 83rd anniversary of 1942 general strike' (2025-09-01) · Major media (Luxembourg English-language) · Orientation: Commemoration coverage — Reflecting the government commemorative perspective — https://chronicle.lu/category/at-home/56523-commemoration-in-wiltz-marks-83rd-anniversary-of-1942-general-strike

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    Protests against the 1719 community reform Early modern (immediately after the establishment of the principality) · 1719–1733

    According to the 'Unruhen' entry in the Historisches Lexikon des Fürstentums Liechtenstein (Bernd Marquardt and Fabian Frommelt, reference date 2011-12-31), the protests against the reform that reduced communal rights, which began in 1719, ended 14 years later with partial recognition of traditional communal rights. The number of participants or the specific course of events is not presented numerically in this entry. The entry describes that there were different perspectives: the monarch's side viewed the reform as necessary, while the residents saw it as an infringement on their traditional rights.

    1. Historisches Lexikon des Fürstentums Liechtenstein, 'Unruhen' (Marquardt·Frommelt, 2011) · Public historical dictionary (national academic compilation) · Orientation: Liechtenstein national compilation dictionary — scholarly writing — https://historisches-lexikon.li/Unruhen

    3. 1760–1815 — Literature

    Ripple effects of the 1809 uprising 1809

    The same dictionary entry states that, amid growing discontent with the ruler's reforms in 1808, events in Tyrol and Vorarlberg influenced the 1809 uprising (the outcome is not stated in this entry). Austrian regional media VOL.AT (2009-11-16, symposium at the St. Peter Abbey in Bludenz) notes that the 1809 uprising also affected Liechtenstein, which had gained sovereignty through Napoleon's favor in 1806; residents wanted the restoration of traditional rights that conflicted with the ruler's modernization policies but did not take military action.

    1. Historisches Lexikon des Fürstentums Liechtenstein, 'Unruhen' · Public historical dictionary · Orientation: National compilation dictionary — https://historisches-lexikon.li/Unruhen
    2. VOL.AT, 'Symposium 1809 Kloster St. Peter' (2009-11-16) · Regional media (Austria Vorarlberg) · Orientation: Academic event coverage article — Summary description — https://www.vol.at/symposium-1809-kloster-st-peter/1979790

    5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

    1939 Anschluss Coup Attempt Coup · 1939

    According to the entry on 'Anschlussputsch' in the Historical Lexicon of the Principality of Liechtenstein (Donat Büchel, reference date 2011-12-31), on the evening of March 24, 1939, the National Socialist organization 'Vereinigung der Deutschen im Liechtenstein' (VDBL) attempted a coup centered around local branch leader Theodor Schädler and in coordination with institutions in Feldkirch. In Nendeln, about 40 Nazi supporters marched toward Vaduz but turned back after being persuaded by government commissioner Anton Prommel; VDBL members gathered at Schädler's home in Schaan were prevented from leaving the house by opposition forces. There were also plans to block roads and bridges over the Rhine upon German entry. Of approximately 100 participants, 36 fled to Feldkirch, and 76 were interrogated, with about 50 being prosecuted. In December 1939, the leaders were released on condition that they leave Liechtenstein, and after a retrial in 1945, four ringleaders were sentenced to five years' imprisonment and three to suspended sentences in January 1946. The entry cites the failure to maintain secrecy of the plan, interventions by figures such as Alois Poggl, and the non-involvement of the German government in Berlin as factors contributing to the failure; it also notes that most resident Jews sought refuge in Switzerland on the night of the event and returned a few days later. No mention is made of fatalities.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    ParticipantsOfficial tally unconfirmedApprox. 100 (40 marchers) (Historisches Lexikon, Büchel)
    Legal Proceedings1946-01 Verdict: 4 ringleaders sentenced to 5 years' imprisonment, 3 suspended sentences76 interrogated · approx. 50 prosecuted · 36 fled (same entry)

    Data record: Powell-Thyne coup data and Archigos do not record the 1939 attempt (it may lie outside the scope due to being a small-state, bloodless failed attempt). The events of Liechtenstein are also absent from COW, UCDP, NAVCO, and MM, and ACLED records 1–5 protests per year with 0 deaths in 2020–2024 (see table).

    1. Historisches Lexikon des Fürstentums Liechtenstein, 'Anschlussputsch' (Donat Büchel, 2011) · Public Historical Lexicon (National Academic Compilation) · Orientation: Liechtenstein national compilation dictionary — academic authorship — https://historisches-lexikon.li/Anschlussputsch

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    Monaco Revolution of 1910 (Constitutional Demand Movement) Protest · 1910–1911

    According to the Swarthmore College 'Global Nonviolent Action Database' entry on 'Monégasque citizens demand end to absolute monarchy (Monégasque Revolution), 1910', Monaco citizens demanded an elected parliament and a constitution to end the monarch's absolute rule, and the establishment of a national treasury to separate the monarch's personal income from state revenue. In March 1910, a delegation submitted a petition to Albert I stating that if their demands were not met, the monarchy would lose its throne; the entry notes that Monaco at the time had no army or navy and fewer than 80 police officers. On March 28, 1910, Albert I ordered parliamentary elections by universal suffrage. The entry records that in October of the same year protesters swarmed the palace and the police were unable to stop them, but violence was not reported and nonviolence is not confirmed. On October 15, the demand for a national treasury was accepted, and on October 19, a constitution was promised. Around the same time, there were anti-French unrest and protests in a separate movement targeting gambling establishments and French influence, and in November, there were protests from the French side against this, according to the entry. Existing interests such as Blanc, Laigle, and Bonaparte opposed the concessions. The constitution took effect on January 7, 1911, and the first parliament was convened on May 3, 1911, according to the entry. However, Monaco's government's chronology of its 20th anniversary of joining the Council of Europe presents the date of the law establishing the constitutional organization as January 5, 1911. The original text of the constitution in Monaco's official legislative database (Légimonaco) is titled 'Constitution of January 5, 1911 (published in the Journal de Monaco on January 7, 1911)', indicating that the two dates correspond to the enactment date and the publication date in the official gazette, respectively. The full text states that Albert I 'voluntarily' granted this constitutional organization to his subjects, and Article 2 specifies that the monarch's sovereignty was recognized by treaties with France dated September 14, 1641, and February 2, 1861. This constitution underwent revisions in 1917, 1922, 1944, and 1946 before being abolished under Article 96 of the Constitution on December 17, 1962. The same chronology describes the 1962 Constitution as an important development.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    DeathsOfficial tally unconfirmedNo records of deaths or repressive violence (Global Nonviolent Action Database)
    Security Forces (at the time)Official tally unconfirmedLess than 80 police officers, no army (same DB)
    Constitution Date1911-01-05 decree, published in the official gazette on 1911-01-07 (Légimonaco original text · government chronology)Effective 1911-01-07 (Global Nonviolent Action Database)

    Data Record: The Monaco event is absent from COW, UCDP, Powell-Thyne coups, Archigos, NAVCO, and MM. The 1910 movement falls outside the NAVCO scope (based on maximalist campaigns), and ACLED records 1–6 protests per year in 2020–2026, 1 political violence event in 2026, and 0 deaths (see table).

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    Belgian Revolution of 1830 (September Crisis of Brussels) Uprising · 1830

    According to the EBSCO Research Starters entry on the '1830–1831 Revolutions', liberal activists inspired by the July Revolution in Paris challenged Dutch authorities in the southern provinces (present-day Belgium), which had been placed under Dutch rule under the 1815 Vienna Settlement, while workers hurt by the economic downturn that had begun in 1827 supported them. On August 25, 1830, a large-scale riot broke out in Brussels and workers virtually took control of the streets. The Dutch government sent troops under Prince William (later William II) on August 28, and began an assault on the city on September 26. Artillery cleared the barricades but suffered from sniping from rooftops, and the Dutch forces decided to withdraw on September 27. Subsequent bombardment of Antwerp increased civilian defection. An international conference led by Britain dissolved the 1815 coalition and recognized an independent kingdom with a German minor prince as its monarch. The Dutch side characterized this as a rebellion, while the Belgian side regarded it as an independence revolution.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    September Crisis FatalitiesDutch and Belgian government tallies unconfirmedNo figures in sources checked for this round (EBSCO)

    Data Record: 1830 is prior to Belgium's inclusion in the COW state system (independent in 1830), and this event does not appear in COW's civil war list as a Belgian civil war. The three COW civil wars in the modern data table (the 1960–63 Congo, 1964–65 Congo, and 1978 Zaire-FNLC) are all internationalized civil wars in which Belgium intervened in the territory of Congo/Zaire.

    1. EBSCO Research Starters, 'Revolutions of 1830–1831' · Academic reference DB (EBSCO) · Tone: introductory narrative — no casualty figures — https://ebsco.com/research-starters/history/revolutions-1830-1831

    Walloon Workers Uprising of 1886 (March Strike) Uprising · 1886

    According to The Brussels Times ('Belgium Unlocked' series), on March 18, 1886, anarchists in Liège held a commemoration for the 15th anniversary of the Paris Commune, drawing thousands of participants (including 800–900 metalworkers), and that evening about 500 people rioted after an oration on Place Saint-Lambert, injuring 17 (6 security personnel) and damaging 104 buildings, with 47 arrested. The strike began in Gemepremoetz on March 19, spreading to Uccle, Seraing, Fleurus, and Thuin, and on March 26, striking miners at the Charleroi coal mines set fire to and looted factories including the Chéhaye glassworks (damage estimated at 2 million Belgian francs). The government mobilized the military on March 20; on the evening of March 26, troops fired on a crowd in front of the Roux glassworks, killing 5. A state of siege was declared in Charleroi on March 27, with 22,000 reservists called up, and on the same day, a second firing at Roux killed 12 workers. The uprising ended after 11 days, on March 29. Subsequently, the parliamentary commission of inquiry into labor led to Belgium's first labor legislation, and the 1889 Mons trial collapsed when infiltration by agents of the state intelligence service (Sûreté Publique) was revealed. The government side describes it as a measure to restore order, while the labor movement side describes it as a workers' uprising.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other

    Mobilized Reservists22,000 called up (cited by Brussels Times)—

    Data Record: COW, NAVCO, and Mass Mobilization (1990 onward) all omit the 1886 event.

    General Strike of Winter 1960–1961 (Opposing the Single Law) Protest · 1960–1961

    According to the Swarthmore College World Nonviolent Action Database (GNAD), a strike against the Aeschen government's austerity bill, the 'Single Law (loi unique),' began on December 20, 1960, with approximately 700,000 participants. The government deployed 18,000 national police supported by the military; in street clashes lasting several hours on January 6, 1961, police injured 75 striking participants, and arrests began on January 9, resulting in over 2,000 people receiving sentences of more than one month in prison. The strike ended on January 23, 1961; although it failed to block the bill, GNAD records that it strengthened demands for Walloon regionalism and federalism. The government side describes it as law enforcement and order maintenance, while the union (FGTB) side describes it as a struggle against austerity.

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    The Occupation of San Marino by Cardinal Alberoni and Popular Resistance Modern Era · 1739–1740

    According to the 'Alberoni, Giulio' entry in the New Catholic Encyclopedia (included in Encyclopedia.com), Cardinal Giulio Alberoni, papal legate to Romagna, occupied San Marino in 1739; this measure was controversial and later denied by the pope for political reasons. The San Marinese side commemorates its liberation from this occupation as the liberation of the republic. The specific course of popular protests during the occupation period, the details of the petition to Rome, and the date of liberation (February 5, 1740, Feast of St. Agatha) could not be verified in this round through public or academic sources and are left unverified. The Vatican account characterizes it as a personal measure by a legate in response to jurisdictional disputes, whereas the San Marinese account characterizes it as an infringement on sovereignty and its recovery.

    1. New Catholic Encyclopedia, 'Alberoni, Giulio' (Encyclopedia.com) · Academic Reference Encyclopedia (Catholic) · Orientation: Catholic Church history perspective — https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/alberoni-giulio

    5. Modern Events as Seen in Literature

    The 1957 Robetta Affair (Dual Government Confrontation) Regime Overthrow · 1957

    According to an article in the Italian daily Il Post (2022-09-30), five Social Party lawmakers defected to form the Independent Social Party, one lawmaker (Attilio Giannini) switched sides, and most of the Grand Council moved to a centrist coalition centered on the Christian Democrats. On the evening of September 30, 1957, new majority figures occupied the abandoned factory in Rovereto, and on October 1, after the term of the magistrates expired, they proclaimed a provisional government ('Rovereto government'). Italy immediately recognized this government; Italian Carabinieri surrounded Rovereto from three sides from the Italian side; and weapons arrived for both sides in Italy. The Rovereto government appealed to the Republic's armed forces and gendarmerie, while the left-wing 'Court government' organized a militia of volunteers. The two sides faced off for about two weeks without armed clashes; on October 14, the Court government declared that it was 'yielding to overwhelming force' and withdrew from the palace, making way for a new government supported by Italy. In the 1959 election, the Christian Democratic Party of San Marino won and remained in power for many years. The Court government side describes this as an illegal coup backed by foreign intervention, while the Rovereto side describes it as a legitimate government based on parliamentary majority. Il Fatto Quotidiano (2012-11-04), introducing Claudio Bisiani's book The Conspiracy of a Republic, notes that the social-communist coalition, which had won the 1949 election and also won again in 1955, was ousted by the 'Rovereto coup' of 1957; when Carabinieri armored vehicles were deployed around three sides of the border warehouse conference hall, the communist side withdrew to avoid casualties.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    DeathsNone reported0 — No armed clashes (Il Post)
    Confrontation Period—1957-10-01 proclamation of provisional government ~ 10-14 withdrawal of Court government (Il Post)

    Data record: The Powell & Thyne coup data, Archigos irregular transfers, NAVCO, and COW contain no entry for the 1957 San Marino event (small state, bloodless government change). ACLED records 2–12 protests per year in 2020–2025 with 0 deaths (see table).

    2.     2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    Swiss Peasants' War of 1653 (Bachen War) Modern · 1652–1653

    According to the Historisches Lexikon der Schweiz (HLS) entry on the Peasant War, in December 1652 Bern, Solothurn, and Fribourg devalued small-denomination coins (Bachen), prompting residents of the Entlebuch valley, who held many of these coins, to demand compensation. When Lucerne rejected this in early February 1653, the uprising began. The uprising spread to rural serfs of Lucerne, Bern, Solothurn, and Basel (Entlebuch, Emmental, parts of rural Solothurn and Basel). Rebel forces formed a larger alliance (the Peasant Alliance) at assemblies in Bolligen (February 26) and Huttwil (May 14), and from May 21 they besieged Lucerne and Bern. On May 29, the Entlebuch leader Niklaus Roienberger concluded a treaty with Bern and Murten, whereby the rebels abandoned their political demands and Bern confirmed economic concessions; on June 4, a second rebel leadership signed a similar agreement at Melchingen. As federal troops from Zurich and Bern troops from Vaud approached, the rebels withdrew. Bern subsequently declared the Murten treaty void and launched a punitive expedition, and Lucerne also imposed executions, galley labor, foreign mercenary service, and exile contrary to the treaty terms. The HLS records that seven rebels were executed in Basel on July 14, 1653. The authorities described it as rebellion by serfs; peasants described it as resistance to currency devaluation and taxation.

    1. Historisches Lexikon der Schweiz (HLS), 'Bauernkrieg' · Public history DB (Swiss Federal Historical Dictionary) · Lean: Academic compilation — Number of executions unrecorded — https://hls-dhs-dss.ch/de/articles/008909/

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    1847 Sonderbund War Civil War · 1847

    According to the HLS entry on the Sonderbund War, the conflict between liberals and conservatives over the 1815 Federal Treaty escalated into the Aargau Convent dispute (1841–1843), the controversy over the invitation of Jesuits in Lucerne, and two volunteer expeditions (1844–1845). The seven Catholic conservative cantons (Lucerne, Uri, Schwyz, Unterwalden, Zug, Fribourg, and Valais) formed a defensive alliance (Sonderbund) on December 11, 1845. The Federal Diet (Tagsatzung) voted to dissolve the Diet on July 20, 1847, and ordered mobilization of 50,000 troops on October 24. The war began on November 3 at the Gotthard Pass and lasted for 25 days. The federal commander-in-chief was Protestant Genevan Guillaume Henri Dufour, while Johann Ulrich von Salis-Soglio commanded the Sonderbund forces. Fribourg surrendered on November 14, Zug signed the surrender document on November 21, and the Battle of Gisikon and Meyerschen Kapelle on November 23 decided the outcome. The Sonderbund War Commission was dissolved on November 26, and the last cantons surrendered by November 29. Basel-Stadt, Neuchâtel, and Appenzell Inner-Rhodes remained neutral. Foreign intervention proposed in January 1848 did not materialize due to the French February Revolution, followed by the 1848 Federal Constitution. HLS notes that this constitution incorporated some federalist elements claimed by the Sonderbund side. The treason charges against the leaders were dropped in 1855. The federal side argued the illegality of the alliance, while the Sonderbund side invoked cantonal sovereignty and religious defense.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other

    1. Historisches Lexikon der Schweiz (HLS), 'Sonderbundskrieg' · Public History DB (Swiss Federal Historical Dictionary) · Orientation: Academic Compilation — Casualties on both the federal and Sonderbund sides are included — https://hls-dhs-dss.ch/de/articles/017241/

    November 1918 General Strike (Landesstreik) Protest · 1918

    The HLS 'Landesstreik' entry characterizes this as the greatest political crisis of the federal state. On November 7, 1918, the federal government (Bundesrat) decided on armed intervention and brought in the military to Zurich; on November 9, protest strikes broke out in 19 industrial centers, and insurgents blockaded the Zurich Paradeplatz. On November 12, the Action Committee of Olln (OAK) declared an indefinite general strike, which drew about 250,000 participants according to union estimates. The demands numbered nine: immediate new elections for the National Council under proportional representation, women's suffrage, general labor obligation, a 48-hour workweek, military reform, securing food supply, old-age and disability insurance, state monopoly on foreign trade, and an estate tax to repay national debt. On November 13, the federal government demanded unconditional cessation; the OAK accepted this on the morning of November 14. On the same day, three strikers were shot dead in Grenzach. Work resumed largely on November 15. Military courts opened proceedings against more than 3,500 (mostly railway workers) and convicted 147; in the trial of OAK leadership from March 12 to April 9, 1919, Robert Grimm, Friedrich Schneider, Fritz Platten, and Ernst Nobs were sentenced to imprisonment. The 48-hour week was introduced in 1919. The government side describes it as maintaining order against revolutionary attempts, while the labor movement describes it as a just strike against wartime inflation and food shortages.

    Military TrialsMore than 3,500 prosecuted, 147 convicted (HLS)—

    2. swissinfo.ch, 'When the army killed civilians' · Major Media (Swiss public broadcaster SRG international edition) · Orientation: Public media retrospective article — https://www.swissinfo.ch/eng/swiss-politics/when-the-army-killed-civilians/6239400

    Geneva Shooting Incident of November 9, 1932 Demonstration · 1932

    According to swissinfo reporting, when the far-right party Union nationale held a rally demanding a public trial of Social Party leaders, Leon Nicoullaud addressed a left-wing crowd gathered outside the building. Authorities called in troops from Lausanne; these were recruits in their sixth week of training. It appears that warning whistle signals were not properly conveyed, and an officer ordered firing; some soldiers fired into the air, while others continued shooting until their magazines were empty. The shooting lasted only a few seconds, killing 13 and wounding 65, with several of the dead being bystanders. Nicoullaud was sentenced to six months in prison for his role in the incident, and many media outlets blamed the protesters. In subsequent elections, the Social Party won a majority in the Geneva government. A formal memorial to the victims was erected in Geneva in 1982. swissinfo notes this as the last instance of the military being deployed against protesting civilians. Authorities and the military describe it as suppressing riots, while the left describes it as a massacre.

    ItemOfficial StatementsIndependent Investigations & Other

    Injuries65 (swissinfo)—

    5. Modern Events through Literature

    Spanish Civil War of 1936–1939 Civil War · 1936–1939

    According to Chapter 3 of Laia Balcells's Rivalry and Revenge (Cambridge University Press, 2017), the war began on July 18, 1936, with a coup by part of the military against the legally constituted democratic government and ended on April 1, 1939. One side (the Republic and loyalists) consisted of government forces, party militias (POUM, FAI, Communist Party), labor unions (CNT, UGT), and the International Brigades, while the other side (rebels and Nationalists) consisted of mutinous military factions, foreign contingents, Falangist and Carlist (Requeté) militias. Balcells cites Casanova (2014) to report approximately 600,000 combatant and civilian deaths in total, and Rubio (1977) to report more than 440,000 refugees abroad. The killing of civilians in the rear is reported at 49,272 in Republican-held territory by the most recent data, and estimated at approximately 150,000 in rebel-held territory (citing Preston 2011). Balcells reports that the microdynamics of violence in both rear areas were not fundamentally different, and attributes them to the interaction between local political elites and armed forces. The Francoist side describes this war as a 'national uprising' or 'liberation war', while the Republican side and subsequent democratization-era memory laws describe it as a military rebellion against the legitimate government. Article 1(3) of Spain's Law on Democratic Memory (Law 20/2022) repudiates and condemns the July 18, 1936 coup and the subsequent Francoist dictatorship, and sets the period covered by the law from the July 18, 1936 coup to the entry into force of the 1978 Constitution on December 29, 1978.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Total Deaths (Combatants + Civilians)Spanish government official total unconfirmedApprox. 600,000 (Casanova 2014, cited by Balcells 2017)
    COW Battle Deaths—309,400 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1, Modern Data Table)
    Rear-Country Civilian Killings—Republican territory: 49,272 / Rebel territory: approx. 150,000 estimated (Preston 2011, cited by Balcells)
    Refugees Abroad—Over 440,000 (Rubio 1977, cited by Balcells)

    Data Record: COW records 309,400 battle deaths, which differs from the definition of the approximately 600,000 total deaths in the scholarly literature (including rear killings). Archigos separately records the abnormal departures of Negrín and Miaja in 1939.

    February 23, 1981 Coup Attempt (23-F) Coup · 1981

    According to reports by eldiario.es, on February 23, 1981, a group of soldiers and security guards led by Lieutenant Colonel Antonio Tejero stormed the Congress of Deputies while it was holding a second vote of confidence for Prime Minister-designate Leopoldo Calvo-Sotelo, taking government officials and lawmakers hostage. At the same time, Lieutenant General Jaime Milans del Bosch declared a state of emergency and the military occupied Valencia. The coup failed. In 1983, the Supreme Court sentenced 30 of 33 defendants to 1 to 30 years in prison for military rebellion, participation in rebellion, or aiding and abetting rebellion. Tejero, Milans del Bosch, and General Alfonso Armada each received 30 years in prison for leading a military rebellion (Tejero was additionally fined 1 million pesetas), and their sentences were heavier than those at the military trial stage. Three defendants were acquitted. Armada was later pardoned, and Tejero served approximately half of his sentence—15 years—before being released on parole in 1996. The Supreme Court supported the 1993 pardon, but the government did not grant it. The defense argued that they had followed the king's wishes, while the courts and government ruled it a rebellion against constitutional order.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

    DeathsNo deaths — No casualties reported in sources checked for this round—

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    The 1641 Irish Rebellion and the '1641 Depositions'Early Modern (House of Stuart) · 1641

    According to Trinity College Dublin (TCD), the '1641 Depositions' — a collection of witness testimonies concerning violence during the 1641 Irish Rebellion — consists of 31 volumes and 19,010 pages in manuscript held by TCD. The manuscripts were transcribed and digitized through a three-year joint project by TCD, the University of Aberdeen, and the University of Cambridge, and have been published in 12 volumes by the Irish Manuscripts Commission since September 2014 (the first 3 volumes have been published; Volume 9 is prepared). The testimonies are primarily those of Protestant men and women from all social classes and some Catholics, recording lost property, military activities, and accusations of crimes committed by the rebel forces. It is noted as a characteristic of the source that the depositions themselves mainly reflect the perspective of the Protestant community, and TCD does not present a total number of fatalities. The rebels (Catholic Irish) have traditionally described it as an uprising against land confiscation and religious discrimination, while the settlers have described it as a massacre.

    1. Trinity College Dublin, '1641 Depositions Published In Volume Editions' · University official announcement (repository of historical sources) · Orientation: The source itself mainly consists of Protestant testimonies — no casualty figures — https://www.tcd.ie/news_events/articles/1641-depositions-published-in-volume-editions/

    3. 1760–1815 — Literature

    The 1798 United Irishmen Rebellion 1798

    According to the 2023 commentary by Dr. Tommy Dolan, senior editor at British Online Archives, the 'United Irishmen' issued their Declaration and Resolutions in October 1791, a state of martial law was declared in March 1798, and the rebellion began on May 23. The three main centers of the rebellion were Wexford, Ulster, and Mayo; on June 13, northern rebels were defeated by General Nugent's royalist forces at Ballinahinch, and on June 21, the defeat at Vinegar Hill brought an end to the Wexford 'Republic.' During the Wexford rebellion, there were massacres of Protestants at Scullabogue and Wexford Bridge. On October 12, the French flagship Hoche was captured by the Royal Navy, and Theobald Wolfe Tone, who had sailed aboard it, died in prison. Approximately 50,000 rebels and 76,000 royalist troops took part. Subsequently, the Act of Union between Britain and Ireland came into effect in 1801. The royalist side characterized it as the suppression of treason, while the United Irishmen characterized it as a republican revolution, and sectarian massacres occurred on both sides.

    1. British Online Archives, '225 years: outbreak of the 1798 rebellion led by the United Irishmen' (Dr Tommy Dolan, 2023) · Academic source publisher commentary · Orientation: Opening commentary — only an estimated range for fatalities is presented — https://britishonlinearchives.com/posts/category/notable-days/617/225-years-outbreak-of-the-1798-rebellion-led-by-the-united-irishmen

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    Easter Rising of 1916 Uprising · 1916

    RTÉ's public history site 'Century Ireland' cites the Glasnevin Trust's 'Necrology of 1916' to state that 485 people died during or as a direct result of violence over six days in Easter Week 1916. More than three-quarters of the fatalities were not insurgents, 40 children died in combat, and 41 (35%) of the military fatalities were Irish. The North King Street massacre occurred on April 29. This article does not present an official British government casualty tally. The British government characterized it as suppression of wartime rebellion, while the uprising's leadership proclaimed the Irish Republic.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    DeathsBritish Government Official Tally — Not found in sources checked for this round485 (Glasnevin Trust 1916 Necrology, cited by Century Ireland)
    Child Deaths—40 (same source)

    Data Record: Because Ireland was not an independent state in 1916, it does not appear in COW or Archigos's Irish state system. Archigos records de Valera's 'irregular accession' in 1919 and Collins's 'removal by rebels (death)' in 1922, but the Irish Civil War of 1922–1923 is not listed in the COW civil war list of modern data tables.

    5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

    The 1848 Vienna Revolution and Uprisings in the Habsburg Empire Uprisings · 1848–1849

    The COW civil war list in the modern data table records the 1848 Vienna uprising (3,241 combatant deaths), the 1848 Five Days of Milan uprising (1,030), the 1848–1849 Hungarian War (104,600, internationalized), and Austria's intervention in the liberal uprisings of 1820–1821 in the Two Sicilies and 1821 in Sardinia under the state system of Austria (Habsburg Empire). In this round, primary Austrian sources containing official casualty tallies for these events could not be accessed and verified, so the narrative is limited to the dataset records. The imperial government characterized them as suppression of rebellion, while the uprising side characterized them as demands for constitutionalism and national self-determination.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    Combat deaths in the 1848 Vienna uprisingImperial government tally unconfirmed3,241 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1)
    Combat deaths in the 1848–1849 Hungarian WarImperial government tally unconfirmed104,600 (COW; internationalized civil war including Russian intervention)

    Data Record: COW connects two Italian uprisings of 1820–1821 to cow_state 'Austria-Hungary', but the actual belligerents in the civil wars were the Two Sicilies and Sardinia, with Austria as an intervener.

    1. Correlates of War, Intra-State Wars v5.1 · Academic Dataset · Orientation: State-centric classification — counts only combat deaths — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

    July 15, 1927 Incident (Burning of the Law Courts) Protest · 1927

    According to the Austrian Supreme Court (OGH)'s historical page on the Law Courts, on January 30, 1927, in Schattendorf, Burgenland, supporters of the Social Democratic Party-affiliated Republican Defense League (Schutzbund) clashed with a veterans' organization (Frontkämpfervereinigung), resulting in the deaths of two innocent bystanders. Following acquittals of the defendants in a jury trial in Vienna, violent protests broke out on July 15, 1927, and the Law Courts were set on fire; police orders to open fire on protesters left 89 dead. The fire destroyed the Supreme Court, the third-largest law library in Europe at the time, and land registers for most of Vienna's districts. OGH describes this event as a prelude to the February 1934 uprising and the collapse of the First Republic. The government and police characterized it as suppression of riots, while the Social Democratic Party described it as a massacre of workers.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Deaths89 (Austrian Supreme Court OGH history page)More than 89 (secondary reports by JURIST, etc. — original texts not verified)
    Shattendorf Incident Deaths (1927-01-30)2 (OGH)—

    Data Record: Not recorded in COW, NAVCO, or Mass Mobilization (since 1990).

    1. Oberster Gerichtshof (Austrian Supreme Court), 'The 1927 fire' · Official history page of a state institution · Orientation: Perspective of the affected institution (court) — No number of wounded — https://www.ogh.gv.at/en/the-palace-of-justice/the-1927-fire/

    February Civil War of 1934 (February Uprising) Civil War · 1934

    According to the official history page of the Vienna City Hall, on the morning of February 12, 1934, a shootout broke out in Linz when police searched a building owned by the Social Democratic Party, and the Vienna Social Democratic Party declared a general strike, which collapsed within hours. The uprising began at 11:46 AM when trams stopped in Vienna, and a state of siege was declared in Vienna, Styria, and Upper Austria on the same day. The most intense clashes occurred in Gugging, Meidling, and Floridsdorf, with municipal housing estates such as Karl-Marx-Hof, Loibnitzhof, Zantinger, and Goethehof serving as strongholds. On February 13, Otto Bauer and Julius Deutsch fled to Czechoslovakia. On February 14, during the first day of military tribunals, Karl Münnichreiter, a member of the Heimwehr who had been seriously wounded, and firefighter Georg Baisel were executed, and by February 21, 140 Heimwehr members had been convicted. Subsequently, the Social Democratic Workers' Party and the Free Trade Unions and their affiliated organizations were dissolved. The government side characterizes it as the suppression of an armed rebellion, while the Social Democratic side regards it as a defensive uprising against fascism.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Government Forces Deaths & Injuries118 deaths, 486 injuries (announced by the government on 1934-02-15, cited by Vienna City Hall)—
    Heimwehr Side Deaths & Injuries196 deaths, 319 injuries (announced by the government on 1934-02-15)—
    Total Deaths314 (sum of the above government announcements)1,500–2,000 deaths, approximately 5,000 injured (estimated by British journalist G. E. R. Gedye, cited by Vienna City Hall)
    Executions2 on February 14 + 7 thereafter (same page of Vienna City Hall)The same page also describes '8 executions' — discrepancy between 9 and 8

    Data Record: The COW civil war list in the modern data table does not include the February 1934 civil war (the official Vienna City Hall tally of 314 is below the COW civil war threshold of 1,000). Archigos records Dolfuss's assassination in July 1934 as an irregular departure.

    1.     2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    The Sicilian Vespers Middle Ages (Kingdom of Sicily) · 1282–1302

    According to the Treccani National Encyclopedia, an uprising against the rule of Charles I of Anjou broke out in Palermo on Easter Monday evening in 1282 during vespers, and this became the beginning of the 'War of the Vespers' with the same name. The war ended with the Peace of Caltabellotta in 1302, when Angevin forces withdrew from Sicily and the crown passed to Peter III of Aragon, husband of Constance, daughter of the Swabian dynasty's Manfred. The Treccani entry does not provide a number of deaths. The uprising tradition portrays it as resistance against foreign rule, while the Angevin perspective describes it as a rebellion.

    1. Treccani, 'Vespri siciliani' · National encyclopedia (academic compilation) · Orientation: Italian national narrative perspective — Number of deaths not presented — https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/vespri-siciliani/

    The Ciompi Uprising (Tumulto dei Ciompi) Late Middle Ages (Republic of Florence) · 1378

    According to Treccani, the Ciompi were wage laborers employed by various guilds (arti) of Florence, especially the wool guild (Arte della lana), without political rights. The uprising began on July 20, 1378. They established their own guild and demanded a quarter of the communal offices; after seizing power, they proposed a reform plan creating three new 'lower-class' (popolo minuto) guilds (two for small traders and one for the Ciompi) and allocating a third of the offices to them. Counterattacks by allied old guilds drove many Ciompi out of Florence, and those remaining were isolated and bloodily suppressed on August 31. The Ciompi then disappeared as a political force. The number of deaths in the suppression is not given in this entry.

    1. Treccani, 'Ciompi' · National encyclopedia (academic compilation) · Tone: Academic summary — Number of deaths not stated — https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/ciompi/

    Masaniello Uprising (Naples Anti-Spanish Uprising) Early Modern (Kingdom of Naples under Spain) · 1647–1648

    According to Treccani's entry on Masaniello, the uprising began on July 7, 1647, when merchants refused to pay a fruit tax (gabella sui frutti) imposed by Spanish authorities. A disturbance during a festival in Mercato Square spread to nearby working-class neighborhoods; the insurgents stormed the royal palace, freed prisoners, and destroyed customs offices. As other leaders withdrew, a fisherman named Masaniello took command, issuing verdicts and organizing militia. An assassination attempt on him failed on July 10, and the next day his father-in-law recognized him as 'Commander-in-Chief of the Loyal Neapolitan People.' Masaniello was killed in a second assassination on July 16. The subsequent course of the declaration of a republic and Spanish counter-suppression through 1648 and the number of deaths are not covered in the pages opened in this round (unverified).

    1. Treccani, 'Masaniello' · National encyclopedia (academic compilation) · Tone: Biographical narrative — Subsequent developments and fatalities not stated — https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/masaniello/
