    5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

    The Fasci Siciliani Movement and Its Suppression Uprising · 1891–1894

    According to Treccani, the Fasci Siciliani was a movement organized by farmers and miners into trade unions in Sicily from 1891 to 1894, adopting socialist tendencies from 1893. Its leaders included G. De Felice Spera, R. G. Bosco, and N. Barbato. In the midst of the agricultural crisis of 1893 and a wave of strikes, the Crispi government opted for armed suppression; in December 1893 to January 1894, military intervention and clashes left 'dozens' dead, hundreds arrested (including leaders), and the movement dissolved. The government describes it as restoring order, while the movement side describes it as a demand for economic rights.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    FatalitiesUnconfirmed by official government tally at the time'Dozens' (Treccani; exact number not stated)
    ArrestsOfficial tally unconfirmedHundreds (including leaders; Treccani)

    Data Record: Not recorded in COW civil war data (presumed to fall short of the 1,000 combat fatalities threshold — not an adjudication). The data table is populated by the generator from data/ITA.json.

    1. Treccani, 'Fasci siciliani' · National Encyclopedia (academic compilation) · Tone: Academic summary — exact number of fatalities not stated — https://www.treccani.it/enciclopedia/fasci-siciliani/

    The Bologna Train Station Bombing (Strage di Bologna) Protest · 1980

    According to an Italian media outlet Il Post's summary of court rulings (2023-08-07), the bombing of Bologna Central Station on August 2, 1980 killed 85 people and injured more than 200. Neo-fascist armed group NAR members Valerio Fioravanti and Francesca Mambro were convicted as perpetrators to life imprisonment, which was confirmed by the Supreme Court (Cassazione) in November 1995; Luigi Chabardin received a 30-year prison sentence in his second trial (1997–2007). In 1995, the Supreme Court convicted Michele Sindona, the head of the P2 branch Riccio Zelli, and SISMI officials for obstructing the investigation (depistaggio). Gilberto Cavallini (2020 first instance) and Paolo Bellini (2022 first instance) were sentenced to life imprisonment; Italian news agency LaPresse reported that Bellini's sentence was confirmed by the Supreme Court on July 1, 2025. Bellini has consistently denied involvement.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Fatalities85 (court ruling basis; Il Post summary)Separate independent tally unconfirmed
    InjuriesOver 200 (Il Post summary)Separate independent tally unconfirmed

    Data Record: As a single terrorist attack, it is not recorded as an event in COW, UCDP, coup, or NAVCO data. The entirety of the 'Years of Lead' (anni di piombo) political violence of the 1960s and 1980s falls outside the dataset categories (coverage gap).

    1. Il Post, 'Cosa dicono le sentenze sulla strage di Bologna' (2023-08-07) · Major media (ruling summary) · Tone: Italian online media — ruling summaries with some confusion over whether rulings have been finalized — https://www.ilpost.it/2023/08/07/sentenze-strage-bologna/
    2. LaPresse, 'Paolo Bellini, confermato l'ergastolo in Cassazione' (2025-07-01) · Wire report · Tone: Factual reporting — confirmed via search summary; original text not reviewed — https://www.lapresse.it/cronaca/2025/07/01/paolo-bellini-confermato-lergastolo-in-cassazione-per-la-strage-di-bologna/

    Anti-G8 Summit Protests and the Dias School Raid in Genoa Protest · 2001

    According to the European Court of Human Rights (ECtHR) judgment Cestaro v. Italy (application no. 6884/11), summarized by the Center for Human Rights at the University of Padua, the harsh treatment received by the applicant, who was 62 at the time, when police raided the Dias-Pertini school at the end of the 2001 G8 summit in Genoa constituted 'torture' under Article 3 of the European Convention on Human Rights. The Court also acknowledged that Italian criminal law was inappropriate for punishing torture and had no deterrent effect (procedural violation), viewing it as a structural problem, and ordered compensation of €45,000 for non-pecuniary damage. The Italian government describes the police operation at the time as maintaining public order, while protest participants and human rights organizations describe it as state violence. Details regarding the death of Carlo Giuliani during the protests and the Bolzaneto detention center incident could not be verified from the original texts in this round (unverified).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Cestaro Case CompensationItalian Government: Not officially acknowledged prior to the ruling (within scope of verification)ECtHR €45,000 compensation and torture determination (2015-04-07)

    Data Record: Whether recorded in protest data such as NAVCO or MM (Mass Mobilization) follows the data/ITA.json table. Fatalities and injury tallies could not be verified in this round.

    1. Università di Padova Centro Diritti Umani, 'European Court of Human Rights: Italy condemned for torture for the facts occurred during the G8 summit in Genoa in 2001' · University human rights center (summary of judgment) · Orientation: human rights research institute — summary of judgment, government-side account not included — https://unipd-centrodirittiumani.it/en/news/european-court-of-human-rights-italy-condemned-for-torture-for-the-facts-occurred-during-the-g8-summit-in-genoa-in-2001

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    Evoira Unrest (Alterações de Évora, 'Manuelino uprising') Early Modern Period (House of Braganza) · 1637

    According to the commentary on the 'Document of the Month' (August 2023) from the Municipal Archives of Évora, the influx of gold and silver remittances from India declined after 1630, and Portuguese taxes continued to become heavier. In August 1637, under the House of Braganza (when the King of Spain also held the title of King of Portugal), a movement arose in Évora against tax increases set by the Lisbon government, known as the 'Manuelino uprising' or 'Évora unrest.' Increases in the water tax (real de água) and existing transaction taxes (sisas) fueled popular anger, which erupted into protests and violence; Évora residents refused to obey the nobility. The same commentary describes this event as a fuse that led to the 1640 revolution and the accession of João IV. The number of fatalities and executions is not recorded in this document.

    1. Câmara Municipal de Évora, 'Documento do mês — agosto 2023: Alterações de Évora' · municipal archive commentary · Orientation: regional narrative perspective (positioning it as a hero of the 1640 restoration of independence) — basis is a 1951 local newspaper — https://www.cm-evora.pt/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/documento-do-mes-agosto-23.pdf

    Porto Riots (Motins do Porto) — Against the Douro Wine Company Early Modern Period (Pombaline Reform) · 1757

    According to an article by Francisco Ribeiro da Silva published in Vol. 16 (2008) of the academic journal População e Sociedade from the CEPESE Research Center at the University of Porto, on the morning of February 23, 1757, the slogan 'Long live the King! Long live the people! Death to the company!' was chanted repeatedly in the streets of Porto. This was the first large-scale protest by commoners against the establishment of the 'General Company of Agriculture of the Vineyards of the Upper Douro' (Companhia Geral da Agricultura das Vinhas do Alto Douro), established by royal charter on September 10, 1756. The author describes the threat of the crowd, reports hastily sent to Lisbon, and the political situation at the time as turning this protest into an 'enormous riot' that forced power to classify it as a crime against majesty (lesa-majestade). About three weeks later, on the night of March 15, a special judicial tribunal (alçada) appointed by the king arrived in Porto. The same article notes that the government account blaming taverns for wine price controls disappeared thirty years later and that 'not few' lost their lives through punishment. The number of those sentenced (478 convictions and 26 death sentences according to the search summary) could not be verified in this round by examining the original text (unverified).

    1. Silva, F. R. da (2008). 'Viva el-rei! Viva o povo! Morra a Companhia!' (The Dark Side of the Pombaline Institution). População e Sociedade, 16, CEPESE · Academic journal article (university research center journal) · Tone: social history perspective — heavy weight given to perspectives on riots and the popular classes, also citing government documents — https://repositorio-aberto.up.pt/bitstream/10216/20418/2/revpopsoc162008frsilva000085287.pdf

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    April 25 Carnation Revolution and Shooting at PIDE/DGS Headquarters Coup · 1974

    According to RTP's article 'A rendição da PIDE', on April 25, 1974, during the military coup, police who had erected barricades outside the headquarters of the political police (PIDE/DGS) on Rua António Maria Cardoso in Lisbon opened fire on a crowd gathered at the entrance, killing four people. The first contingent of approximately 70 marines (commanded by Eugénio Cavalheiro), dispatched to the scene, lifted the siege and withdrew after being persuaded by Alpoim Calvão inside the headquarters; a second naval force under Luís Costa Correia subsequently accepted the surrender. The agents were arrested, and Silva Pais, head of the secret police, was detained as well. The search summary includes an account that adds 45 wounded, the names of four deceased (João Arruda, Fernando Giesteira, José Harteley Barneto, Fernando Reis), and one PIDE employee killed while attempting to flee, bringing the death toll to five; however, this could not be verified from the original text in this round. The Movement of the Armed Forces (MFA), which staged the coup, characterized it as the end of the dictatorship, whereas the former regime regarded it as a mutiny.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    Deaths outside PIDE/DGS headquartersOfficial announcements by the former regime and transitional government not confirmed4 (RTP article) — the account counting 5 is found only in the search summary (unverified)
    CasualtiesOfficial tally unconfirmed45 reported (only in search summaries, unverified)

    Data record: The Powell & Thyne coup data record 'success' on 1974-04-25 and 'failure' on 1962-01-01 (the attempt known as the Vaz revolt — original text for this round not verified) (see table). Civilian deaths on the day of the revolution are not separately tallied in coup data.

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    Jacquerie Middle Ages (Hundred Years' War period) · 1358

    According to the 'Jacquerie' entry in the Encyclopædia Universalis, the uprising began on May 28, 1358, in Saint-Leu-d'Esserent and spread to Île-de-France (the Paris region), Beauvaisis, and Brabant. The background included economic depression from the late 14th century onward, the Hundred Years' War, the Black Death, and growing discontent following the defeat at Poitiers in 1356; the uprising targeted feudalism. The leader was Guillaume Carle (also spelled Karle). A noble force led by Charles II of Navarre ('the Bad') defeated the peasants at Mello on June 9, 1358, while Parisian forces were defeated at Meaux around the same time. Étienne Marcel, a representative of Parisian merchants, was assassinated in July 31, the same year. This entry does not provide figures for participants or fatalities.

    1. JACQUERIE - Encyclopædia Universalis · Encyclopedia — https://www.universalis.fr/encyclopedie/jacquerie/

    The Fronde Early Modern (Minority of Louis XIV) · 1648–1652

    The 'Fronde' entry in Encyclopædia Universalis describes this as a period of unrest and civil war spanning four years, from 1648 to 1652, during the minority of Louis XIV. The background is presented as the costs of the war with the House of Habsburg that began in 1635, loans and taxes levied to fund it, the creation of new offices for sale, a tax farming system that fattened financiers, local tax rebellions supported by some nobles, and growing resentment toward the regency government under Anne d'Autriche and the chancellor Mazarin following the death of Louis XIII in 1643. In early 1648, the Parlement de Paris demanded that its powers be defined, but the regent refused; Pierre Brûlé, a member of the Parlement, became spokesman for the opposition. The article is organized into sections titled 'The Barricades of 1648', 'The Parlementary Fronde and the New Fronde', and 'Civil War'. The year of termination is given as either 1652 or 1653 in different sources.

    1. FRONDE - Encyclopædia Universalis · Encyclopedia — https://www.universalis.fr/encyclopedie/fronde/

    War of the Camisards (War of the Seven Years) Early Modern (Personal Rule of Louis XIV) · 1702–1704 (rebellion attempts until 1710)

    According to the commentary of the Museum of French Protestantism (Musée protestant), the uprising began in the Cévennes region, where Protestant persecution continued after the revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685, when on July 24, 1702, Father Jean Chaillet, the vicar apostolic of the Cévennes, was killed at Pont-de-Montvert. The most well-known leaders were Roland and Jean Cavalier. The monarchy mobilized two generals and approximately 25,000 troops. In May 1704, Cavalier negotiated surrender with General Villars and was allowed to leave the country; other leaders either perished or surrendered. The uprising effectively ended in May 1704, and all attempts at a resurgence until 1710 failed. The commentary is from an institution representing Protestant interests, and it does not provide figures for Camisard forces.

    1. Musée protestant, 'The war of the Camisards (1702-1710)' · Memorial institution · Orientation: Protestant memorial and educational institution (perspective of the victim community) — https://museeprotestant.org/en/?p=2746

    5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

    The Paris Commune and the 'Bloody Week'Uprising · 1871

    According to the City of Paris's (Ville de Paris) 150th anniversary commentary on the Commune, the 'Bloody Week' (Semaine sanglante) lasted from May 21 to 28, 1871. After the suppression, approximately 43,000 people, including men, women, and children, were arrested. A law of March 22, 1872, authorized deportation to New Caledonia, and between 1872 and 1878, about 3,800 people were transported in 20 convoys, while thousands were transferred to prisons in western ports. General Auffray's report records that approximately 46,835 people were brought before the courts. About 6,000 fled abroad, and a pardon law was passed in 1880, but about 10,000 did not benefit from it. There is no consensus among historians regarding the number of deaths. The City of Paris commentary notes that while it records 3,000 to 5,000 combat deaths and approximately 20,000 massacres together, there is no strict consensus, and Robert Tombs lowered the estimate from approximately 25,000 to approximately 20,000 and then to 6,000 to 7,500, believing that about half of the deaths occurred during military trials after the fighting. Karine Varley responded that both the number of deaths and the circumstances of their occurrence remain significant uncertainties. The Commune side characterized it as popular self-government and massacre, while the Versailles government characterized it as suppression of rebellion.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    'Bloody Week' Commune-Side DeathsOfficial consolidated statistics unconfirmed — Paris City commentary (2021): combat deaths 3,000–5,000 + massacres approx. 20,000, 'no consensus among historians' explicitly statedR. Tombs revised estimate 6,000–7,500 (previous approx. 25,000 → approx. 20,000 → 6,000–7,500), summarized by K. Varley (2011)
    Deaths and injuries among Versailles government forcesParis City commentary: deaths 500–800, wounded 5,000Independent estimates unconfirmed
    Arrests, trials, executionsApprox. 43,000 arrests / Affer report: trials referred 46,835, dismissed 23,727, convicted 10,137, convictions in absentia 3,313, acquitted 2,445, 'refused to be investigated' 7,213, death sentences 95 of which 25 carried outIndependent tallies unconfirmed
    New Caledonian deportationsParis City commentary: 20 deportations 1872–1878, approx. 3,800 personsIndependent tallies unconfirmed

    Data record: Recorded in COW as the 'Paris Commune War of 1871' (combat deaths 21,379). This value is higher than Tombs's revised estimate (6,000–7,500) and closer to the Paris City commentary's upper bound (approx. 23,000–25,000).

    1.     2. Varley, K. (2011). Reassessing the Paris Commune of 1871: a response to Robert Tombs. H-France Salon 3(1) · Academic — https://strathprints.strath.ac.uk/32451/

    2. Gleditsch, Nils Petter, Peter Wallensteen, Mikael Eriksson, Margareta Sollenberg & Håvard Strand (2002). Armed Conflict 1946–2001: A New Dataset. Journal of Peace Research 39(5): 615–637. / Pettersson, Therése (2026). UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset Codebook v26.1.
    3. Chenoweth, Erica & Christopher Wiley Shay (2020). List of Campaigns in NAVCO 1.3. Harvard Dataverse, doi:10.7910/DVN/ON9XND. / Chenoweth, Erica & Maria J. Stephan (2011). Why Civil Resistance Works: The Strategic Logic of Nonviolent Conflict. New York: Columbia University Press.
    4. Clark, David & Patrick Regan (2016). Mass Mobilization Protest Data. Harvard Dataverse, doi:10.7910/DVN/HTTWYL, V5.1.
    5. Raleigh, Clionadh, Andrew Linke, Håvard Hegre & Joakim Karlsen (2010). Introducing ACLED: An Armed Conflict Location and Event Dataset. Journal of Peace Research 47(5): 651–660.

    Items omitted for lack of confirmation: 1382 Maiolaten uprising, 1639 Norman barefoot uprising, 1675 Red Cap uprising — original texts unread; 1775 Flour War, 1789 Revolution, 1793–1796 War in the Vendée (disputed death toll estimates), 1799 Brumaire Coup — Universalis entry on the Vendée, text unread; 1830 July Revolution, 1848 February and June Revolutions (recorded in COW) narratives — not surveyed in this round; 1934 February 6 affair, 1958 Algerian crisis, 1961 OAS and October 17 events, 1968 May events (striker turnout of 7 million–10 million: search summaries only, original texts unread); 2005 suburban unrest — not surveyed; Yellow Vest Movement (2018–): first-day turnout of 282,000 (Ministry of the Interior), over 400 injured (Minister Castaner) and other figures seen only in search summaries; Jacques riot participants and fatalities, Frond fatalities — not found in consulted encyclopedia articles; Joutard (1976) bibliographic details — original not verified

    Scope of Application of Six-Layer Discourse — The narrative of this monograph series spans from Layer 1 (data and records) to Layer 5 (interpretations through prior research). It does not provide prescriptions, moral evaluations, leader assessments, or predictions. Layer 6 is reserved exclusively for Cheonghae (淸海).
